- **Honest coverage:** for every case above, the per-holder report lists each holder's bytes, decisions-only, evicted and unknown ranges, with offline holders dated, and never calls a range lost.

### 8.5 Restore, recovery and admission (R2-CORE-04, -06, R2-TRUST-03; finding 10; recovery admission and client identity)

**Required:** exactly one active Core writes a dataset; a local lock does not stop a second server running a copy; restore and migration must fence or stop the previous Core (R2-CORE-04). A restore that moves history backward establishes a new epoch; a restored Core starts with outbound side effects suspended (R2-CORE-06). Revoked origins stay rejected (R2-TRUST-03).

**Decision:** single-Core design (O-2); no cluster coordination.

#### 8.5.1 Restore procedure, the writer inventory, writer fences and sender fences

**Three questions, never merged:**
- **Inventory:** which Cores could write this dataset? Lisa's own records can't answer this alone. A restore made elsewhere from an older backup, a copied data directory or a VM snapshot leaves no trace in a ledger that predates it, and every device that knows it may be offline. Only the **owner's independent inventory**, checked against everything Lisa knows, answers it.
- **Writer fence,** per inventoried writer: can that Core still change the dataset by **any** path? A Core that runs can accept stale clients' edits, run its scheduler and connectors (plans, briefs, reminder sync, mail and calendar ingestion), take owner commands from its own terminal, sign receipts with the dataset's key, and restart. Only evidence that it is **stopped, with every restart prevented**, closes this question. Neither losing its VPN route nor rotating its sender credentials does: its scheduler, connectors and local commands keep writing, and a provider may honor an issued token after a revocation (§8.10.6).
- **Sender fence**, per channel (APNs, provider mail or SMTP, Twilio, model provider): can a previous Core still submit to that provider? Only the writer fence answers it: no provider documents that a credential change invalidates every credential an old writer may hold within a bound, so rotating or revoking credentials is containment, recorded as information, and never closes either question (§8.10.6).

**Volumes and copies.** Every volume gets a random **volume ID** at `init`, `restore` or `migrate run`, recorded in the lock file, `health.json`, the recovery ledger, every journal record and `volume.json` — `{datasetID, volumeID, createdAt}`, owner-only, written once, so host-local tools can identify a stopped volume without opening its database (§8.11.5). **A volume ID names a volume's history, not one copy of it:** a copied data directory, a VM or storage snapshot, or a restored volume image carries the same volume ID and the same Core key, and each copy can start on its own. Lisa therefore never closes a writer by its volume ID. Each startable copy is its own inventory entry and needs its own fence evidence.

**Restore bootstrap and writer inventory.** Run steps 1–3 offline on the selected host. After stage 1 starts, the listener owns inventory, challenge issuance, imports and attestations. Step 5 also requires an offline operation under the stopped previous writer's own volume lock.
1. **Verify the destination, anchor and signer before publishing a restore intent.** Run `lisa-deploy offline restore <backupID> --operation <id> [--fresh] [--key-backup <dir>] [--reanchor]`. The CLI generates and prints a random operation ID before beginning if omitted. A restart uses the recorded ID. Bind the command to the owner's selected physical directory/volume and expected dataset. The verification harness supplies the run's independently recorded resource identity (§10.8). Under its exclusive directory lock use the following mutually exclusive paths.
   - **Fresh.** Require explicit `--fresh`, the designated empty destination, the expected anchored backup dataset (or explicit re-anchoring), and confinement of all paths to that physical resource. Accept only the directory and its reserved lock file, with externally mounted input backups and secrets. The check neither opens SQLite nor creates an empty database. An unknown file, leftover WAL or a manifest on an otherwise empty target requires inspection.
   - **Existing.** Require the expected installed logical generation. Read the committed dataset, identity and schema with SQLite and its WAL. Cross-check `volume.json`, the physical target and the expected predecessor. Reject an unrelated store before any replacement.
   - **Interrupted or already activated.** Match the durable intent or activation receipt to the operation ID, physical target, predecessor (or designated fresh state), dataset, backup/anchor digest, signer identity and allocated successor. The phase journal names the old, aside and staging locations and identities, and permitted pre/post states of the next atomic action. Inspect those particular locations under the same lock and reconcile a rename or activation that landed just before its phase record. Refuse extra/conflicting files, another operation or an unexpected successor. A completed retry returns the original receipt. Missing active SQLite is permitted only for the designated fresh case or the precise recorded phase that accounts for its absence.
   - **Select the signer.** Validate the backup and merge verified local/off-host key transitions before selecting the newest known active signing key. Verify possession using the configured service secret or the owner's read-only key backup. In the backup-only case the owner mounts that protected source as the matching service secret for bootstrap and later startup, keeping secrets outside the data volume. Missing, retired or mismatched input stops an anchored restore before fencing, activating, or changing any live dataset. `--reanchor` is the owner's explicit alternative with a new independently established key and the §8.5.6 quarantine. Once inputs match, sync the intent and its parent directory. Record the input digests, physical identity, phases and newly allocated volume/epoch, excluding private material. An unfinished intent prevents serving either generation.
2. **Fence this host under the same lock.** A fresh destination records that it replaced no writer. For an existing generation, journal the predecessor and intended aside name before moving it. Durably fence the predecessor in place before renaming it, include its committed WAL safely, and record its terminal journal chain and commit position. Sync files and parent directory at each publication boundary. A restarted operation derives the actual phase from verified files and intent, so it neither recreates nor removes a missing active store. Only this replaced volume gets local fence credit.
3. **Install stage 1 offline.** Build the catalogue from the backup roots and recovery ledger. Stage the restored database, volume identity and evidence-only gate, invalidate restored bearers and suspend delivery. Use the verified signer from step 1 to sign the epoch record binding the backup epoch and cut. Flush the generation and atomically publish its activation and recoverable receipt under the lock. On start, the configured signer must match the activated key. The owner starts `lisa-deploy up` only after activation and uses the stage-1 listener for inventory, attestation, fence and trust-evidence codes. No code can be issued at stage 0. Device reports extend the preliminary candidate list. Inherited records after `--reanchor` close no fence.
4. **The owner's inventory.** The owner enters every **writer candidate** they know of, from their own knowledge: each host, VM, container definition and data volume — **every snapshot and copy as its own entry** — that holds this dataset and could start Lisa Core without a new restore, and every restore made from any backup set of this dataset. `lisa core fence inventory add <label> [--volume <volumeID>|unknown] [--host <host label>] [--kind host|restore|copy|snapshot]` records each one under its own **entry ID** (`W-` followed by six base32 characters); two byte-for-byte copies are two entries with the same volume ID. A candidate whose volume ID the owner can't read is entered with `--volume unknown` and stays unaccounted until fenced. Then `lisa core fence inventory attest` shows both lists side by side, refuses if any volume in Lisa's list has no entry in the owner's (the message names it), and otherwise records the owner's typed statement that the inventory is complete, with the search checklist of `docs/OPERATIONS.md` § "Finding every Lisa Core" (hosts and VMs the owner controls, container engines and their stopped containers, systemd units and scheduled jobs, VM and storage snapshots, copied data directories, restores made from any backup set, and other people with access), the time and the owner's text. The attestation is owner evidence: Lisa can show that it is consistent with what Lisa knows, never that it is complete — in particular, it can't count copies.
5. **Writer-fence states,** one per inventory entry:

   | State | Evidence | Established by | Restart refusal | Closes the writer fence | Binds the journal's end (§8.10.6) |
   |---|---|---|---|---|---|
   | `fenced-local` | This host's replaced volume, fenced under its lock | Lisa (step 2) | **Enforced by Lisa:** the schema marker makes every start on that volume refuse | Yes | Yes |
   | `fenced-on-host` | Response to this entry’s portable challenge, produced under the lock on its stopped previous writer and authenticated on the restored listener as specified below | Lisa, on that copy's volume | **Enforced by Lisa** (marker), on the copy where the owner ran it | Yes, for that entry only | Yes |
   | `owner-attested-stopped` | The owner states, for one entry and following `docs/OPERATIONS.md` § "Stopping a previous Core", that the container is removed (not only stopped), or the host is powered off and kept off, or the volume is destroyed, **and** that every automatic start is disabled (restart policy, systemd unit, Compose `restart:`, scheduled jobs). The method, the stop time, the attestation time and the owner's text are recorded | The owner (`lisa core fence attest --stopped <entry>`) | **Attested by the owner, not enforced:** nothing on that volume refuses a start | Yes | No |
   | `unknown` | Anything else, including a fence record whose MAC doesn't verify for this entry — made before its code was issued, for another entry, on another restore, or inherited from a backup — which is shown as an **unbound fence record** | — | None | No | No |

   **Why a code as well as a signature.** The signature can't tell two copies of one volume apart — they hold the same key and volume ID — and after `--reanchor` the key itself isn't trusted. The code shows that the owner ran this fence, for this entry, after this restore began. Lisa can't see whether the owner ran two entries' codes on the same copy; the documented procedure runs each on its own host, and each evidence record keeps the host label the owner gave.

   **Portable fence challenge.** On the restored listener, `fence code <entry> --out <file>` issues a signed, versioned challenge file and displays the eight-word, 24-hour code separately. The file binds dataset, restore epoch and signing key fingerprint, inventory entry and expected old volume (or explicitly unknown), restored backup ID and manifest digest, authenticated ancestor segments and exact parent cut, nonce, expiry and a domain-separated verifier for the separate high-entropy code. The restored listener enforces expiry using its clock. The owner carries the file to that entry’s physical host, verifies the restored key fingerprint there against the restored console through an independent channel (including after re-anchoring), and enters the code through a protected prompt. The file and ordinary logs contain no code. For an unknown target volume the owner confirms the observed dataset, old volume and host against that entry before fencing. Keep challenges and outputs owner-readable only.

   **Offline execution and response.** `fence --local --challenge <file> [--export-tail <dir>]` requires no network or restored database. Verify pinned challenge signature, code against its signed verifier, dataset, identity and format under the old volume’s exclusive lock before changing anything. Reconcile committed WAL, persist a restart-blocking intent, terminal journal record and durable fence marker idempotently. Refuse serving after an interrupted fence until it completes. Compare the challenged cut with the old volume’s authenticated lineage. Export signed segments outside that cut, including post-fork segments, with their coverage and exact-byte/metadata/missing ranges (§8.4.5). Where common history or retained bytes cannot be proved, report the gap and keep affected work unresolved. Never substitute local time or another epoch’s sequence number for the cut. The old key signs the response. A MAC derived from the separate code covers the challenge digest and entire response, including observed volume, final run/sequence/chain hash and commit position, marker digest, tail digest and coverage. Carry the response and any tail back.

   **Import, expiry and repeat.** `fence import <file>` on the restored listener verifies the active nonce, restore epoch/key, entry, target binding, cut and expiry, old signature as an identity claim, code MAC, terminal and marker bindings and any archive integrity/coverage. Preserve the re-anchor rules below. Consume the nonce with the durable receipt. Reconcile a lost reply by exact response digest even after expiry. An expired, superseded, wrong-target or wrong-cut unused response gains no credit. Reissue on the restored listener with a new nonce and separate code, carry it back to the same physical copy, and rerun against its existing marker under lock. A fenced copy can sign its immutable terminal facts again and regenerate a tail for the newly supplied cut, without reopening for writes or appending a second terminal. Incomplete uploads stay quarantined. Keep challenges and responses separate per entry, including byte-identical copies.

   **Information, never a fence:** `lisa core fence note --client-isolated <entry>` (its VPN route removed), `--credentials-rotated <entry>` (sender credentials rotated or revoked, with the channels) and `--unreachable <entry>` (the owner can't reach the host) are recorded and shown in the review, and leave the state `unknown`.
6. **One evidence record per inventory entry,** in the recovery ledger and its off-host copies: `{entry ID, writer label, volume ID or "unknown", host label, inventory entry and attestation digest, state, established by, method, time, restart refusal "enforced by marker" or "attested", marker digest and code binding (fenced states), final run, journal sequence, chain hash and commit position (fenced states), attested stop time (attested state), tail archive digest and its retained-evidence report (if exported), staging state of the tail, information notes}`. `lisa core fence status` prints every record; the restore review and the delivery review cite them.

**Fence evidence after `--reanchor` is independently supported.** Re-anchoring trusts the backup's data, not its records about trust or writers. Only evidence created under the new anchor counts: a code-bound `fenced-on-host` record imported into this restore, `fenced-local` for this host's replaced volume, or an attestation the owner makes in this restore's inventory. Inherited fence records, attestations and evidence records are shown as history ("recorded in the unverified backup: H1 fenced on host at <time>") and close nothing; an inherited exported tail is staged as unverified evidence and never replayed unless a code-bound fence exports it again.

**Example: two byte-for-byte copies.** The owner inventories `W-3K7Q2D` "H2 on the server" and `W-8M4T1X` "copy of H2's volume on the NAS", both with volume V. Each gets its own code. The owner runs `fence --local --challenge <file>` on the server with the first code and on the NAS with the second, or fences one and attests the other stopped. A record carrying one entry's MAC never closes the other, so the inventory stays open until both are accounted for.

**Evidence-collection mode.** While the inventory is unattested, while Lisa knows a candidate the inventory lacks, or while any inventory entry is `unknown`, the restored Core stays at stage 1 (§8.5.4):
- **Open:** challenges, evidence uploads and trust bundles into quarantine, **evidence staging** (fence imports, exported tails, newer-backup imports and uploaded bodies verified and stored in quarantine, with nothing applied), receipt lookups, fence codes, fence imports and attestations, capabilities, read-only status, and the `trust review` and `fence status` displays.
- **Closed:** every ordinary write — v2 operations, v1 mutations, CLI owner mutations, imports; replay; the scheduler (planning, reminder sync and firing, briefs, `recover`); connectors; the outbox, every delivery and new notification assignments; head confirmation; credential refresh.

A confirmation then would publish a second live head while another writer may still extend the first.

**When the replacement may promote.** New candidate evidence invalidates an older inventory attestation. Stage 2 opens only when the owner's inventory is attested and holds every candidate in Lisa's list, and every inventory entry is `fenced-local`, `fenced-on-host` or `owner-attested-stopped` with evidence accepted for this restore (with the other stage-2 conditions, §8.5.4). Whatever a previous writer wrote after the cut is then recovered from devices' journals, from its exported tail, or not at all, and the restore review says which, per range, with the retained-evidence classes of §8.4.5. Derived writes (plans, briefs, reminder schedules) are derived again at stage 3b. Connectors read their sources again from the restored cursors, and deduplication stops a second ingestion (§8.19.2). Firings whose deliverable window overlaps a writer's possible activity stay held (§8.10.6).

**Mail evidence has two independent results.** Verify the authenticated sending account, Sent record, expected sender/recipient and slot-specific Message-ID (the stored identifier for a migrated R1 delivery). Use provider message references and envelope metadata where available. A match proving provider acceptance closes that slot to automatic resend, even if the writer is unknown. A possible match stays held for review. Store the provider reference and the attribution evidence separately. The optional `Lisa-Writer: <volume ID>.<run generation>` contains opaque random identifiers. Compare them for equality only. Neither copies sharing a key/volume/run nor provider timestamps identify a physical copy.

| Additional evidence | Writer classification | Recovery action |
|---|---|---|
| The independently matched provider acceptance is linked to the exact slot, claim, run and provider reference in the current writer’s authenticated journal | Current journal accounts for the submission | Cite that record. If cloned history leaves physical origin ambiguous, show the ambiguity. |
| A fenced candidate’s verified, entry-bound terminal chain independently links the same acceptance to the same claim, run and slot | Accounted for in the previous history (`sentByPreviousWriter`); copies sharing the same records may remain indistinguishable | Cite the chain and evidence record. Ordering is by verified sequence within that chain. |
| No unique journal link, including missing, malformed or foreign header, a shared volume ID, lost tail, uncertain clock, or a match by timestamp alone | Provider outcome as proved; writer unverified | Retain the record, show potential candidates, and request inventory review. A header alone never establishes discovery or clears an ambiguous hold. |
| Independent authenticated evidence establishes an unaccounted active writer or conflicting lineage, e.g. a challenged device returns a verifiable competing head | Discovery, independently of whether the slot’s submission can be attributed | Record discovery and contain atomically. A newly identified candidate requires a fresh inventory attestation and its own fence evidence. |

Twilio's call log and APNs carry no writer identity. A call matching the number and window is recorded as a possible submission by some writer — it may be another call to the same number — and is never a discovery by itself (§8.10.6).

**Another writer discovered after promotion.** The owner's inventory can be wrong. Any of these is a **discovery**, recorded with its evidence:
- a device reports, by challenge or ordinary sync, a confirmed head, epoch or volume ID that isn't in this Core's lineage or inventory (for example, two restores that each confirmed sequence n+1, which devices refuse as a tie, §8.5.2);
- authenticated live `backup ingest` or `fence import` supplies independent evidence of a competing confirmed head or a writer outside the inventory (§8.4.4). A files-only scan produces the report the owner brings to the listener;
- a provider investigation produces independent evidence of another writer as specified above;
- the owner reports one (`lisa core fence inventory add` after promotion).

**One sequence, for a discovery and for every restore.** Each step names who acts, what is recorded, what a restart does and what it opens. A first restore finishes offline bootstrap, starts the stage-1 listener, and runs steps 2–9. Step 2 accounts for every candidate, including a locally fenced predecessor where one existed. A discovery starts at step 1. No step needs an authority a later step grants.

| # | Step | Who acts | Durable record | After a restart | Opens |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Discovery and containment** | Core, automatically, in the transaction that records the evidence | The discovery record; the dataset state `contained`. Serialize this transaction with new writer admissions and scheduler/outbox claims. A claim that linearized before containment remains tracked as in flight. A later claim cannot start. Ordinary writes, replay, the scheduler, connectors, the outbox and new assignments stop at once; a delivery already submitting finishes and is journaled. Devices get `trust_recovery` with "Another Lisa Core is active for this data", keep their work in their journals and keep the last head they accepted. Nothing committed is discarded | Stays contained | Stage 1's evidence collection only |
| 2 | **Inventory the writer** | The owner (`fence inventory add`) | An entry for each candidate, initially `unknown` except for its matching local fence from bootstrap | — | — |
| 3 | **Fence it** | Owner issues `fence code <entry> --out <file>` here, carries file and separate code to the old host, stops that writer and runs `fence --local --challenge <file> --export-tail <dir>` there, or attests that entry stopped | Active nonce and expiry, terminal marker, bound response and per-entry receipt on import | Resume intent on the old copy. Reissue expired unused challenges and obtain a fresh response over the existing marker. Exact imported responses return the prior receipt | — |
| 4 | **Stage its evidence** | Core, at `fence import`; devices, by challenge uploads | The tail and uploaded bodies **staged, never applied:** each verified (signature, code MAC, the chain against the fence record's bound hash, retained-evidence classes) and stored in quarantine with a staging record per chunk. Signed device inventories can declare exact receipted operations and their dependencies before bodies arrive (§8.5.4) | Staging resumes from the last recorded chunk; an incompletely staged tail can't be confirmed, only completed or set aside | — |
| 5 | **Attest the inventory again** | The owner (`fence inventory attest`) | A new attestation covering the new entry | — | Stage 2 eligibility |
| 6 | **Authoritative membership confirmation** (stage 2) | The owner (`trust confirm`), only when every entry is fenced, the inventory attested, revocations merged and every staged item verified or set aside (§8.5.4) | A confirmation whose sequence exceeds every known head and supersedes the discovered head (§8.5.2). Its `reviewedEvidenceDigest` fixes the **replay set**: the finite manifest in §8.5.4, including eligible receipted entries declared by devices with outstanding payloads | Resumes at stage 3a | Credential refresh; stage 3a |
| 7 | **Eligible replay** (stage 3a, restricted) | Core | Per item, its disposition in the same transaction as its effect — applied, duplicate (never applied twice), rejected and kept, or held under an explicit reviewed disposition with reason — and the replay cursor. A replayed writer's submission journal is merged as delivery evidence; owner commands are re-checked under the merge rules; derived state is marked for re-derivation, not derived | Resumes at the next undecided item; only manifest items and their committed dependency bytes are admitted | Stage 3b, once the replay report shows every item decided |
| 8 | **Readmission of ordinary work** (stage 3b) | Core, automatically, when step 7 completes | The stage record | Ordinary intake reopens; the scheduler starts only after holds and intervals reload (§8.11.4), and derives plans, briefs and reminder schedules again before `recover` runs; delivery stays closed | — |
| 9 | **Delivery review** (stage 4) | The owner (`delivery review`, then `resume`) | Holds computed from every entry's evidence record, the discovered writer's merged journal among them; the review lists firings both Cores may have delivered, because a duplicate already sent can't be withdrawn | Holds reload before any channel | The outbox, channel by channel; new notification assignments |

**What survives outside the rewound database:**
- the recovery ledger on the data volume: lineage, confirmations, trust records, the inventory and its attestations, fence codes and writer-fence evidence records, discovery records, the staging records, sender-fence records, the submission journal and the backup index;
- the staged evidence in quarantine;
- backup sets;
- the secret files;
- off-host exports;
- every device's replica and evidence;
- a previous writer's own volume, and its exported tail, if the owner can reach it.

If the whole data volume is lost, only off-host copies and devices remain, and the review in §8.5.3 shows that.

**Recovery facts (fencing).** *Survives:* the inventory, its attestations, the codes and the per-entry evidence records, with the final positions they bind, in the ledger and its off-host copies; staged tails; a reachable old volume. *Who acts:* the owner, who inventories every candidate and copy, stops each old Core and runs the code-bound fence on its host or attests under the documented procedure; Lisa cross-checks the inventory against its own list, verifies fence records and their codes, stages evidence and contains a discovered writer. *Safe after an interruption:* `restore`, `fence --local`, inventory entries, codes, imports and attestations are idempotent and resume from their ledger records; containment is recorded in the same transaction as the discovery, so a restart stays contained; every stage resumes at its recorded point. *What the owner sees while a fence or the inventory is open:* "Previous Lisa Core on <host, volume> may still be running and changing data — through its schedule, its connectors or local commands — even if your devices can't reach it. This Lisa Core accepts no changes until you stop it with restart prevented (Operations › Stopping a previous Core) or fence it on its host with a code from `lisa core fence code <entry> --out <file>`." With the inventory unattested: "List every place this data could run, each copy separately (Operations › Finding every Lisa Core), and attest the list before Lisa can accept changes." After a discovery: "Another Lisa Core (<label>) has been active since <time>. Lisa stopped scheduling and delivery here. Fence it and import its tail; Lisa will ask you to confirm before anything from it is applied."

#### 8.5.2 Lineage, confirmations and the current head

- **Epoch record:** `{epochID, parentEpochID, parentCut, restoredBackupID, createdAt, coreKeyID}`, signed.
- **Owner confirmation:** `{epochID, confirmationSequence, supersedes: [epoch heads], reviewedEvidenceDigest}`, signed by Core when the owner runs `lisa core trust confirm` (§8.5.4).
  - `confirmationSequence` is one more than the highest sequence in the recovery ledger, in the restored database, and in every device report received during recovery.
  - `supersedes` lists every head known at that moment that isn't an ancestor of the new epoch, including a sibling restore of the same backup.
  - `reviewedEvidenceDigest` covers the inventory attestation, every writer-fence evidence record and the **replay set**: canonical manifest, evidence and explicit dispositions (including known items awaiting bodies) that the confirmation authorizes stage 3a to process (§8.5.4).
- **How a client chooses the current head.** It accepts a head H only if H carries an owner confirmation that the client verifies (Core-signed with a key in its key history, or after re-anchoring), that confirmation has the highest sequence the client knows for the dataset, and one of these holds:
  - H descends from the client's head through verified epoch records;
  - H's confirmation supersedes the client's head, directly or through a chain of confirmations.

  **A confirmed active head is required for both moves.** An epoch without a confirmation (recovery stages 0–1) is never accepted as current, whether it descends from the client's head or is a sibling of it. The client deals with such an epoch only through challenge-authenticated evidence uploads or a trust bundle.

  So when two restores of the same backup both carry valid confirmations, the later one names the earlier in `supersedes` and has a higher sequence. A client holding either one can verify which is current.
- **When the supersession can't be shown:** a client whose head is neither an ancestor nor superseded refuses ordinary sync with H. It still authenticates by challenge and reports its head as recovery evidence; Core records it as a **discovery** (§8.5.1) and lists it in `lisa core trust review` as an unknown head. After containment, fencing and the staging of its evidence, the owner issues a supplementary confirmation that supersedes it, and the client accepts that; the staged evidence is replayed only afterwards, at stage 3a (§8.5.1, §8.5.4).

  A tie can't be resolved automatically, because devices never pick between confirmed heads by time.
- **The confirmation sequence also orders notification commands** across epochs (§8.10.2), so a supplementary confirmation outranks every command issued before it.

#### 8.5.3 Trust evidence and key history after a restore

- **Collected before confirmation, by challenge (§8.1.5):** each device uploads its newest roster, epoch records and confirmations, key-transition records, its assignment inventory and observations (§8.10.2), and an inventory of the operations it retains: IDs, digests, origin positions and receipts, not bodies.
  - Bodies may also be uploaded into a quarantined staging area.
  - Nothing is applied before confirmation.
- **Merging:** revocations are a union and are kept. Key transitions are verified as a chain from the anchored key history: K1→K2 signed by K1 and K2.
  - A revocation signed by K2 after the backup is verified through that chain and applied before any work is admitted.
  - Devices enrolled after the backup are re-registered as "membership unconfirmed" until a verified roster names them.
  - `lisa core trust import` adds the recovery ledger's trust records and off-host exports.
- **Core's own key.** A recovered K1→K2 transition proves only the public-key history. Core signs as K2 only after obtaining K2's private half from the verified bootstrap secret/key-backup input, or by stopping the installed service and using `lisa core trust import-key` for a transition discovered after bootstrap (§8.1.4). Until then it signs no roster, confirmation or receipt. If K2's private half can't be found, devices that already trust K2 refuse K1-signed records after K2's activation, Core never signs with K1 again, and re-anchoring every device (§8.1.4, §8.5.6) is the only way forward.
- **A device that alone holds newer trust evidence, and whose key Core knows.** Suppose only the iPad holds the K1→K2 record and K2-signed rosters (with a revocation made after the backup), because the ledger copy that reached the new host predates them. The iPad refuses the restored head, which can be signed only with K1. It then offers a bounded, **evidence-only** exchange, which the owner starts on the device: "Lisa Core was restored from an older backup and can't yet prove it holds your newest key. Send this device's trust records to Lisa Core?"
  - It authenticates with a `trust-evidence` challenge (§8.1.5) and uploads only signed trust artifacts, within the bundle bounds below. It accepts nothing from the restored Core in that exchange.
  - Core verifies the artifacts as below, merges the revocations before any admission, and shows them in `trust review`.
  - The owner stops the evidence-only service, imports K2's matching private half offline against the merged transition, and restarts evidence collection. If the newly learned activation invalidates the signature on the provisional epoch, create a fresh signed epoch under K2 in the locked restore procedure, expire prior-epoch codes, rebind the inventory/fence evidence, and confirm only the valid lineage. With no K2 private key, the owner can re-anchor under §8.5.6.
- **A device whose key Core doesn't know.** A device that enrolled after the backup, or rotated its key after it, can't answer any challenge, because its current key isn't in the merged roster. Its trust records reach Core as an **owner-mediated trust bundle**:
  1. **Carrier.** The owner runs `lisa core trust evidence-code` at the administrative terminal on the evidence-only listener from stage 1 onward, including the later recovery of a returning device; stage 0 has no listener. It prints a one-time code of eight words, valid for 10 minutes and for one bundle, bound to this epoch and run generation; at most three are outstanding. On the device, "Send trust records to a restored Lisa Core" asks for the code and uploads the bundle to `POST /v2/recovery/trust-bundles`, authenticated by an HMAC keyed from the code. Without a network path, the device exports the bundle to a file, and the owner runs `lisa core trust import-evidence --file <bundle>`.
  2. **Bounds.** A bundle holds only signed trust artifacts: at most 64 roster pages, 16 key transitions, the epoch records and confirmations they reference, and the revocations they carry, in at most 4 MiB. It holds no operation, body or credential. Core accepts at most 16 bundles per recovery and refuses an oversized one before allocating it.
  3. **Quarantine, then verification from an independent anchor.** Nothing in a bundle is trusted on arrival. Each record is verified from the restored backup's **anchored** key history (§8.4.4), or from the owner's key backup: a key transition must be signed by both of its keys and chain from the anchor; a roster must belong to this dataset, be complete (every page with its inclusion proof) and be signed by the key valid at its version. Each record ends as one of:
     - `verified`;
     - `invalid` — a wrong signature, dataset or inclusion proof, or a forged transition: refused, logged as a security event, never merged;
     - `incomplete` — missing pages, or a roster signed by a key whose transition is missing: kept in quarantine as "unverified — needs <record>", with nothing merged. It is verified again when the missing record arrives in another bundle or through `trust import`.
  4. **Applied before anything is admitted.** Verified revocations are merged at once: before stage 2 can open, and before any credential refresh or write. A verified roster that names the device's current key makes that key known, so the device can then answer a `recovery-evidence` challenge and continue like any other device.
  5. **Nothing flows back.** The device accepts nothing from the restored Core during the upload.
  - The restore review asks: "Did any device enroll or change its key after <backup time>? Its trust records may hold changes this Lisa Core doesn't know, such as a revocation. Collect them before confirming: Trust › Import from a device."
  - A bundle never substitutes for Core's own key: without K2's private half, re-anchoring (§8.1.4, §8.5.6) remains the path.
- **A head or volume a bundle or device names** that the inventory lacks is a discovery (§8.5.1): before promotion it blocks stage 2 until the owner inventories and fences it; after promotion it triggers containment.

#### 8.5.4 Admission order and gates

| Stage | Opens | Requires | Stays closed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0. Restore and local fencing (§8.5.1) | Nothing | An anchored backup, or `--reanchor` (every inherited roster entry quarantined and no inherited fence evidence counted, §8.5.6, §8.5.1) | Everything. Recovery commands requiring a listener begin only after stage 1 is committed offline and the evidence-only service starts |
| 1. Evidence collection and staging | Challenges, evidence uploads and trust bundles into quarantine; **staging** of fence imports, exported tails, newer-backup imports and uploaded bodies (verified and quarantined, never applied); receipt lookups; inventory entries, fence codes and attestations, backup evidence ingestion and pre-confirmation replay holds; capabilities; read-only status; the `trust review` and `fence status` displays | Nothing more; the inventory may be open and writer fences unknown | Every ordinary write, replay, the scheduler, connectors, the outbox and new notification assignments, confirmation, credential refresh |
| 2. Membership confirmation (`lisa core trust confirm`) | The confirmation record binding the reviewed replay manifest, the new head, and credential refresh (§8.5.5) | The owner's inventory attested and holding every candidate Lisa knows; every inventory entry fenced with evidence accepted for this restore (§8.5.1); every verified revocation merged; every trust bundle verified, refused, or set aside by the owner with `lisa core trust set-aside <bundle>`, which records that its unverified contents stay unknown; every staged item verified or set aside the same way; after `--reanchor`, every inherited roster entry validated, re-enrolled, retired or set aside (§8.5.6) | Replay, ordinary work, delivery |
| 3a. Replay (restricted) | Applying only operations and dependency bytes authorized by the finite manifest, whether staged already or supplied by a later resubmission matching its entry; merging a replayed writer's submission journal as delivery evidence; backup evidence quarantine, manifest-bound deferral and marking plans, briefs, reminder schedules and occurrence expansions for re-derivation | Stage 2 | All unlisted submissions, including ones with a valid old receipt (the device keeps them journaled and gets `trust_recovery` with stage `replay` and a retry time), v1 mutations, ordinary CLI owner mutations and ordinary imports, the scheduler (planning, reminder sync and firing, briefs, `recover`), connectors, new notification assignments, the outbox |
| 3b. Ordinary work | v2 operations, v1 mutations, CLI owner mutations, imports, explicit replay-hold settlement, connectors, and the scheduler, which first derives the marked state again and only then runs `recover` | The replay report: every item of the replay set decided — applied, duplicate, rejected and kept, or held under an explicit durable disposition with reason; waiting for bytes is unfinished work | The outbox, delivery and new notification assignments |
| 4. Delivery review and resume (`lisa core delivery review`, then `resume`) | Ordinary work including current-head replay-hold settlement; the outbox, channel by channel; new notification assignments | The holds of §8.10.6, computed from every entry's evidence record and reloaded | Held items until evidence or the owner settles them one by one |
| Contained (a discovery after promotion, §8.5.1) | As stage 1 | — | As stage 1, until steps 2–9 of §8.5.1's sequence reopen stages 2, 3a, 3b and 4 in that order |

**Finite replay manifest.** At stage 1, persist each signed inventory’s source device, dataset, operation ID and expected content digest or signed receipt binding it, originating epoch/lineage, and authenticated commitments to dependency operations and blob IDs/digests. Include exact entries from staged tails, newer backups and quarantined bodies. Deduplicate matching commitments. Conflicts are recorded and set aside or resolved before confirmation. Verify declared dependency commitments against authenticated evidence. If the required closure cannot yet be identified and bound, obtain the signed envelope/metadata, or use the stage-1 owner hold below for those entries and their dependants; a device declaration alone cannot authorize an arbitrary dependency. Show present bytes, missing bytes, conflicting evidence and explicit holds in the review. Persist the canonical sorted manifest and digest atomically with the signed confirmation.

**Holding before confirmation.** The authenticated owner uses `trust replay review <entry>` in stage 1 or while contained. Show recovery epoch, evidence version, root IDs/digests, known dependencies/dependants, gaps, conflicts and possible effects, with a canonical review digest. `trust replay hold --review <digest> --entry <id> --reason <reason>` records the explicit hold on that item and its affected dependent set. Bind the record to this head, evidence version, exact set and reason in the recovery ledger. Duplicates return the receipt. Reject a stale review and show changed members. Unidentifiable dependencies are unresolved and cannot authorize application. An item depending on unresolved evidence cannot be applied by omitting it from the displayed group. Stage 2 signs those dispositions into the finite manifest. Independent items can progress. The holds remain visible in the replay report.

At stage 3a accept only matching manifest bodies and content-addressed dependency bytes, in dependency order, after checking receipts, lineage, content, roster and revocation policy. Save the disposition and effect atomically. A missing required body/blob keeps 3a open across restarts. The owner can explicitly defer a named unavailable item and its dependants through the authenticated recovery listener using `trust replay defer --manifest <digest> --entry <id> --reason <reason>`. Show the exact dependent set and effects for review. Persist the owner-authorized disposition, affected IDs, reason and confirmation digest in the authenticated recovery ledger. A retry with the same decision returns the recorded result. A stale confirmation or changed dependent set requires fresh review. The signed manifest stays immutable. Deferred items stay inspectable and excluded from automatic application. Later matching bytes attach to the hold for an explicit review under the current head. Persist the replay report and transition to 3b atomically after every item has a terminal disposition. A manifest with no eligible entries may finish immediately. A valid but unlisted late receipt/body gets a retry-at-3b response and remains on its device; ordinary intake then runs the usual provenance, dependency, revocation and duplicate checks. New evidence of a competing head follows containment first (§8.5.1).

**Settling held or deferred items.** Later authenticated bytes attach to the hold across all intake routes. Arrival cannot release it. At 3b/4 the owner runs `trust replay review <entry>` to inspect the current-head review digest, exact root and dependent IDs/digests, receipts, bytes still needed, proposed effects and unresolved dependencies. Then `trust replay resolve --review <digest> --action accept|keep|reject --decision <owner-request-id>` selects only the displayed group. Keep preserves the hold and reason. Reject retains evidence and a durable rejection preventing automatic application if the bytes are submitted again. Accept requires exact authenticated bytes, dependency closure and order, current membership and revocations, lineage, conflict, tombstone, receipt/duplicate and owner-authority checks. Missing, inconsistent, revoked or stale material stays held or rejected with its reason. Unlisted dependencies require independent eligibility under current-head intake and fresh review. An unresolved dependant cannot silently become applied. Preserve original signed manifest and earlier outcomes. Record head, review, selection, decision ID, operator, reason, time and per-item receipt. Bind each effect and disposition atomically. Resume bounded batches under recorded authorization with fresh stage/head checks and return prior receipts on repeat. Conflicting reuse of a request ID fails. Containment pauses unapplied items and requires fresh review under the next confirmed head. Settling replay cannot reopen delivery.

**Gating during stages 0–3a and while contained:**
- v2 mutations get `trust_recovery` and retain their device journal entry. At 3a only matching manifest resubmissions and their authenticated dependency bytes may proceed.
- v1 mutations get HTTP 503 with the R1 code `unavailable` and a retry time. A frozen R1 queue keeps those entries pending, not uncertain (§1.6).
- Ordinary CLI mutations are refused with the stage named. A stage-3a-only `trust replay defer` disposition can record an explicit hold as described above.
- Stage 1/contained permits reviewed replay holds. Authenticated evidence-only backup ingestion obeys the discovery and stage rules in §8.4.4. Explicit replay hold settlement is available only at 3b/4.
- Connectors and the scheduler are paused, and the outbox is suspended.

**Replay at stage 3a, in dependency order:**
- Each operation is re-verified against the confirmed roster and current policy: signature, dataset, origin status.
- An operation from an origin revoked in the merged evidence is admitted only if it carries a receipt from a superseded epoch whose lineage places it before the revocation on that same lineage. Committed legitimate data stays committed. Every other operation from that origin is rejected and kept for inspection. The post-downgrade tool applies the same rule (§8.14.8).
- An operation from a quarantined inherited member (§8.5.6) is held, not applied, until the entry is validated or retired.
- Only entries of the **exact** retained-evidence class replay; decision-only ranges are reported, never replayed (§8.4.5).

**Restart at every stage.** Each stage is recorded before it opens, and a restart resumes at the recorded stage: at 1, staged items stay staged and nothing is applied; after the confirmation, at 3a, from the replay cursor, with ordinary intake still closed; at 3b, ordinary intake reopens and the scheduler starts only after holds and intervals reload (§8.11.4), with delivery still closed; at 4, each channel resumes per its own record, after the holds reload.

#### 8.5.5 Old receipts, credentials, in-flight work and delayed requests

- **Old receipts** from superseded epochs are provenance. They don't prove commitment in the new epoch: the operation is resubmitted. They serve only as evidence of pre-revocation commitment (§8.5.4) and as replay inventory.
- **Bearer credentials:**
  - The restore invalidates every device credential verifier issued before it. A reset or revocation after the backup would otherwise be undone, and an old leaked credential could work again.
  - From stage 2 on, and for as long as the dataset lives, a device active in the confirmed roster registers a fresh verifier with a `credential-refresh` challenge signed by its key. A quarantined inherited entry (§8.5.6) can't refresh.
  - **A late device** (offline through the whole recovery, already a member of the confirmed roster) gets `401` with the typed code `credential_invalidated_by_restore` on its first request. It refreshes its credential by challenge, verifies the confirmed head (§8.5.2), and then reconciles as usual. Nothing it holds is discarded. A late device whose current key the confirmed roster doesn't know uses a trust bundle first (§8.5.3). A late device that holds a head from an uninventoried Core triggers a discovery (§8.5.1).
  - Legacy v1 devices have no keys. They stay disabled until the owner re-approves each one in `trust review`, after which they keep their R1 credential. Any legacy revocation found in the recovery ledger stays applied.
- **In-flight operations** whose responses the old Core never returned are resubmitted from journals; identity rules make this safe.
- **Delayed requests:**
  - A v2 request naming the old epoch gets `epoch_mismatch`, and the client reconciles.
  - A v1 request that arrives late is processed from stage 3b on. A legacy request that the old branch applied after the cut is unknown to the restored database. Converting it again produces the same children, with the same IDs and bodies (§8.12.2), so the change is restored once, and no replica sees two bodies under one ID. Replaying the same request again finds the new parent.
  - Replay imported from a newer backup or an exported tail carries its v1 parents, children and administrative decisions (§8.12.2), so a later retry of the same request is answered as a duplicate.

**Recovery facts (admission).** *Survives:* the ledger's confirmations, inventory, fence evidence and verified trust records; quarantined bundles and inherited entries; devices' journals, heads and receipts. *Who acts:* the owner inventories, confirms, fences, validates or retires inherited entries and issues evidence codes; devices refresh credentials and resubmit by themselves. *Safe after an interruption:* each stage is recorded before it opens, and a restart resumes at the recorded stage; challenges and evidence codes die with the run generation and are reissued; a bundle stays in quarantine until it is verified. *What the owner sees when something is unknown:* the stage, the inventory and each writer's evidence record (restart refusal enforced or attested, with any information notes), which devices haven't reported, each bundle's verification state, each inherited entry's state, and which trust evidence is still missing ("Newest signing key known but not held: import it or re-anchor").

#### 8.5.6 Re-anchoring: inherited membership

`restore --reanchor` restores a backup whose anchor can't be verified (§8.4.4). Its data is restored, because the owner chose to trust it. Its **trust records can't be trusted**: without an anchor, the roster in it may contain an entry no owner ever approved — an extra device with its own key, or a raised role — and its records about previous writers may be stale or forged.
- **Every inherited roster entry is quarantined** (`inherited-unverified`): it can't answer a challenge, refresh a credential, submit or receive operations, receive deliveries, witness a head, or count as a member. Its key, role and history are kept and shown, never used. Operations committed in the backup under its key stay as data, labeled "authored by an unverified inherited member" in review.
- **Inherited writer evidence closes nothing.** Fence records, attestations and evidence records in the backup are shown as history; every inventory entry needs evidence created under the new anchor — a code-bound fence record or an attestation made in this restore's inventory (§8.5.1).
- **The new Core's own identity** is a new anchor, created at the restore and fingerprinted at the administrative terminal; devices re-anchor to it by the procedure of §8.1.4.
- **An entry leaves quarantine in one of three ways:**
  1. **Independently validated** (`lisa core trust inherited validate <entry>`): its key and role are proven by evidence that doesn't come from the unverified backup — a roster that names it, signed by a Core key the owner verifies from an off-host anchor fingerprint or key backup, or arriving in a verified trust bundle (§8.5.3) that chains to such an anchor. The entry is then a member with exactly the proven role.
  2. **Re-enrolled by the owner** (`lisa core device enroll --replaces-inherited <entry>`): the owner enrolls the real device afresh at the administrative terminal and chooses its role there. **Pairing authenticates only the device's current key to the new Core.** It doesn't show that the device is the one the inherited entry describes, nor that the inherited role was ever authorized. The new entry therefore takes only the role the owner grants now. It is linked to the inherited entry's history only if the device also signs a link challenge with the inherited key; otherwise the inherited entry stays separate and is retired.
  3. **Retired** (`lisa core trust inherited retire <entry>`) or **set aside** (`… set-aside <entry>`, which records that it stays unverified and inactive).
- **Before credential refresh or any write,** stage 2 requires every inherited entry to be validated, re-enrolled, retired or set aside.
- **Recovery facts.** *Survives:* the quarantined entries, with their keys and roles, and every validation, enrollment link and retirement record. *Who acts:* the owner. *Safe after an interruption:* each decision is one recorded transaction; re-running it returns the recorded result. *What the owner sees:* "Inherited from an unverified backup: Mac mini (owner device, key …4F2A). No device has proved this key since the restore. Validate, re-enroll or retire."

**Acceptance (M3; S-10, narrative §19.1):**
1. **Setup:** backup A under K1, then K1→K2 with K2's private half saved to a key-backup directory, then C revoked with K2. H2 restores A and starts stage 1. H2 issues a challenge for H1, the owner fences H1 on its host and imports the response on H2. Only then is H2 confirmed (E2) and allowed to run its scheduler.
2. **An uninventoried writer with every witness offline:** H3 restores backup A, whose ledger predates H2; every device that synced with H2 is offline. Lisa's list on H3 holds only H1. Until the owner's inventory is attested, `trust confirm`, a v2 operation, a v1 mutation, a CLI owner mutation, a scheduled reminder and a delivery on H3 will each be refused or stay stopped, naming the open inventory. In variant (i) the owner lists H2, which stays `unknown` and blocks stage 2. In variant (ii) the owner, mistakenly, attests an inventory without H2, H3 promotes, and then an iPad that synced with H2 comes online and reports H2's confirmed head: H3 will record a discovery and become `contained` in the same transaction (its scheduler, connectors and outbox stopped, ordinary writes and replay refused). It will then follow §8.5.1's sequence in order and no other: H2 inventoried; fenced with its own code and an exported tail; the tail staged with nothing applied; the inventory attested again; a supplementary confirmation superseding both heads and fixing the replay set; the tail replayed at stage 3a; ordinary work readmitted at 3b; firings whose windows overlapped H2's activity held at stage 4 (§8.10.6, S-11).
3. **A previous writer cut off from clients but still writing:** H2 keeps running. The owner removes H2's VPN route (`fence note --client-isolated`) while a plan recomputation and a reminder sync are due on H2 within the minute, and runs `lisa task add` on H2's own terminal. The harness will show H2's scheduler and local command committing after the isolation.
4. **Neither isolation nor credentials is a writer fence:** on H3, rotate the APNs, SMTP and Twilio credentials and revoke the provider mail grants (`fence note --credentials-rotated`); then try `trust confirm`, a v2 operation, a v1 mutation and a CLI owner mutation. Each will be refused, naming H2's `unknown` writer fence and showing both notes; challenges, evidence uploads, bundles, staging and `trust review` will work; connectors, scheduler and outbox will stay stopped.
5. **Marker-enforced versus attested restart prevention,** in two variants: (a) the owner stops H2, disables its restart and attests `owner-attested-stopped`; the evidence record will say "restart prevention attested by the owner, not enforced", and a harness that then re-enables H2's restart policy will find H2 starting normally, which Lisa on H3 can learn only through a discovery; (b) the owner stops H2, takes a code with `fence code` on H3, and runs `lisa core fence --local --challenge <file> --export-tail` on H2's volume, then `fence import` on H3 (`fenced-on-host`, binding H2's final run, journal sequence and head); the record will say "enforced by marker", and every start of H2's Core on that volume — by the restart policy, a unit or by hand — will refuse to open the database. Only then will `trust confirm` succeed. In variant (b), the tail will carry H2's local `task add` and its scheduler's writes, with their retained-evidence classes; they will be staged before the confirmation, the owner's task replayed at stage 3a, and the derived writes derived again at stage 3b. In variant (a), the review will list H2's post-cut writes as unknown unless a device holds them.
6. **Then, in order:** confirmation n+2 superseding E2 and fixing the replay set; credential refresh by Mac and iPhone; replay at 3a; ordinary work at 3b; delivery review run separately (S-11).
7. **Confirmed heads only:** before step 6, neither a client holding E1's head (descendant case) nor one holding E2's head (sibling case) will accept E3; after it, both will.
8. **A late, already-confirmed iPad** whose bearer the restore invalidated: `credential_invalidated_by_restore`, refresh by challenge, then sync of its pending work.
9. **A device that alone holds K2 evidence and whose key Core knows:** the off-host ledger copy predates K2; only the iPad holds the K1→K2 record and the K2 roster revoking C. The iPad refuses the K1-signed head and uploads by evidence-only exchange; Core merges C's revocation before admission. Variant (a): the owner imports K2's private half (a mismatching key is refused), and the iPad accepts the confirmed head. Variant (b): no K2 private half; Core signs nothing with K1 after K2, and re-anchoring proceeds.
10. **Devices whose keys Core doesn't know, carrying a valid newer roster:** device D enrolled after backup A, and the Mac rotated its key after A; only they hold roster v9 (K2), which revokes device E. Their challenges will be refused as unknown keys. With an evidence code, D's bundle will be verified from A's anchored key history and the K1→K2 record; E's revocation will be merged before `trust confirm` is allowed; D's and the Mac's current keys will become known, and their challenges will then succeed. Variants:
    - **invalid:** a bundle with a tampered roster page, and one from another dataset — refused, logged, nothing merged;
    - **incomplete:** v9 without the K1→K2 record when no ledger copy holds it — quarantined as "needs K1→K2"; `trust confirm` refused while it is pending; verified after `trust import` of the record, or set aside by the owner with the stated consequence;
    - an expired code, a reused code and a fourth outstanding code refused; an oversized bundle refused before allocation.
11. C's pre-revocation operation will be admitted at 3a through its reviewed manifest entry and same-lineage receipt, and its post-revocation operation rejected.
12. A client whose head the confirmation doesn't name will refuse it, report it, and accept the supplementary confirmation.
13. An old credential reset after A will stay refused, and a challenge replayed from the first restore will be refused.
14. **Re-anchoring an unverified backup with an extra entry:** an unanchored backup offered to `restore` will be refused. With `--reanchor`, the restore will proceed under a new anchor, and every inherited entry will be quarantined — including an extra active entry, "Mac mini" with key Kx and an owner-device role, that exists only in the backup and that no real device holds. Kx's challenge, credential refresh and operation submission will be refused; its committed operations will show as "authored by an unverified inherited member". Pairing the real iPhone will give it only the role the owner chooses; the iPhone's inherited entry will be linked only after the iPhone signs the link challenge with its inherited key, and a pairing without that signature will leave the inherited entry separate. `trust confirm` will be refused until "Mac mini" is retired or set aside, and nothing will ever refresh its credential.
15. **Fence evidence after re-anchoring:** the same unverified backup records "H1 fenced on host" and an attestation for H4. On the re-anchored Core both are shown as inherited history; H1 and H4 stay `unknown` and `trust confirm` is refused, naming them. A fence record H1's volume produced before the restore (no code) is imported and shown as an unbound fence record, closing nothing. Then `fence code W-…` for H1, `fence --local --challenge <file>` on H1's volume and `fence import` make H1 `fenced-on-host`; the owner attests H4 stopped in this restore's inventory; `trust confirm` then succeeds.
16. **Two byte-for-byte copies with one volume ID:** H2's volume V is copied to a NAS before H2 is stopped; the owner inventories both as separate entries. A code-bound fence run on H2 closes only H2's entry: importing the same record for the NAS entry is refused (its MAC names H2's entry), and `trust confirm` is refused while the NAS entry is `unknown`. In variant (a) the owner fences the NAS copy with its own code: both entries are `fenced-on-host`, each record naming its host label. In variant (b) the owner attests the NAS copy stopped and then, by mistake, starts it; a matching Sent record with a different random run and a later provider timestamp settles the slot if acceptance is verified, while writer attribution remains unverified. A subsequent independent challenge verifying the copy’s competing head triggers containment in the same transaction. Swap the lexical ordering of run IDs and repeat with a clone sharing the current run: the result depends only on evidence.
17. **Unknown writer versus confirmed discovery.** H1’s tail is lost. A verified Sent record for the slot with H1’s header at 10:15 settles the slot as submitted, writer unverified. Repeat with an absent header (including a migrated R1 message), malformed header, shared active volume ID and an unfamiliar volume ID. None alone establishes discovery. Wrong recipient/account stays unconfirmed; authenticated terminal-chain acceptance with a uniquely linked provider reference classifies prior history. A duplicate header from a copy cannot identify a physical host. A Twilio number/window match remains a possible submission only.
18. **Restarts around confirmation and replay, with nothing ordinary admitted early:** Core is killed (a) after the tail is staged and before `trust confirm`: after the restart it is at stage 1, the tail is staged and unapplied, and an iPhone capture without a superseded-epoch receipt, a v1 mutation and a CLI `task add` are refused; (b) after `trust confirm` and before replay starts: it resumes at 3a, the iPhone's new capture gets `trust_recovery` (stage `replay`) and stays in its journal, the iPhone's resubmission of X (carrying E2's receipt, and listed in the confirmed manifest) is applied, and no scheduler, connector or delivery runs; (c) halfway through the replay: it resumes from the replay cursor, every item is applied once, and ordinary intake stays closed until the replay report is complete; (d) after 3b opens: the scheduler starts only after holds and intervals reload, the regenerated reminder slots are held at creation, and nothing is delivered before `delivery resume` at stage 4; (e) while contained: it stays contained. In each case the database diff will show nothing applied outside the stage's rules.
   - **Fresh, existing and interrupted restore through the public wrappers (S-10/S-20/S-24).** Start with only the mounted K2 key backup on a genuinely empty H2 target. Run the designated-fresh command, verify K2 signed the new epoch before activation, start the evidence-only listener and import the first fence response. On an existing test target with committed WAL, kill after durable intent, fencing, the predecessor rename, staging, activation and before the harness receives its receipt. Resume the same operation to a single activated volume/epoch. Exercise an empty unselected volume, stray WAL, mismatching signer, unrelated populated database, substituted physical resource, stale receipt and different backup. Assert no target dataset, fence, signature, replacement or activation is changed by a refused operation. Stage-0 code requests produce no code.
   - **Manifest and late work:** enumerate X and Y1, with Y1 depending on blob b. Delay all three; ordinary work stays closed. Restart, supply X twice and Y1 before b; apply X once and hold Y1 until b verifies. Reject a digest mismatch; a valid unlisted old receipt gets a retry at 3b. An unavailable listed item and all dependent items require an explicit durable owner disposition before the gate opens. Replay after restart yields the identical report.
19. **Portable fence and live backup ingestion.** Run the old-host tool disconnected, with only its local volume, portable challenge, separately supplied code and independent restored-key fingerprint. Exercise a sibling fork (same numeric cut on different epochs), a missing range and `--reanchor`. Verify per-copy credit, wrong entry/target, altered cut/archive and expiry. Expire a challenge after fencing the old volume. A reissued challenge produces a valid response on that copy with unchanged marker, terminal hash and journal length. Stop after each durable boundary and reconcile receipts. At 3b and again at 4 scan a foreign, invalid, known-writer and genuinely new-writer backup. Scan alone never changes stage. Ingest via the owner listener alongside concurrent scheduler/write/outbox admission. Verify that authenticated new-writer evidence and containment are atomic with partial body transfer, crash, duplicate report and a changed head. Known-writer intake obeys stage and held-item rules.
20. **Replay-hold lifecycle.** Stage 1 has a missing dependency commitment. Review and hold the affected entries, change evidence to exercise stale-review refusal, then confirm the updated review. Separately defer a missing body and its dependant at 3a. Send valid late bytes and conflicting, revoked and missing inputs. Exercise keep, reject and authenticated acceptance at 3b/4, repeat a decision ID, restart mid-batch and trigger containment before an unapplied item. Assert effects once, blocked dependants, immutable manifest, durable per-item receipts, no automatic release or redelivery and durable audit after restart.

### 8.6 Clients: store, states, installation, export and privacy (R2-DATA-01–05, R2-SYNC-04, -05, -08, R2-LIMIT-02; findings 5, 6, 13; privacy and local behavior)

#### 8.6.1 Store, atomic change and states

- **Location (O-8):** one SQLite database per installation, in a replica directory holding the database (with committed blobs inside it, §8.16.3), its `-wal` and `-shm` sidecars, incoming blob staging, the storage ballast (§8.15.2) and export staging.
  - The directory is owner-only and protected until first unlock on iPhone and iPad.
  - It is excluded from device backups by setting `isExcludedFromBackup` on the directory, which covers its contents, and it is never copied between devices.
- **Contents:**
  - canonical merge states and derived records (calendar from 30 days back to 90 ahead, briefs for 30 days, plans in the window, evidence excerpts, source freshness);
  - tombstones and replay metadata;
  - the journal of local and relayed operations with signed bytes and receipts; drafts (§8.6.2); blobs;
  - peer progress and custody acknowledgements;
  - conflicts and draft resolutions;
  - local plans and briefs; plan intents;
  - notification assignments and scheduling results;
  - inference requests and budget; device-only settings;
  - the search index; quarantined R1 queue items;
  - partition cursors and coverage; the local change journal (§8.6.3); lineage, confirmations and key history.
- **Atomic local change (R2-DATA-02).** One write transaction:
  1. validates the command;
  2. allocates the operation ID and this incarnation's next sequence;
  3. signs through `ReplicaSigning`, or stores a draft if signing is unavailable (§8.6.2);
  4. stores the operation with its original input;
  5. updates the effective view and, through `ReplicaIndexing`, the index;
  6. appends to the local change journal and advances the local state generation;
  7. records follow-up work.

  The app says "saved" only after commit; disk-full, I/O and signing failures are reported as they are.
- **Three kinds of safety, never confused (R2-SYNC-04):**

  | State | Meaning | Shown as |
  |---|---|---|
  | Local save | Committed in this replica | "Saved on this device" |
  | Peer custody | A peer returned a signed acknowledgement that it durably holds these exact bytes | "Also held by <device>" |
  | Core commitment | A Core receipt in the current head's lineage | "Synced with Core" |

  Further states: submitted, committed with a conflict, rejected, waiting for dependencies, needs review. Peer custody protects against losing the originating device, but the origin never discards an operation because of it.
- **Effective view:** canonical state plus every pending operation in causal order, through LisaMerge. It is recomputed per affected item when an operation, page, receipt, rejection or revocation arrives. Revoked and rejected operations leave the view but stay in the journal.
- **Working set, provenance and time (R2-DATA-03–05):**
  - Defaults are configurable. Eviction removes cache only, never owner records, pending work, conflicts, uncertain delivery records or replay metadata.
  - A filtered replica says it is incomplete, and nothing missing is read as deleted.
  - Every item keeps its origin, source times, last retrieval or sync, revisions, interpretation status, and whether it is canonical, local or provisional.
  - The planning zone is a replicated preference. Instants are UTC milliseconds; timeouts and backoff use the monotonic clock.

#### 8.6.2 Saving before enrollment, before first unlock, or without signing keys

| Condition | What is stored durably | Status shown | What is recoverable |
|---|---|---|---|
| Not yet enrolled | Input as a **draft** (unsigned, with its original text and time) in the replica | "Saved on this device — not connected to your Lisa yet" | Drafts become signed operations after enrollment. Export before enrollment is described below the table |
| Before first unlock after a restart (iPhone, iPad) | Nothing: the replica and Keychain are unavailable, and nothing is written to an unprotected buffer | Siri: "Unlock your iPhone to use Lisa." A notification action does nothing and the notification stays | Nothing from that attempt. The input was never accepted, and Lisa doesn't claim otherwise |
| Signing key unavailable (Keychain error; a this-device-only key missing after restoring to another device) | Input as a draft; already-signed operations are unaffected | "Saved on this device — can't be sent until this device is enrolled again" | Drafts are signed under the new identity after re-enrollment. Old signed operations still submit unless their key was revoked |
| Disk full | Nothing new | "Not saved: storage is full" with the action needed | Earlier saves are intact (§8.15) |

**Exporting before enrollment.** No Core export key is known yet, so the export screen offers two recipients and says what each survives:
- **This installation only:** sealed to the device's own export key. The screen says before saving: "Only Lisa on this device can open this file. If this device is lost, reset or replaced, the file can't be opened." The archive header records the recipient kind `installation-only`.
- **Your Lisa Core's export key, entered as text:** the owner pastes or scans `lisa-export-key:1:…`, printed by `lisa core trust export-key` (the public key, dataset ID, Core signing-key fingerprint and a checksum). Its authenticity rests on the owner's custody of the text, as with pairing text. The header records the recipient kind `owner-supplied-core-key`, the key fingerprint and "not yet verified against a roster". Core or the post-downgrade tool opens such an archive with the matching private key from the owner's key backup. When the device later enrolls, it checks the entered key against the roster and records the result in the archive's inspection metadata.

If no Lisa Core exists yet, only the first choice is possible, and the warning stays on the screen.

#### 8.6.3 Snapshot installation (finding 6 preserved and strengthened)

This section is the **full snapshot** (first installation, or a new epoch). Repairing one partition (a bucket or a collection) never swaps tables; it follows §8.4.3.

- **Local change journal.** Every transaction that advances the local state generation G appends `(generation, kind, references)` to `local_changes`. Kinds: journal entry, draft change, receipt, rejection, revocation, canonical page, coverage or working-set change, conflict resolution.
  - Entries are kept until no staging needs them. The store can therefore retrieve the complete, ordered sequence of changes after any generation.
- **Staging.**
  - Record G0 and the snapshot cut.
  - Write verified chunks into staged tables inside the live database, each in its own short transaction.
  - Build the staged effective view and the staged search index from the staged canonical tables plus the journal as of G0.
  - Staging reads chunks from the network or blob files, never through a long database reader.
- **Catch-up passes.** Before the swap, `local_changes` after the last applied generation are applied to the staged view and staged index in passes of at most 512 changes. Each pass is a short transaction on staged tables, without holding the main write lock between passes.
  - The passes repeat until at most 64 changes remain, or five passes have run.
- **The installation point,** one write transaction. **The backlog is evaluated before anything is applied:**
  1. every chunk digest in the signed manifest has been verified;
  2. the backlog (changes in `local_changes` after the last applied generation) is counted. Staging is invalidated, without applying anything, and restarted from a newer cut, if any of these holds:
     - the backlog exceeds 2,048 changes;
     - a canonical page beyond the cut was installed after G0;
     - the epoch or coverage changed;
     - a change cannot be reconciled exactly;
  3. otherwise the backlog is applied;
  4. **never backward:** the cut must be at or after every installed partition cursor it covers, in the same epoch or a newer confirmed one (§8.5.2);
  5. **swap:** staged canonical tables, staged effective-view tables and the staged search index are renamed over the live ones together, and cursors, epoch and coverage are set. Journal, drafts, captures, conflicts, receipts and assignments are untouched.
- **Under continuous writes:**
  - Captures take the same write lock, so each commits before G0, between G0 and the swap (and is reconciled), or after the swap.
  - After three invalidations in an hour, the replica asks for a fresher cut and installs when the app has had no input for five seconds, or at the next launch.
  - Writers delayed by the swap are measured and counted in capture p95 (§8.15.3).
- **Crashes:** a crash before the swap leaves the old view; a crash after it leaves the new one. Everything stays in one database file, because SQLite in WAL mode does not commit atomically across attached databases.
- **Storage:** staging needs twice the snapshot size plus a 64 MiB reserve and stops before the reserve, so captures keep room. Reader abort and WAL limits are in §8.15.2.
- **Pages and absence across the swap.** Pages that arrive during staging are applied to the live tables as usual and are also recorded in `local_changes`, so the backlog carries them into the staged view; a partition being repaired defers them (§8.4.3). The swap installs every partition's membership records at the cut, so the absence rule of §8.4.2 holds from the first reader after the swap: an older bucket snapshot installed later can't make an event count in a partition whose newer proof excluded it.

**Acceptance (M2; S-06).** Expected outcomes are computed from the backlog at the installation point, before anything is applied:
- a rejection, and separately a revocation, arriving during staging: both will be reconciled;
- a newer canonical page during staging: it will invalidate staging;
- an older cut: it will be refused;
- captures during staging and at the swap, with latency measured, including writers delayed at the swap;
- **a burst that the passes absorb:** 3,000 changes made before the first pass. Five passes apply 2,560, the backlog at the installation point is 440 (≤ 2,048), and the installation **succeeds** with all 3,000 reflected;
- **a genuine above-threshold case:** the same 3,000, plus a writer adding 600 changes during each pass. The backlog at the installation point is 3,000 − 5 × 512 + 5 × 600 = 3,440 (> 2,048), so staging is **invalidated without applying them**, and a fresher cut is fetched;
- the boundary: a backlog of exactly 2,048 installs; 2,049 invalidates;
- process kills at each step on real files;
- disk exhaustion inside a rootless container tmpfs;
- **after the swap:** a delayed older snapshot of one bucket, containing an event that the full snapshot's newer cut proved absent from a neighboring bucket, will not make the event busy, listed or found there; a page that arrived during staging will be reflected exactly once.

#### 8.6.4 Journal retention and replay after a restore

- **Never dropped:** uncommitted operations and drafts.
- **Committed bodies:** kept until the catalogue pruning rule of §8.4.5 allows dropping them, or until cap eviction drops one on a device at its cap; an offline device prunes under the catalogue it last learned for at most 7 days. Each drop is recorded and reported, and the device's checkpoint and exports state the range as decision only or unknown (§8.4.5). Blobs follow their reference counts.
- **An overlay** leaves the view only when canonical state containing its operation is installed, never when the receipt arrives.
- **Caps:** 90 days or 64 MiB of committed bodies per replica. Evictions follow §8.4.5 and are reported.
- **After a new head:** the device resubmits every retained body committed after the restored cut, on any branch. Core re-verifies and applies them (§8.5.4), keeping the old receipts as provenance.
- **Recovery limits:**
  - Owner changes that reachable devices or backups still hold can be recovered.
  - CLI and v1 changes made after the backup are recovered only from a newer backup or a fenced writer's exported tail (§8.5.1), because their callers keep no signed journal.
  - Core-derived results are re-derived.
  - Deliveries are held (§8.10.6).

**Acceptance:**
- **M2, replica side:** a copied replica with its key material, restored after the device continued, will start a new incarnation with a correct chain and no sequence reuse; the overlay will stay visible until the canonical change installs.
