- **M3, with the real ledger:** the Core incarnation test. The fork will be recorded, pre-backup pending operations will return `duplicate`, and new ones will be accepted; a per-installation incarnation will serve as the negative control.

#### 8.6.5 The direct HTTPS path and the shared transport (R2-SYNC-08)

- **One production transport:** `LisaSyncHTTP` implements `CoreTransport` on LisaClient's `URLSessionTransport` (extended to `/v2/`), with the `Apps/Shared/Trust` evaluator injected. The X2 probe (built at M3) and the full apps (M5, through LisaClientCore) link this same module, so X2 exercises the production adapter.
- **Reconciliation order:**
  1. Authenticate and negotiate.
  2. Fetch trust, revocations, policy, the head and the backup catalogue.
  3. Stop using authority that is no longer valid.
  4. Recover the baseline: pages, or a snapshot when a cursor or epoch no longer fits.
  5. Submit pending operations in dependency order, uploading blobs first.
  6. Collect outcomes, then rebuild the view.
  7. Surface conflicts and rejections.
  8. Recompute plans, briefs and local schedules.
  9. Resume incremental sync.

  Pending work is never discarded before it is preserved.
- **Linux evidence:** URLSession on Linux can't use a custom anchor (R1 D-45). So the harness runs LisaSyncEngine over a test-only libcurl transport that performs the same pin and anchor evaluation, and runs `LisaSyncHTTP` itself over plain HTTP on loopback. The Apple trust path is device evidence (X2).

#### 8.6.6 Protected export and the archive format (O-8; finding 13)

**Required:** a protected export route for unsynchronized owner work (R2-LIMIT-02); local save, peer custody and Core commitment kept apart; the loss of an only copy explained (O-8).

**Mechanism**
- **Archive (`LisaArchive`, format `lisa-archive` v1).**
  - A header names the dataset, origin device, incarnations covered, creation time, each recipient with its kind (`core-roster`, `owner-supplied-core-key` or `installation-only`, §8.6.2) and key ID, the signer key ID, the roster version and the head known at export.
  - Typed, length-prefixed entries follow:
    - **signed operation:** exact bytes, known receipts and custody acknowledgements;
    - **blob:** digest and bytes;
    - **draft:** unsigned input, times and intended kind;
    - **conflict:** item, field, candidate versions and any draft resolution;
    - **quarantined legacy bytes:** the original R1 queue entry bytes, their class and evidence (§8.14.3);
    - **rollback acknowledgement** (§8.14.7);
    - **reviewed-set artifact:** the signed, paged list each exported resolution references (§8.3.2), so an import can apply the resolution exactly; an export never carries a resolution without its artifact while the device still holds it.
- **Sealing.**
  - A random key encrypts the payload with AES-256-GCM.
  - The key is sealed with HPKE (RFC 9180, DHKEM(P-256, HKDF-SHA256), HKDF-SHA256, AES-256-GCM) to Core's export key or keys from the roster, and to this installation's export key.
  - The device signs the result, or marks it unsigned if its key is unavailable.
  - HPKE comes from the approved swift-crypto `Crypto` module, which on Apple is CryptoKit's (iOS 17 and macOS 14, below the targets), so no new dependency is needed.
- **Writable destinations.**
  - On the Mac, an `NSSavePanel` under `com.apple.security.files.user-selected.read-write` lets the owner save outside the sandbox container: Documents, an external disk, a server share.
  - The old-queue import stays read-only: it is opened read-only, persisted only as a read-only security-scoped bookmark, and its file is checked unchanged after the import (§8.14.4).
  - On iPhone and iPad, the document picker's export mode writes to Files: On My iPhone, iCloud Drive or an external drive.
- **Opening and executing an archive.** Possible by the same installation while its keys survive, by `lisa core import-journal`, or by the post-downgrade tool (§8.14.8). Before executing any operation, the opener re-checks:
  - the dataset;
  - the signature;
  - **authenticity apart from decryption:** opening the archive with an export key only decrypts it; each entry is authenticated by the device's signature, checked against that device's key in the trusted roster;
  - **eligibility against trusted revocation evidence,** by the one rule `LisaArchive.eligibility` that `import-journal`, `lisa archive inspect` and `lisa archive reconcile` all use (§8.14.8): the origin must be active in the newest roster the opener trusts — Core's current roster, or after a rollback the Core-signed roster in the rollback archive, verified through the key history in the owner's key backup — and not revoked by any revocation the owner recorded since. An operation from an origin revoked in that evidence is eligible only with a Core receipt, on the same lineage, that places its commitment before the revocation. A device-supplied creation time or the archive's own copy of an older roster never establishes pre-revocation eligibility;
  - the current admission policy: Core's, or translatability to R1 after a rollback.

  Drafts, ambiguous entries and ineligible operations are never executed automatically. They go to a durable inspection list, where the owner can read each one and deliberately re-enter its content as a new owner action, or discard it.
- **Keys the owner must keep.** `docs/SECURITY.md` lists Core's current and retired export keys and the signing-key history as secret files to back up offline and restore separately (§8.11.2). Without them, nobody can open an archive after Core is lost, by design.
- **The only-copy risk.** Until Core commits it, work exists only on the devices holding it. An isolated device with the only copy loses it if the device is lost, wiped or restored from a device backup, because the replica is excluded from backups.
  - The app shows "Only on this device: n changes" and offers export.
  - Peer custody reduces the risk to losing both devices.
  - `docs/OFFLINE-BEHAVIOR.md` explains all of this.

**Acceptance (S-13):**
- **M5, Linux:** export, then reopen by the same installation and by `import-journal`, with no duplicates. Also:
  - a second installation will fail to open the file;
  - a modified file will fail its signature check;
  - a file sealed before an export-key rotation will open with the retired key;
  - drafts and quarantined entries will land in the inspection list, not be applied;
  - an operation from a device revoked in the trusted roster, carrying an early creation time and the device's older roster, will land in the inspection list, while one with a same-lineage pre-revocation receipt will be eligible;
  - a pre-enrollment export: `installation-only` shows its warning and can't be opened elsewhere; `owner-supplied-core-key` opens with the key backup, and a mistyped key is caught by its checksum;
  - a restored installation, with and without its keys, will keep identities safe;
  - **reviewed sets across exports and epochs (S-07):** a resolution over 600 reviewed candidates exported with its artifact and imported by `import-journal` into a new epoch applies to exactly those candidates. The same resolution arriving first and its artifact a day later (operation-first), and the reverse (artifact-first), converge on the same state, and a candidate that arrived after the review stays in conflict in both. With the originating installation lost and no holder of the artifact, the resolution waits until its deadline; the owner's signed fallback, received first, finalizes it (listed IDs only); an artifact found afterwards is recorded and changes nothing; with neither, the deadline finalizes it as expired with the conflicts kept. Each export discloses, per range, what it holds as exact bytes, as decisions only, or as unknown;
  - an archive that decrypts with the export key but whose signature doesn't verify against the trusted roster will be listed and never executed.
- **X3 on devices:** the exclusion attribute will be inspected on the directory and its sidecars. On the sandboxed Mac, export will be saved outside the container and the import file shown unchanged. On iPhone, export will be saved to Files.
- **M9 and X7:** loss of the originating installation, then recovery through Core, and after a rollback through the post-downgrade tool using only the documented key backup (§19.2).

#### 8.6.7 Deletion, expiry and privacy across copies (privacy and retention)

**Required:** deletion propagates to canonical records, replicas, search, caches, reminder and delivery work, and evidence-derived outputs, keeping only minimal metadata (R2-SYNC-07).

- **One tombstone,** propagated in the same transaction or by a durable follow-up, removes:
  - content from projections and replicas;
  - search index rows;
  - blob references, so a blob shared with something live stays;
  - derived outputs: briefs and plans are regenerated, and inference results citing the evidence are invalidated with their suggestions withdrawn and a visible reason;
  - future reminders and deliveries, with pending OS requests cancelled and delivered alerts removed where the platform allows;
  - the title copies R1 keeps in reminders and intents;
  - every stored v1 response body that contains any of its content, found through the reply dependency index and invalidated in the same transaction (§8.12.2).

  The tombstone keeps IDs, the caller-scoped request key, the deletion position and the actor; for a capture, also its content-free `CaptureDTO` fields (kind, capture time, processing state, idea and news IDs), so a v1 retry can still be answered (§8.12.2).
- **Expiry by retention** uses the same propagation. Core retention is in §8.17.
- **Where deleted content can remain, and until when.** Lisa's deletion promises name the stored record and the boundary; nothing broader is claimed:

  | Store | Records | Gone by | Bound |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Core and replica databases, logically | Canonical rows, projections, search rows, reply bodies (through the dependency index), reminder and intent titles, evidence excerpts | **Logically removed** in the deletion's transaction, or its durable follow-up: from that commit on, no query, API answer, replica page, export or reply contains it | The commit |
  | Main database files, as raw bytes | The pages that held the deleted rows, and pages the deletion frees | With `PRAGMA secure_delete = ON` (never `FAST`, which leaves traces on freelist pages), the deletion writes zero-filled page images — in WAL mode, to the WAL, not to the main file. The main file keeps the old bytes until a checkpoint copies those pages back | The first **successful** checkpoint after the commit |
  | WAL files, as raw bytes | Frames written before the deletion that hold the content | They stay in the `-wal` file until it is truncated or overwritten: a checkpoint doesn't normally truncate it, and secure deletion doesn't touch frames already written. Lisa runs `wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` within 15 minutes of a deletion and at a clean shutdown, and counts only a **successful** one: busy flag 0 and the WAL truncated to zero bytes. A reader on an older snapshot makes it return busy; Lisa retries, aborts readers past their bound (§8.15.2), and reports the deletion as "raw removal pending since <time>, blocked by a reader" | The first successful TRUNCATE checkpoint after the commit |
  | Replicas | As the live database | When the device applies the tombstone; an offline device holds content until it syncs, and can't resurrect it, because the tombstone dominates | The device's next sync |
  | Backups | The image at its verified snapshot cut, plus the other manifest-listed artifacts under their own retention rules | A pre-deletion image can retain the data until the owner deletes that backup. A post-deletion image reflects committed logical removal even while old bytes await checkpoint in the source. Test raw bytes only for named artifacts and known canaries | Snapshot cut and artifact retention (§8.4.4–5) |
  | Owner exports, rollback archives | — | Lisa can't reach them | Not Lisa's |
  | R1 files | The `prepare` backup, R1's pre-migration ledger, R1's database after a downgrade (R1 doesn't secure-delete) | Lisa can't redact them; the migration and rollback reports name them | Not Lisa's |
  | Logs and diagnostics | IDs, counters, hashes and states only | Never hold content | — |
  | Below the file system | Journaling, copy-on-write or flash remapping; snapshots of the volume | Overwritten or truncated bytes may survive below the file; Lisa claims nothing there | Not Lisa's |

  `docs/SECURITY.md` says this plainly.
- **After a restore:** the restored database may contain items deleted after its cut.
  - Tombstones that a device still holds, or that replayed operations or a newer backup carry, are re-applied at admission. Devices keep tombstones with their versions (§8.4.2), so any device that synced a deletion reports it.
  - **When no surviving evidence identifies a deletion,** Lisa can't know which item it was and doesn't claim to. The restore review states the cut and the uncertainty: "Restored to <time>. Anything deleted after <time> may reappear unless one of your devices still knows about the deletion. Lisa can't tell which items these are." What can reappear: owner items deleted after the cut whose tombstone no device, backup or archive holds, with their notes, reminders and search entries. The owner reviews by sorting or filtering by "last changed before <cut time>" and deletes again; a later device report of the tombstone also removes the item.
- **Diagnostics** carry IDs, counters, hashes and states only — no titles, notes, addresses or content.

**Acceptance (M5 local deletion, M8 notification parts; S-19):**
- a deleted task with notes, a shared blob, a search hit, a brief citation, an inference result and a pending reminder will lose each of these;
- a stale device's old edit will not resurrect it;
- a v1 replay of the creating request will expose nothing;
- **change preference A, delete preference B, then replay A's request before daily maintenance:** A's stored body, which listed B, will have been invalidated in B's deletion transaction, and the replay will return the terminal reply (§8.12.2);
- a restore after which one device still holds the tombstone will re-apply it at admission;
- a restore after which no holder has the tombstone will show the cut-based notice. The item will reappear, and the review will not claim to identify it;
- **an error with identifiable input** (§8.12.2): the canary error replayed, then expired, then the dataset reversed. The canary will be absent from every response, recorded body, log line and audit detail at all times; it will remain, as the owner's input, in the request's stored bytes and the restricted child's body at Core while those exist; after they are deleted it will be logically absent at the commit; absent from the raw main file and WAL after the first successful TRUNCATE checkpoint — not after one a reader blocked; absent from the tested SQLite backup image whose verified cut follows deletion of all records carrying the canary and from the sanitized reversed v4 database; and retained in the seeded pre-deletion image until that backup leaves the retained set; the migration and rollback reports will name the R1 files Lisa can't redact (the rollback backup from `prepare` and R1's own pre-migration ledger);
- **a deleted task's content at each boundary, by raw bytes:** logically gone at the commit (no query, API answer, replica page, export or reply has it); in the raw files, present in the WAL and possibly in the main file until the first successful TRUNCATE checkpoint, and gone from both after it (busy flag 0, WAL zero bytes); **with a blocked reader** — a read transaction held open across the deletion — the checkpoint returns busy, both files may still hold the content, `lisa core status` reports "raw removal pending since <time>, blocked by a reader", and after the reader ends or is aborted at its bound the next checkpoint succeeds and the bytes are clean; **after a kill before the checkpoint,** the content may still be in the WAL after recovery, until the next successful checkpoint cleans both files; compare a seeded pre-deletion backup, a post-commit/pre-checkpoint online backup, and a post-TRUNCATE backup, each with a new destination and bound cut. Check the row and unique raw test marker separately in each image: present in the seeded first image, gone from the last two for this simple secure-deleted fixture. Include a long backup that starts before the delete and ends afterwards; the recorded cut determines the expected result; gone from an offline replica only after it syncs and its own checkpoint succeeds.

### 8.7 Nearby synchronization (R2-NEAR-01–05; finding 4 for Design A; trust lifecycle)

**Required:** encrypted, mutually authenticated sessions between enrolled devices, without Internet and without an access point; bounded discovery; relay that never rewrites, impersonates or invents (R2-NEAR-01–05, R2-TRUST-02).

**Platform facts (§1.5):** `NWListener`, `NWBrowser` and `NWConnection` with `includePeerToPeer`, Bonjour and custom framing exist on macOS 15 and iOS 18. Lisa uses these older APIs; the newer family needs iOS 26. TLS-PSK works only there, only over TLS 1.2, and has no documented suite constant. Mutual TLS has every hook except certificate creation. Multipeer Connectivity is deprecated and unused.

**Session layer, under either design**
- **Hello:** protocol version, device ID, roster (epoch, version), head, nonce.
- **Authentication:** each side signs, with its operation key:
  - `"lisa-nearby-auth-v1"`;
  - both nonces and device IDs;
  - a 32-byte TLS exporter value (label `EXPORTER-lisa-nearby-v1`);
  - the roster version and head.

  Each side verifies the other against its newest roster. A relay sitting between two devices sees different exporter values on its two sessions, so the signatures fail and the session closes before data moves.
- **Exchange:**
  - inventories first;
  - then bounded exchanges in §8.16 frames: signed operations, Core receipts, change pages, snapshot chunks, blobs, rosters and paged artifacts;
  - then custody acknowledgements signed by the receiver (§8.6.1).

  The newer roster and head always go first, so a stale peer is updated before it sends work.
- **Connection behavior:** TCP keepalives; send timeouts; backpressure by awaiting each send; 30-second idle close; deduplication by device ID (the connection opened by the lower ID survives); random Bonjour instance names, with nothing identifying in TXT records except Design A's rotating tags.

**Design A: pair-specific pre-shared keys**
- **Pair key:** K_ij = HKDF-SHA256 over the P-256 agreement between devices i and j, with the dataset ID as salt and `"lisa-nearby-psk-v1"` plus both sorted device IDs as info.
- **Normal discovery:**
  - The listener publishes a tag rotating every 15 minutes: the first 16 bytes of HMAC-SHA256(discovery secret, `"lisa-disc-v1"` ‖ window ‖ device ID).
  - The initiator finds its peer by tag and sends its own tag as the PSK identity.
  - The discovery secret is fetched separately (`GET /v2/trust/nearby-secret`, authenticated, `Cache-Control: no-store`, kept in the Keychain) and never travels in the enrollment response.
  - It rotates on every revocation and every 30 days. Devices keep the current and previous versions for 7 days, and a peer passes a newer version only inside an authenticated session.
- **Rejoining after the overlap.** A device whose newest secret is older than both versions in use can't compute or recognize tags. It rejoins without the secret:
  - It browses for Lisa services and opens a trial connection to each one found (at most 9).
  - As PSK identity it sends `HMAC-SHA256(K_ij, "lisa-rejoin-v1" ‖ window)` truncated to 16 bytes, one per candidate peer j.
  - The listener's PSK selection block computes the same value for each active roster peer (at most 9 HMACs) and selects K_ij on a match.
  - The handshake and the session layer then authenticate both sides. The peer sends its newer roster and the current discovery secret, but only if the rejoiner is active in that roster.

  Observers can't link these identities, which change per window and pair. A revoked device is refused at the session layer.
- **When Core is the only way back:** a device whose roster is older than the maximum nearby trust age (30 days by default) can't start any nearby exchange (R2-TRUST-03). It must reach Core, directly or by re-pairing. The UI says so precisely: "Connect to Lisa Core once to resume nearby sync (trust older than 30 days)."
- **Limits:** TLS 1.2 only; a PSK suite only by raw value; forward secrecy only if an ECDHE-PSK suite is negotiated.

**Design B: roster-bound mutual TLS**
- **Identity:** a P-256 TLS key in the Secure Enclave and an X.509 certificate for it (v3, random serial, one-year validity, `digitalSignature`, server and client authentication), stored beside the key so that Security forms a `SecIdentity`. The key's hash is listed in the device's roster entry.
- **Handshake:** TLS 1.3 with mutual authentication required. The verify block accepts a peer only if its leaf key matches an active roster entry; no CA chain or host name is involved.
- **Discovery:** connect to the discovered services (at most 9) and learn who answered during the encrypted handshake.

**Certificates: proof-only at X1, production at X4**
- **X1, proof-only provisioning.**
  - A fixed-template DER encoder, compiled only into `Apps/Proof`, signs with the Secure Enclave key through CryptoKit. It is labeled proof-only and never linked into a product.
  - The same Foundation-only encoder file is compiled by a Linux harness with a software key, and its output is checked with `openssl x509` and `openssl verify`.
  - X1 evidence covers Network framework mutual TLS with a Secure-Enclave-backed identity and roster pinning. It does not cover production issuance.
- **X4, production (P-08, conditional).** P-08 applies only if N-1 selects Design B after B's proof passes; if Design A is selected, P-08 is recorded as inapplicable. If it applies, the production issuer is swift-certificates, which the owner prefers:
  - **Pin:** 1.21.1 at `84f03fafeca28c19f5fc4b528df3d22ce8896bd7`.
  - **Compatibility:** tools 6.1, which builds with Swift 6.3.2 and 6.4.2; swift-crypto `3.12.3..<6.0.0`, satisfied by the pinned 5.1.1; swift-asn1 `≥ 1.1.0`, satisfied by the pinned 1.7.4.
  - **Expected lockfile impact:** one new pin and no change to existing ones. This is confirmed only by resolution after authorization.
  - **Binary impact:** `_CryptoExtras` adds BoringSSL-backed code to the Apple apps; app size will be measured with and without at X4.
  - **Protected-key integration:** a Secure Enclave `SecKey` (`kSecAttrTokenIDSecureEnclave`, permanent) signs through `Certificate.PrivateKey(SecKey)` without leaving the enclave. The certificate goes into the Keychain, Security pairs it with the key as a `SecIdentity`, and `sec_identity_create` hands it to Network framework.
  - It is linked only through the conditional `LisaDeviceCertificates` module. **No package or lockfile change happens until the owner reviews and authorizes the pin.**
- **Still to verify on devices with the selected library (X4, E-11):**
  - Secure-Enclave-signed creation on the Mac, iPhone and iPad;
  - `SecIdentity` formation in the data-protection Keychain, including in the sandboxed Mac;
  - TLS 1.3 mutual authentication on all three pairs;
  - key extraction in the verify block;
  - renewal at 80% and rotation through key updates;
  - behavior while locked after first unlock;
  - reinstall followed by a key update.

**Working nearby sync is required on every pair and condition.** Mac–iPhone, Mac–iPad and iPhone–iPad must each synchronize nearby in three conditions: on a shared Wi-Fi network; on a shared network with the Internet disconnected; and with no access point at all (peer-to-peer Wi-Fi). These nine cells are release conditions (R2-NEAR-01–05), closed only through the Apple clients gate (§16, §22).

**N-1 selects a design only by proof.** `Apps/Shared/Nearby`'s production transport is written only after N-1's device result is recorded:
1. **Proofs at X1 (selection).** The owner runs the proof-only builds of both designs (`Apps/Proof`, §10.8) on all nine cells. Design A's proof passes only if every cell meets the session criteria (§10.5, N-1) and negotiates a forward-secret ECDHE-PSK suite. Design B's proof passes only if every cell completes TLS 1.3 mutual authentication with Secure-Enclave-backed identities and roster pinning.
2. **Adoption follows a passed proof, never a failed alternative.** If A's proof passes, A is adopted (it adds no dependency). Otherwise, if B's proof passes, B is adopted, and P-08 applies: no package or lockfile change until the owner approves the exact pin (below). A failure of A never adopts B by itself.
3. **If both proofs fail,** nothing is adopted. A redesign is written into this plan as a new candidate with its own proof, returned to the owner, and N-1 runs again; the Apple clients gate and the R2 release gate stay open meanwhile, and the failures are reported as recorded.
4. **X1 selects; X4 verifies production.** An X1 pass shows only that the proof build of the selected design works. The production transport, built into the production apps (with the production certificate issuer if B), is verified separately at X4 on the same nine cells (R2-A03–A07). Only an X4 pass closes the nearby rows of the Apple clients gate; an X4 failure reopens the selection for that design.

The portable session layer, relay and storage proceed meanwhile.

**After a failure or retry**
- **Handshake or authentication failure:** close; back off per endpoint on the monotonic clock; retry after the backoff or a roster change. There is never an unauthenticated fallback.
- **Interrupted transfer:** resumes from per-peer durable progress; duplicates are harmless.
- **Revoked peer:** refused until a roster says otherwise.
- **Local network denied** (−65570 or `localNetworkDenied`): a visible unavailable state, with no retries until permission changes. The first nearby operation runs in the foreground (TN3179).

**Relay rules:**
- A peer forwards other devices' signed operations to Core with its own credential, and returns Core-signed receipts and pages.
- It never rewrites, re-signs, invents a receipt, shares its credential, or forwards URLs, commands or general traffic.
- Direct and relayed paths may run at once; identity rules make duplicates harmless.

**Background:** bounded `BGAppRefreshTask` requests for Core sync on iPhone and iPad. Nearby runs only in the foreground. No audio, location or VoIP mode. The UI distinguishes "available now", "waiting for the app to be open", "background time requested but not granted", and "permission or radio unavailable" (R2-NEAR-04).

**Acceptance (proposed checks):**
- **Linux, simulated link (M6; S-08, S-12 shared):** the harness will check hello and authentication; exporter mismatch; unknown and revoked peers; resume and deduplication; the Design A rejoin with an expired discovery secret; refusal with trust older than 30 days; R2-L04, L15, L17, L36; the R2 §18.2 scenario; the coverage relay of §8.4.2.
- **Apple (owner-run):** N-1 at X1 — both designs' proofs on all nine cells, with the selection recorded only from a passed proof; the production transport on all nine cells at X4 (R2-A03–A07), with the P-08 behaviors if Design B applies. Each cell is recorded per device with a correlated case ID and the timing method of §10.8.

### 8.8 Capabilities and transitions (R2-CAP-01–04)

- **Tracked separately:** authenticated Core reachability and compatibility; each fallback provider; nearby availability; data freshness and coverage; notification permission and schedule coverage; enrollment and trust (including recovery stage and head); storage health. Core reachability comes only from an authenticated exchange, never from a VPN or path signal.
- **The mode matrix of R2-CAP-02 is kept as written.** Features follow the capability they need, as `docs/OFFLINE-BEHAVIOR.md` specifies. If a required client capability can't be provided on a platform (for example, public retrieval failing E-1), the feature is shown as unavailable there, and the Apple and release gates stay open (§22). Changing the matrix is the owner's decision.
- **Transitions:** a connectivity change never interrupts a durable capture, replaces a draft, clears the database, resends a finished or uncertain inference, replays an expired delivery or resets retries. Retries use cancellation, bounded timeouts, backoff with jitter and a manual "Try again". Authentication failure, revocation, recovery and version mismatch stop retrying and never fall back to anything insecure.

### 8.9 Offline intelligence, fallback inference and public retrieval (R2-CAP-03, R2-INT-01–05, R2-WORK-01–04; finding 14; fallback model accounting)

#### 8.9.1 Offline intelligence

- **With no model:** search; sorting by deadline, priority and state; overdue detection; a provisional plan from R1's planner and constraints, with explanations of unscheduled work and conflicts (§8.3.4); a structured local brief; permitted preference changes; managing tasks and owner reminders. Free-form captures stay pending; nothing is invented.
- **Local plan (R2-WORK-01):** records its canonical checkpoint, the provisional operations included, calendar coverage and freshness, open conflicts, the algorithm version and when it was made. It is shown as provisional until Core reconciles. Stale calendar gaps are shown as unverified; locked and accepted blocks never move silently. Actions on it are plan intents (§8.2.3).
- **Local brief (R2-WORK-02):** has its own identity (device, input version) and never replaces the canonical brief. Ages, missing sources and regeneration failures are shown, and the readable and spoken versions carry the same uncertainty.

#### 8.9.2 Fallback inference and its accounting

- **Providers (R2-INT-01, extended by the owner's decision of 2026-11-22):** OpenAI, Anthropic and a self-hosted Ollama, through `LisaOpenAICompatible`, `LisaAnthropic` and `LisaOllama` (§8.19.3), over `LisaHTTP1` (§8.9.3). Each is configured per device: provider, endpoint, model, a Keychain credential reference, explicit data-sharing consent, timeout, input and output bounds, the budget. Nothing is enabled by default, and Core's settings never authorize a client.
- **Workflows (R2-INT-02):**
  - interpret a capture into proposed tasks, ideas or events;
  - summarize selected local evidence;
  - improve the local brief;
  - explain the provisional plan;
  - answer bounded questions from the working set.

  Results name their local basis and missing or stale sources, and pass the same validation as Core's suggestions.
- **Logical intent key:** `SHA-256("lisa-infer-intent-v1" ‖ workflow ‖ sorted input references with revisions ‖ template version ‖ model ID ‖ endpoint ID ‖ SHA-256 of the owner's question, if any)`. While a request with that key exists in any state, repeated taps, restarts and reconnects return it. A new request needs a new intent: changed inputs or template, or an explicit "Ask again".
- **One in flight per device:** a durable single-row slot. Other intents wait in a local queue unreserved and show "waiting".
- **Request states:**

  | State | Budget unit | On restart |
  |---|---|---|
  | `queued` | none | stays queued |
  | `reserved` (one transaction: record the configuration snapshot and consent version, reserve one unit of the planning-zone day and of the rolling 24-hour window; refused at 10) | held | continues to send if consent, configuration and inputs are unchanged; otherwise `cancelled` and released |
  | `sending` (persisted before the first byte) | held | becomes `uncertain`, never resent |
  | `answered_valid` / `answered_invalid` | spent | final |
  | `failed_before_send` | released | final |
  | `uncertain` (timeout or connection loss after sending) | spent, labeled "may have been charged" | final until the owner acts |
  | `cancelled` (consent withdrawn, configuration changed, evidence deleted, owner cancel) before sending | released | final |

- **Repair:** at most one, reserving its own unit. If no unit is left, the invalid result is shown without repair.
- **Consent withdrawn between reservation and send:** the transaction that would persist `sending` re-checks the consent version, so a withdrawn consent cancels and releases. Nothing is sent.
- **Model or template changes:** a request keeps the configuration it reserved with. A change cancels unsent requests (released), and results under an old template stay labeled.
- **Planning zone and clock:**
  - The day key is fixed at reservation, and a zone change can't free units.
  - The last-seen wall time is persisted, and the day key uses the later of it and now, so a backward step can't return to an earlier day.
  - The rolling window counts reservations stamped within the last 24 hours, including stamps that now look like the future. A forward step followed by a reboot can release units early; this residual is documented.
- **Deleted evidence:** checked again at send (cancel and release). After an answer, results citing deleted evidence are invalidated and their suggestions withdrawn with a reason (§8.6.7).
- **After an uncertain, possibly charged attempt:** the owner sees "Outcome unknown — the provider may have processed and charged this request" and can:
  - dismiss it;
  - "Ask again", which is a new request, reserves a new unit, and warns that the request may be charged twice.

  Nothing is resent automatically.
- **"Ask again" has a durable identity.** Each finished or uncertain request R has at most one ask-again successor, whose intent key is `SHA-256("lisa-infer-ask-again-v1" ‖ R's request ID)`. The first tap creates the successor and reserves its unit in one transaction. A double tap, a second tap from another window, or a restart before the result finds the same successor, so it never reserves a second unit. To ask a third time, the owner taps "Ask again" on the successor once it has finished; each deliberate repeat reserves exactly one new unit.
- **No global cap:** per-device limits are not a global spending cap, and the documentation says so (R2-INT-05).
- **On reconnection:** results go to Core as proposals with their evidence. Nothing is regenerated just because Core returned.

**Acceptance (M7; S-18):** the harness will check:
- the five workflows against loopback OpenAI-, Anthropic- and Ollama-shaped servers, and against the controlled local Ollama container (S-22), with each provider's usage fields feeding the budget;
- a process kill in each state, and repeated taps and reconnects landing on the same request;
- consent withdrawal between reservation and send;
- a template change, a zone change at midnight, a backward clock step and a forward step with a reboot (the residual documented);
- evidence deleted before send and after the answer;
- repair counting;
- "Ask again" after an uncertain attempt: a double tap, a process kill between tap and reservation, and a restart all yield one successor and one unit; a deliberate repeat on the finished successor yields one more.

#### 8.9.3 Public retrieval and the destination policy (finding 14)

**Required:** foreground, explicitly requested retrieval of owner-configured public feeds and owner-supplied public URLs, keeping R1's URL, redirect, size, parser and network-safety controls (R2-INT-03); required in the "Internet, Core unavailable" mode (R2-CAP-02). **E-1 is a release requirement (owner decision, 2026-11-22):** release on an Apple platform requires the verified Core-unavailable case there.

**What R1 enforces, and the Apple path keeps (§1.6):**
- every resolved address checked before a socket is opened to it, with refused addresses skipped;
- the connected address checked again before the request is sent;
- no proxy;
- TLS 1.2 or later with verification;
- redirects re-checked hop by hop: none for model calls; at most 3 by default (10 at most) for sources, with credentials dropped across origins;
- a non-idempotent request sent at most once per exchange.

**Destinations**

| Destination | Policy | Client path |
|---|---|---|
| Owner-requested public URL or feed, and every redirect target | public only | `LisaHTTP1` over `Apps/Shared/PublicFetch` |
| Model endpoint named by DNS (OpenAI, Anthropic, a DNS-named proxy) | public only | Same, with system trust |
| **The configured self-hosted model endpoint** (`localhost`, or a loopback or private IP literal: the owner's Ollama or its proxy) | local networks allowed, **for that endpoint's model route only**: its exact scheme, host, port and path prefix, the authenticated `POST` of a model call, no redirects. A retrieval URL, a redirect target or any other port or path on the same host stays public only | Same; the owner may pin its SPKI on the device |
| Model endpoint over plain `http` | loopback only (meaningful on the Mac) | Same |
| Core | The paired endpoint and trust (§8.1.2) | `LisaSyncHTTP` |

**Every URL is checked as if it were the first.** The policy runs on the URL the owner selects and again, from check 1, on every redirect target, which is treated as a new initial URL: its scheme, host, literal form and port are checked before anything is resolved. Model calls follow no redirects at all, as in R1.

**Checks, in order**
1. **Before opening TCP:**
   - R1's URL policy: scheme, no credentials, host syntax, port.
   - The endpoint's scope, as `ConfiguredHostScope` decides.
   - **One interface for the whole request.** The request takes the interface its current `NWPath` would use (the first available interface) and records that interface's index. Resolution, discovery and the connection all use it, so their evidence describes one path.
   - **Path-scoped resolution:** `DNSServiceGetAddrInfo` on that interface index, for A records, off the main thread; every answer arrives with its TTL (§1.5).
   - **IP literals bypass DNS, so they get the same verification rule, not a shortcut.** An IPv4 literal is classified like an A answer. An IPv4-mapped IPv6 literal (`::ffff:a.b.c.d`) is classified by its embedded IPv4 address and dialed as that IPv4 address. An IPv6 literal inside `64:ff9b::/96`, `64:ff9b:1::/48` or a prefix discovered on this interface is a translated destination: its embedded IPv4 address is classified, and it is dialed only if that address is public and the path is IPv6-only with that prefix discovered. **Any other IPv6 literal is never dialed by a device:** it may translate to a private IPv4 address under a prefix the device can't learn, so the request is deferred to Core, the same as an AAAA-only name. A private literal is allowed only as the configured self-hosted model endpoint above.
   - **Path-scoped NAT64 evidence,** on a request whose path is IPv6-only: the RFC 7050 query for `ipv4only.arpa` AAAA through `DNSServiceQueryRecord` on the same interface. Synthesized answers give the prefixes (the well-known addresses found at octet boundaries, RFC §3).
   - **Conservative revalidation:** evidence is never carried from one request to the next, and within one request, redirects included, it is used no longer than the smallest TTL received. Nothing depends on an API reporting a TTL for a cache, because there is no cache.
   - Each address is classified with R1's `NetworkAddress`, extended for `64:ff9b:1::/48` and the discovered prefixes. Refused addresses are skipped; if none remains, the request fails with R1's "resolves only to addresses Lisa does not connect to".
   - Which addresses may be used at all is decided by the path table below.
2. **Before starting TLS:** `NWConnection` to one permitted IP endpoint at a time, in the order the path table allows (staggered 250 ms), never by name, with `preferNoProxies` and `requiredInterface` set to the request's interface. TCP and TLS therefore reach only checked addresses, over the path the evidence describes. This deliberately departs from TN3151's advice to connect by name: connecting by name lets the system resolve again and connect before any check runs, which R1's gate never allows.
3. **TLS:** the system stack, minimum TLS 1.2. The URL's host is the TLS server name (`sec_protocol_options_set_tls_server_name`) for SNI and certificate validation. Trust is the system default, or the owner's pin for a permitted local model endpoint.
4. **Before sending HTTP:** `currentPath.remoteEndpoint` must equal the chosen endpoint, and `currentPath` must use the request's interface. Anything else — a proxy, a relay, a rewrite or another route — makes the destination unverifiable.
5. **HTTP:** `LisaHTTP1`, HTTP/1.1, one request per connection, `Accept-Encoding: identity`, no cookies, caching, upgrades or pipelining, Content-Length or chunked bodies, R1's header, body and time limits.
   - GET for retrieval; POST for model calls, never re-sent on a new connection.
   - Each redirect re-runs checks 1–4 on a new connection, starting with the literal rules above; a redirect to an IPv6 literal is deferred to Core like any other unverifiable destination, and a redirect never inherits the self-hosted model endpoint's allowance.

**Which address family the device can verify.** A public-looking IPv6 address can be a translation of a private IPv4 address under a prefix the device can't learn: a network-specific prefix, or a translator that serves only some names. A negative `ipv4only.arpa` answer describes only the response for that one name (RFC 7050 §5.1). So **the device never connects to an AAAA answer**. The evidence it accepts is an A record whose public IPv4 address R1's classification allows; that address is the one Lisa reaches, natively or through a discovered translator. When a name has no usable A record, the client defers to Core.

**Network paths** (as seen on the request's interface):

| Path | Rule |
|---|---|
| IPv4 only (including a CLAT that presents IPv4) | A records, classified directly; connect to the chosen public IPv4 address |
| Dual stack | The same: A records only. AAAA answers are not requested and never dialed, with or without synthesis |
| IPv6 only, a NAT64 prefix discovered on this interface | For each public A record, connect to the address synthesized with the first discovered prefix (RFC 7050 §3), or with `64:ff9b::/96` if that is the prefix. The translator forwards to the verified IPv4 address |
| IPv6 only, discovery finds no synthesis, fails or times out | Fail closed; defer to Core |
| A name with no usable A record, on any path | Defer to Core: "Lisa can't verify this destination on this device; Lisa Core will fetch it when it's reachable." |
| An IPv6 literal, on any path | Dialed only as the translated or IPv4-mapped forms above, after classifying the embedded IPv4 address; otherwise deferred to Core with the same message |
| A VPN on the path (split or full tunnel) | The request stays on its one interface and uses that interface's resolver answers and discovery. If the connection's `currentPath` reports a different interface (the destination routes into or out of the tunnel), the request fails closed: "Couldn't verify where this link leads". A VPN-supplied proxy makes the endpoint differ, so the request also fails closed |
| Path changes during a request (interface switch, VPN up or down, new prefix) | The connection is cancelled. A GET may restart once from check 1 on the new path, with fresh evidence; a POST is never resent and ends `uncertain` if bytes were sent |

**Fail closed.** No request byte is written when resolution fails, NAT64 discovery fails where it is needed, the connected endpoint differs from the chosen one, or the path type isn't covered.
- A retrieval then shows "Couldn't verify where this link leads; not fetched" and is left for Core.
- A model call ends `unverifiable_destination` with its unit released.
- Nothing is ever retried through URLSession.
- Links in content are never followed. Results carry client provenance and never advance Core cursors.

**Feasibility first (E-1, at X1).** `Apps/Proof` runs a fetch probe with no Core configured, on the Mac, the iPhone and the iPad:
- an owner-authorized public URL fetched over `https`;
- a name resolving only to a private address, refused before any TCP connection (a listener on the owner's network records whether one arrived);
- a mixed name dialed only at its public address;
- a configured system proxy not used;
- an IPv6-only NAT64 network (macOS Internet Sharing) handled per the table;
- a dual-stack network: A records only, and an AAAA-only name deferred;
- **an independently translated destination:** on a network whose DNS synthesizes a public-looking AAAA for a test name only (the owner's controlled resolver), while `ipv4only.arpa` gets no synthesis: the name is never dialed by its AAAA;
- the owner's split-tunnel VPN connected while fetching a public URL: the request stays on one interface or fails closed;
- the platform behavior the design relies on, each recorded: `DNSServiceGetAddrInfo` and `DNSServiceQueryRecord` scoped to an interface index, with TTLs; `NWConnection` honoring `requiredInterface`; `currentPath` reporting the interface and remote endpoint before the first write; and whether the SDK exposes `nw_nat64_copy_prefixes_async` (§1.5), used for comparison only.

**If a platform fails E-1** (it can't connect to an IP endpoint while validating TLS for the URL's host, can't scope resolution and connection to one interface, or can't report the connected endpoint before the first write), the client defers retrieval and DNS-named providers to Core on that platform as a **runtime fallback only**. That platform's Apple gate and the R2 release gate stay open: R2 isn't released on that platform until a compliant redesign within the same destination policy is written into this plan and its proof passes on that platform. Only a redesign that needs a new permission, a new dependency or a change of requirement comes back to the owner (§22).

**Core applies the same principle.** Core runs R1's gate on Linux (every resolved address checked before the socket, the connected address re-checked). It dials an AAAA answer or an IPv6 literal only on a host whose configuration states that its IPv6 path has no translator (`network.ipv6Translation: none`, which `lisa core doctor --network` checks with the RFC 7050 query and reports); otherwise it treats IPv6 destinations as the devices do: translated forms by their embedded IPv4 address, anything else refused.

**Acceptance (S-14):**
- **M7, Linux:** `LisaHTTP1`'s parser, limits and redirects against loopback servers. The policy will be driven through the test resolver and byte stream across every table row: public, private, link-local, IPv4-mapped, well-known and network-specific prefixes, mixed answers, **IPv4 and IPv6 literals as the initial URL and as redirect targets (a public IPv4 literal fetched; a private IPv4 literal refused; `::ffff:10.0.0.5` refused; `64:ff9b::a00:5` refused as private; `64:ff9b::5db8:d822` dialed only on an IPv6-only path with that prefix; a public-looking global IPv6 literal deferred to Core), a redirect from the self-hosted model endpoint refused, a retrieval to the model endpoint's host refused, a model call on another port or path of that host refused,** **an AAAA answer never dialed on any path, including a public-looking AAAA under a translator that discovery doesn't report while `ipv4only.arpa` is negative; an AAAA-only name deferred to Core; a dual-stack path using A records only**, a split-VPN interface mismatch refused, evidence not reused by the next request and not used beyond the smallest TTL within a redirect chain, a connected endpoint other than the chosen one, a path change during GET and during POST, a redirect to a private address, and R1's redirect limit.
- **X5, on the Mac, the iPhone and the iPad with Core unavailable,** with the production apps against controlled endpoints the owner runs: a permitted public GET through the production retrieval path; a disallowed destination refused before connection; a redirect to a private address and a redirect to a global IPv6 literal, each stopped at the redirect; an IPv6-literal URL deferred with its message; an AAAA-only name deferred; a path change (Wi-Fi to cellular, or a VPN toggle); the owner's split-tunnel VPN connected; and an authenticated model POST to the self-hosted endpoint on a private IP literal with a pinned certificate, for each provider shape (OpenAI, Anthropic, Ollama), with a redirect from it refused. The listener on the owner's network records that no refused destination received a connection. These are the verified Core-unavailable cases that release requires on each platform; a failure on one platform keeps that platform's gate open until the redesign's proof passes there.

### 8.10 Notifications and external delivery (R2-NOT-01–06; findings 11 and 12; notification coverage)

**Required:** stable occurrence identity with monotonically advancing assignment generations, where a content revision alone creates no new occurrence; per-device ownership records with R2-NOT-03's states; the five-step handoff; no automatic reclaim of an uncertain assignment; visible coverage gaps; a 48-hour horizon and at most 32 pending Lisa requests per device; Core-only email and calls with R1's outbox rules; no blind resend after restart or restore (R2-NOT-01–06, R2-L24–L29).

**Core decides, under R1's unchanged policy:** eligibility, escalation, quiet hours, owner-only destinations, generic content, validity windows and stale suppression. Every enabled paired device is targeted, now including the Mac, which registers for APNs.

**What exists when.** `LisaFiringIdentity` (§8.10.1) and the Core side of ownership in LisaDelivery (§8.10.2–§8.10.3: assignment records, reservation serialization, report intake, holds and their v2 routes) are built at **M3**, so the M3 and M4 restore checks (S-11, N-A) run against them with a scripted device client. The device side (`LisaNotificationOwnership`: command storage, OS-request intents, the 32-request cap and reconciliation) arrives at **M8**, which repeats those scenarios with the real implementation.

#### 8.10.1 Occurrences, firings and identifiers

- **Occurrence ID:** the first 16 bytes, in base32, of `SHA-256("lisa-occurrence-v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ dataset ‖ 0x00 ‖ kind ‖ 0x00 ‖ subject ‖ 0x00 ‖ slot)`. It is fixed when created and never changes.
- **Firing number `f`.** It distinguishes an intended new alert from a change to an existing one:
  - **New firing:** an explicit snooze names the firing it snoozes, f, and creates f+1 at the snooze instant, matching R1, where a snooze fires again under a new key.
  - **Same firing, new generation:** a reschedule (deadline or block moved), a content-only edit (a title change, which R1 does not treat as material), or a reopen.
  - A firing that has already fired is never re-created by a reschedule, content edit or reopen. A reopen schedules only future slots that haven't fired, as R1 creates only future offsets.
- **Scope:** ownership, reservations, submissions, observations and late reports all apply to `(occurrence, firing, recipient)`.
- **Mapping from R1's three key levels (§1.6):** the slot leaves out the mutable parts.

  | R1 key | R1 level | R2 identity |
  |---|---|---|
  | `deadline:task:<id>:<seq>:<offset>` | Reminder | occurrence (deadline, task, offset) |
  | `deadline:task:<id>:<seq>:soon` | Reminder | occurrence (deadline, task, `soon`) |
  | `dateonly:task:<id>:<seq>` | Reminder | occurrence (dateonly, task) |
  | `overdue:task:<id>:<date>` | Reminder | occurrence (overdue, task, date) |
  | `block:<plan>:<block>:<startMs>` | Reminder | occurrence (block, plan and block) |
  | `proposal:<id>:<date>` | Reminder | occurrence (proposal follow-up, proposal, date) |
  | `reminder:<reminderID>:<firingMs>` | Notification intent, one per firing | That reminder's occurrence; firing numbers in ascending `firingMs` order |
  | `brief:<date>:<zone>` | Notification intent | occurrence (brief, date and zone), firing 1 |
  | `commitmentChange:<subject>:<seq>` | Notification intent | occurrence (commitment change, subject, change sequence), firing 1 |
  | `notify:<key>:device:<id>`, `email:<key>:<address>`, `brief-email:…`, `call:task:<id>:<key>` | Delivery | Kept unchanged on migrated deliveries and linked to (occurrence, firing, recipient). New deliveries are keyed by occurrence ID, firing and recipient |
  | (new) owner reminder | — | occurrence (owner reminder, reminder) |

  When R1 logic replaces a reminder key for the same slot (change-sequence bump, moved block), the occurrence keeps its ID and gets a new generation. A change that removes the slot cancels the occurrence.
- **OS request identifier:** `lisa.r.` followed by the first 26 base32 characters of `SHA-256("lisa.local-notification.v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ dataset ‖ 0x00 ‖ occurrenceID ‖ 0x00 ‖ deviceID)`.
  - It is one per (occurrence, device), so scheduling firing f+1 or a new generation replaces the previous request.
  - `userInfo` carries the occurrence, firing and generation. R1's APNs collapse IDs are unchanged.
  - Neither identifier is ever used for idempotency (R2-NOT-05).

#### 8.10.2 Commands and observations

- **Commands** per (occurrence, device): `schedule(f, g, trigger, content)` and `cancel(f, g)`.
  - `g` increases across all firings and is epoch-qualified: `(confirmationSequence, counter)`, compared in that order. `confirmationSequence` is the sequence of the owner confirmation under which Core issued the command (§8.5.2): it is Core-signed, strictly increasing across restores, sibling restores and supplementary confirmations, and every device can verify it through its accepted chain of confirmations. `counter` increases per occurrence within one confirmation. A restored Core's commands therefore outrank every older one, and a command issued after a supplementary confirmation outranks one issued before it.
  - A device applies a command only after verifying its signature and its confirmation; a command under a confirmation the device hasn't verified waits until it has.
  - A command at or below the stored generation is stale and ignored.
- **Observations** are facts about one firing: `scheduled(f, g)`, `fired(f, g)`, `absent(f, g)`, `failed(f, g)`, `expired(f)`. They are never discarded as stale.
  - `fired(f)` arriving after `cancel(f, g+1)` records that firing f was displayed, so its slot is not reclaimed.
  - `fired(f)` has no effect on f+1.
- **Ownership states** per (occurrence, firing, device): R2-NOT-03's list — Core-owned, reserved, local confirmed, scheduling failed, suppressed or cancelled, expired, outcome unknown — plus `fired`.
  - "Scheduling failed" (confirmed not scheduled) and a confirmed cancellation return the slot to Core.
  - `fired`, `expired` and `outcome unknown` never do.

#### 8.10.3 Reservation against push attempts

Reservation runs in one transaction that reads the delivery for the same (occurrence, firing, recipient). It uses R1's delivery states (§1.6):

| Delivery state | Reservation for firing f |
|---|---|
| none, or `pending` | Suppress the delivery and reserve |
| `inProgress` (preparing or submitting) | Not now; retried after the attempt reaches a final state |
| `acceptedByProvider`, `confirmedDelivered`, `outcomeUnknown`, `capturedDryRun` | No reservation for f, which is delivered or uncertain; firing f+1 can still be reserved |
| `failed`, `cancelled`, `suppressed` | Reserve only if the firing is still valid |

The outbox never claims a delivery whose (occurrence, firing, recipient) is reserved or device-owned. Email and calls stay Core-only and follow R1's outbox (R2-NOT-06).

#### 8.10.4 Handoff, replacement, cancellation and reconciliation

- **Handoff:**
  1. In one transaction, Core reserves (occurrence, f, device) and suppresses its own push.
  2. The device fetches the signed assignment `schedule(f, g)`.
  3. In one replica transaction, the device stores it as `accepted`.
  4. The device commits an `add` intent (below), adds the OS request under the stable identifier with `userInfo` {occurrence, f, g}, reads the pending list back, and marks the assignment `scheduled` (**verified pending**), clearing the intent.
  5. It reports `scheduled(f, g)`, which Core records, never as proof of display.
- **OS intents are recorded before the OS is told.** Before adding, replacing or removing a request, the device commits an intent (`add`, `replace` or `remove`, naming the target firing f and generation g, the generations involved, and every trigger time the OS might hold for that firing). The intent is cleared when the result is verified. The OS can therefore have acted even though Lisa never recorded success: an alert may have fired and been cleared by the owner before Lisa restarts.
- **One OS identifier spans several firings,** because it is one per (occurrence, device) (§8.10.1). So Lisa never acts on an identifier alone:
  - **Before removing or replacing a pending request,** the device reads that request's `userInfo` and acts only if it is exactly the intent's target — the same occurrence, firing f and generation g — and no newer durable command exists for that occurrence. Otherwise the intent is **superseded**: it is completed without touching the OS request. An old `cancel` of firing 1, recovered after a crash, therefore never removes firing 2's pending request.
  - **Delivered alerts are bound to their own firing:** each entry of `getDeliveredNotifications`, and each `willPresent` or action callback, records `fired` for the (firing, generation) in its own `userInfo`, never for the occurrence's current command.
- **What survives while Lisa is stopped, and what it can prove.** The OS, not Lisa, runs triggers: while Lisa is stopped, a request can fire, be shown and be cleared, and the wall clock can be moved in either direction any number of times. Lisa therefore never decides from clocks alone that a trigger hasn't been reached. The evidence it uses, and what each piece establishes:

  | Evidence | Survives an app restart | Survives a reboot | Establishes |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | The OS pending list (`getPendingNotificationRequests`) | Yes | Yes | A listed request hasn't fired; its `userInfo` names the firing and generation |
  | The OS delivered list (`getDeliveredNotifications`) | Yes | Yes | A listed firing fired. Its absence proves nothing: the owner can clear it |
  | Lisa's store: each firing's state, a `verified pending` mark, and every open intent | Yes | Yes | What Lisa last handed to the OS and verified, and every OS call Lisa may have made without verifying |
  | **The continuity marker:** one extra pending request, `lisa.c.<installation ID>`, with a trigger one year ahead (refreshed at every reconciliation) and the neutral text "Open Lisa to update your reminders", counted within the 32-request limit (§8.10.5) | Yes | Yes | **Present:** the OS pending store hasn't been reset since Lisa last verified it (no reinstall, device restore or system reset of Lisa's requests). **Absent:** it may have been |
  | The wall-clock high-water mark (the latest wall time Lisa observed, saved with every intent and reconciliation) | Yes | Yes | The latest time Lisa itself saw. Nothing about the time reached while it was stopped |
  | The monotonic clock (`mach_continuous_time`) with the boot's start time, saved with the mark | Within one boot | No | A lower bound on real elapsed time, and a net wall-clock step (wall time and monotonic time moving differently). It can't exclude a forward step later undone, so it never proves that a trigger wasn't reached |

- **Reconciliation, in this order of precedence,** for each firing that is `scheduled`, has an open intent, or has an `accepted` assignment:
  1. **Seen as delivered:** in `getDeliveredNotifications` with this firing in its `userInfo`, or reported by `willPresent` or an action handler → `fired(f)`, whatever the intent was.
  2. **Pending:** a request under the stable identifier whose `userInfo` names this firing → `scheduled` with that generation; replaced if older than the stored command; removed if the intent was `remove` and the request is the intent's exact target, then checked again. A pending request for another firing of the same occurrence is left alone.
  3. **Verified pending earlier, now absent from both lists, with no open intent:**
     - **continuity marker present** → the OS removed the request without Lisa asking, and its store wasn't reset: Lisa records **`fired`** ("shown while Lisa was closed, or silenced by Focus or settings") and never adds it again. This is Lisa's classification, not a receipt: the surviving marker establishes nothing about this reminder's delivery or display — the alert may have been suppressed, or shown and cleared unseen — and Lisa doesn't know whether the owner saw it (§17.1);
     - **marker absent** → **`outcome unknown`**: the request may have fired and been cleared, or the store may have been reset. Never added again automatically.

     This holds whatever the clocks say, including when the trigger is still later than both the current time and the high-water mark.
  4. **An open `add` or `replace` intent whose request is absent from both lists** → **`outcome unknown`**, never carried out again automatically: Lisa can't tell an OS call that never happened from one that happened and fired before a clock step.
  5. **An open `remove` intent whose request is absent:** `fired` if the delivered list shows it; otherwise `outcome unknown` — a removal can't be told apart from a firing that was then cleared. A cancellation is never reported as `cancelled` without verified absence while Lisa runs.
  6. **An `accepted` assignment with no intent:** the OS was never told, so it is scheduled.

  The stable identifier rules out two pending requests for one occurrence.
- **What the owner sees, and the only way to show it again.** An `outcome unknown` firing still inside its validity window appears in the Reminders view as "10:00 Call the plumber — may have been shown while Lisa was closed", with **Show again**. That action is the owner's explicit authorization of a possible duplicate: it creates firing f+1 (as a snooze does) under the same identifier, scheduled once. Nothing else reschedules it. Core keeps the slot device-owned and shows a coverage gap.
- **Genuine snoozes stay distinct.** The owner's snooze action on firing f is itself proof that f was displayed, so f becomes `fired` even if it was `outcome unknown`. The snooze creates firing f+1 with its own trigger under the same identifier. f's uncertainty never blocks f+1, and f+1 follows the same rules.
- **Replacement** (new generation of the same firing): intent, add under the same identifier, verify, mark, report. **Known finding KF-2** (open, deferred by the owner, §17.1): a request Lisa read as pending can fire between that read and the add that replaces it under the same identifier; the OS then schedules the replacement as a new request, and the owner can be alerted again. The exact-target check and the intents above don't close this window, and no case below claims they do. The stopped-app clock case (rules 3–5) and the owner's deliberate snooze and **Show again** are separate cases with their own rules.
- **Cancellation:** intent; remove the pending request if it is the intent's exact target; verify it is absent while Lisa runs; check the delivered list; report `cancelled(f, g)`, `fired(f, g)` if it was delivered, or `outcome unknown` per the precedence above.
- **Expiry:** nothing is scheduled past `validUntil`, computed as R1's `DeliveryTiming` does. A reminder is valid until the earlier of 240 minutes after due and its deadline; a block until its end; overdue, proposal and brief notices until local midnight. A firing past `validUntil` without evidence is `expired(f)` and is never replayed. Expiry uses the current wall clock; a clock moved back can only delay an expiry, never cause a duplicate, because rules 3–5 never re-add.
- **Alerts already shown:**
  - Anything in `getDeliveredNotifications`, or seen by `willPresent` or an action handler, is `fired` for the firing its `userInfo` names.
  - Stale delivered alerts for resolved subjects are removed where the platform allows (R2-NOT-05), and the `fired` record is kept.
- **Lock state:** scheduled notifications fire while the device is locked. Acknowledge and snooze work from the lock screen after first unlock; complete requires unlocking, as in R1 (`.authenticationRequired`). Reports wait until the app runs. Before first unlock, nothing is saved (§8.6.2).
- **A disconnected device** keeps its reservations, and its reports queue. Core never reclaims the slot; expiry makes it `expired` or `outcome unknown`.
- **A device ahead of a restored Core:**
  - At recovery stage 1 the device reports its assignment inventory with epoch-qualified generations and observations (§8.5.3).
  - The restored Core keeps device-owned every slot the device reports as scheduled or fired, and issues commands under its new confirmation.
  - Slots nobody reports stay `outcome unknown` until a report arrives or they expire.
  - Core never pushes a slot that any device reports as scheduled.
- **Locally created reminders (R2-NOT-04):** owned by the creating device until Core accepts them. Peers show them as pending and schedule nothing for that slot. Once accepted, Core adds future slots for the other devices, keeps the creator's, and replays no missed past reminder. No device takes over a Core-owned slot because of an outage.
- **Reconciliation** runs on launch, foregrounding, sync and notification actions. It repeats unfinished steps idempotently, cancels obsolete requests, applies acknowledgements, completions, cancellations and deletions, keeps uncertain handoffs, refreshes the continuity marker, and reports permission changes (R2-NOT-05).
- **Controlled tests and owner observations are kept apart.** The precedence is a pure function in `LisaNotificationOwnership`, tested on Linux (M3 logic, M8 with the simulated notification center) with injected wall and monotonic clocks, a boot identifier, a marker that is present or missing, and every crash point between intent, OS call and verification. Those are controlled results (`fixture-pass`). On devices (X6), the owner can do and observe only what the platform allows: force-quit Lisa or quit it on the Mac, let a test reminder fire and clear it, turn off automatic time and set the clock back, reboot, reopen Lisa, then watch for a second alert at the trigger time and read Lisa's display; the owner-verification build's diagnostics panel lists Lisa's pending identifiers and the marker so the owner can record them. A reset of the OS store without a reinstall, and crash points inside one OS call, are covered only by the controlled tests.
- **The Mac:** its bundle ID joins `notifications.apns.topics`. It gains the `com.apple.developer.aps-environment` entitlement and needs an App ID with Push Notifications and a matching provisioning profile from the owner's developer account. Only the owner uses that account (§22); the owner harness's Apple preflight checks it (§10.8).

#### 8.10.5 The local horizon, the 32-request cap and coverage holes

- **Selection:** each device schedules firings due within 48 hours, at most 31 firings plus the continuity marker, so at most 32 Lisa requests (R2-NOT-02), in this priority: confirmed time-sensitive, then confirmed, then the rest; within each class, by time.
- **Coverage holes:** with more than 31 eligible firings, an omitted firing can be earlier than a scheduled one. For example, with 40 reminders tomorrow, a 08:00 routine reminder may be left out while a 21:00 time-sensitive one is scheduled.
  - Omitted firings stay Core-owned and are pushed through APNs when Core and the device are reachable.
  - On an offline device they don't fire. That is the hole.
- **What the owner sees:**
  - "Notifications scheduled through <time>", where the time is the earlier of the horizon's end and the earliest omitted eligible firing;
  - "n reminders not scheduled on this device (earliest 08:00)".
- **Refill** happens when the app runs (launch, foreground, a granted background refresh) and is not guaranteed while the app is suspended (R2-NEAR-04).

#### 8.10.6 External delivery after a restore (finding 11)

**Required:** a restored Core starts with outbound side effects suspended; restart or restore never blindly resends attempted communications (R2-CORE-06, R2-NOT-06, R2-D12).

**What counts as proof.** Only the sending writer can show that it did *not* submit something, and only over a journal known to be its **last**. A journal copy made before its last submissions proves nothing about the missing tail; neither does a `clean-shutdown` record, which a later run on the same volume can follow, nor a fence marker the replacement appends. Lisa therefore releases a held delivery automatically only on positive evidence from the old sender, bound to its final journal entry by a writer fence, or from the device that may hold the slot. Otherwise it holds the delivery until evidence arrives, the delivery expires, or the owner decides.

**Why holds follow the whole window in which a submission could happen (R1 behavior, kept by R2).** R1 fires a reminder whenever its scheduler first finds it due, however late: `ReminderService.dispatchDue` fires every `scheduled` or `snoozed` reminder whose `due_at` has passed (`Sources/LisaAgenda/ReminderService.swift`, `Reminder.isDue` in `Sources/LisaDomain/Reminder.swift`), and after downtime `ReminderService.recover` keeps every missed reminder that `RecoveryPolicy.decide` finds still relevant, with no lookback limit (`Sources/LisaPolicy/RecoveryPolicy.swift`). The deliveries it creates stay valid until `DeliveryTiming.validUntil` (`Sources/LisaPolicy/DeliveryTiming.swift`): a reminder until its due time plus `defaultValidityMinutes` (240 by default, `Sources/LisaDomain/NotificationSettings.swift`), capped by the task's deadline; an overdue, event-proposal or brief notification until the end of that local day; a plan-block reminder until the block ends; an email copy until the task's deadline, or without limit when there is none; a call no earlier than five minutes after the firing (`ReminderService.fire`). So a reminder due at 08:00 and missed until 10:01 is still submitted at 10:01. A hold that looked back only an hour before the cut would miss it.

**Mechanism**
- **The submission journal** (recovery ledger, one per Core volume, hash-chained). Record kinds:
  - `run-start {volumeID, runGeneration, epoch, previousRunLastSeq, previousRunClosed}`, signed with the Core key;
  - `submit-start {seq, claimID, slot (occurrence, firing, recipient), deliveryID, channel, attempt, releaseID?, epoch, run, wallTime, monotonicTime}`;
  - `submit-outcome {seq, startSeq, claimID, result, providerReference?}`;
  - `heartbeat {wallTime, monotonicTime}`, every minute;
  - `terminal {run, lastSeq, chainHash, kind: clean-shutdown | fenced-local | fenced-on-host}`, signed with the Core key. Only a process holding that volume's lock writes it.
- **The order of one attempt** across SQLite, the journal and the provider:
  1. SQLite transaction: claim the delivery (`preparing` → `submitting`, attempt n, lease, and a random 128-bit **`claimID`**), after checking in the same transaction that the slot isn't held. Commit.
  2. Journal: `submit-start` carrying the same `claimID`, then `fsync`.
  3. Provider I/O.
  4. Journal: `submit-outcome`, then `fsync`.
  5. SQLite transaction: the outcome. Commit.
- **One message identity per slot.** Every email Lisa sends for a slot carries `Message-ID: <lisa.<26 base32 characters of SHA-256("lisa-message-id-v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ dataset ‖ 0x00 ‖ occurrenceID ‖ 0x00 ‖ firing ‖ 0x00 ‖ recipient)>@<sender domain>>`, so two Cores sending the same slot produce the same identifier, which a provider-side lookup can find (below). Every email also carries `Lisa-Writer: <volume ID>.<run generation>` — two random identifiers naming the sending volume and run, and no content — as candidate-matching hints; attribution and containment need independent evidence (§8.5.1). R1's random per-delivery `Message-ID` (`Sources/LisaDelivery/DeliveryProcessing.swift`) stays on migrated R1 deliveries only.
- **Restart on the same volume:**
  - `submitting` with a `submit-start` of its `claimID` and no outcome → `outcomeUnknown`, never resent;
  - `submitting` with an outcome in the journal → that outcome is recorded;
  - `submitting` with no `submit-start` of its `claimID` → the provider was never called, because step 3 follows a durable step 2, so the attempt returns to `pending` without counting;
  - the new run's `run-start` names the previous run's last sequence and whether it closed with a `terminal` record.
- **Which journal entry is the last one.** A `clean-shutdown` record closes one run; the volume can start again, so it says nothing about later runs. Only a **fence record that binds the volume's final run, journal sequence and chain hash** shows that no later entry can exist: it is written under the volume's lock after the schema marker that stops every restart, by `restore` (`fenced-local`) or by `fence --local` on the old host (`fenced-on-host`) (§8.5.1). `owner-attested-stopped` binds no sequence, so it never supports a completeness proof.
- **Off-host copies** (`lisa core backup export`, `trust export`) copy the journal as it stands. Every backup records `s_cut`, the journal sequence at its cut, its wall-clock cut time `t_cut`, and the `claimID` of every delivery in `submitting` at the cut (§8.4.4).
- **Completeness proof for a previous writer W** after a cut:
  1. W's writer fence is `fenced-local` or `fenced-on-host`, and its record binds W's final run, sequence and chain hash;
  2. Lisa holds W's journal from the `run-start` of the run that was live at the cut (the run containing `s_cut`) — or from the volume's first `run-start`, for a volume created after the cut, such as an earlier restore's — through that final sequence, with an unbroken chain ending in the bound hash;
  3. every run boundary in that range is closed, by a `terminal` record or by a following `run-start` naming the last sequence.

  The replacement's own fence records never close W's journal.
- **Attempts open at the cut.** A delivery in `submitting` at the cut was claimed but not finished. Its `submit-start` may lie before `s_cut` or after it, and its provider may have accepted after the cut. The proof matches each such claim by its `claimID` within the proven range:
  - no `submit-start` with that `claimID` anywhere in the range → the provider was never called by that claim;
  - a `submit-start` and its outcome → that outcome (`acceptedByProvider` is final and never resent);
  - a `submit-start` and no outcome anywhere in the range → **`outcomeUnknown`**, held for the owner. An otherwise complete suffix never clears it, and neither does a later run's silence.

**When a previous writer could have submitted.** For each inventoried previous writer W (§8.5.1) and channel c, Lisa records a **possible-submission interval** `A(W, c) = (t_cut − σ, t_end(W, c) + σ]`:
- `t_end(W, c)` is the time W's writer-fence record establishes: the bound final journal entry for `fenced-local` and `fenced-on-host`, the stop time the owner attests for `owner-attested-stopped`, or the time of the attestation itself when the owner can't state one. No credential change shortens it (below). Before every writer is fenced, delivery is closed anyway (§8.5.4); a writer discovered later gets its interval when it is fenced.
- `σ`, the clock allowance, is one hour by default and widens to the largest wall-clock step that W's surviving heartbeats show (wall time moving differently from monotonic time), because W's scheduler judged due times by W's own clock.

Each slot `s` has a **deliverable window** `D(s) = [earliest(s), latest(s))` under R1's rules above: `earliest` is the slot's due or snooze time (plus five minutes for a call); `latest` is its `validUntil`, or unbounded when R1 sets none. The intervals and windows are stored with the holds, so they survive restarts.

**What proves that a previous writer can no longer submit.** Only its writer fence: the volume marker that stops every start, or the owner's attested stop with every restart prevented (§8.5.1). It ends every channel at the time its evidence record states. A credential change is accepted as ending a channel earlier only where the provider documents both that the change invalidates **every** credential the previous writer may hold — including refresh tokens newer than the backup's and access tokens it already holds — and a bound on how long that takes. No provider's documentation read for this plan meets both:

| Channel | What a previous writer may still hold | What the provider documents (§1.5) | Credential cutoff accepted |
|---|---|---|---|
| Outlook.com mail (Graph `sendMail`) | A refresh token newer than the backup's, because tokens "replace themselves with a fresh token upon every use" and the platform "doesn't revoke old refresh tokens when used"; access tokens it already holds | Access tokens live "a random value ranging between 60-90 minutes" by default (one hour on the CAE page; 20–28 hours in CAE sessions, which Lisa never requests). The pages read don't say that removing a personal account's consent for the app revokes every refresh token of the grant, or how quickly | **None.** Lisa's own `invalid_grant` with the restored token shows only that this copy is dead |
| Gmail (`users.messages.send`) | The refresh token, and access tokens that live for their grant's `expires_in` | "It might take some time before the revocation has full effect", with no bound; no fixed access-token lifetime | **None** |
| iCloud SMTP (app-specific password), Proton Bridge SMTP, APNs, Twilio | Passwords, keys or tokens on the old volume | The plan cites no provider documentation bounding when a revoked credential stops working | **None** |

So `t_end(W, c)` above is the writer fence's time for every channel, and an unfenced writer keeps delivery closed (§8.5.4). If a provider later documents both conditions, the cutoff can be added as a plan change citing that documentation. Rotating or revoking these credentials is still worth doing to contain a writer, and is recorded as `fence note --credentials-rotated`, which is information only.

**Holds** are keyed by the slot `(occurrence, firing, recipient)` from `LisaFiringIdentity`, which is stable. A hold therefore survives regeneration of the slot from replayed work, R1's recovery firing it again, ordinary `delivery resume`, and restarts.
- **Sender uncertainty** holds:
  1. every backup row in `pending` or `inProgress` at the cut;
  2. every slot with a `submit-start` after `s_cut` in any journal Lisa holds, recreated as held if the restored database lacks it;
  3. **every slot whose deliverable window overlaps some writer's possible-submission interval for its channel** — `D(s) ∩ A(W, c) ≠ ∅` — whether it is in the backup, missing from it, or first created afterward by replay, by `recover`, or by the scheduler. The check runs in the transaction that creates or claims the delivery (step 1 above), against the stored intervals.
