- **Kept:** every identifier; explicit corrections as owner authority; preferences; source cursors; calendar occurrences; brief and plan history; reminder state; delivery attempts and unknown outcomes; device registrations (as legacy registrations that can be upgraded); capture request keys.
- **Authority of migrated values:**
  - `ownerCorrected` fields become owner authority.
  - Other fields of extracted tasks keep model authority, so R1's extraction behavior is preserved.
  - Tasks created by the owner are owner authority throughout.
  - **Notes carry no flag in R1, and the model writes them only at creation.** An extracted task's notes may therefore be model text or owner text. They become one note piece of **ambiguous authority**: protected like an owner value, never replaced by model or source writes, and displayed "from R1, author unknown".
  - **Notes of any length** migrate losslessly: a note over its value limit becomes a blob, and one over 1 MiB a blob list whose whole-value digest is checked (§8.3.3). `migrate inspect` reports the longest note and the number of blob lists.
  - Idea fields are owner authority, except the planner's unschedulable fields, which are system authority.
- **Files outside SQLite.** Carry the tagged input inventory into migration and reverse preparation. A valid schedule supplies job state. A verified never-run source can start from frozen `RuntimeState()`; otherwise recover scheduling from the frozen cut and subsequent safety evidence, and hold affected work if an unsafe run cannot be excluded. R1 treats absence of its budget file as no local reservation yet. Use zero only when the history of this owner-local day supports it (for example, a newly created deterministic-only installation). A missing file alone is insufficient to erase known or uncertain spending. Carry forward known charges and count uncertain charges conservatively up to the effective allowance, or keep inference disabled. An absent dry-run log contributes no capture records. Archive a present log unchanged. Malformed or unexpectedly lost inputs require explicit recovery from verifiable evidence or remain blocked. The installer always generates and verifies both required output sidecars (§8.14.7).
- **Deliveries, in R1's states:**
  - `outcomeUnknown`, and `inProgress` in the submitting phase, stay unknown and are never resent.
  - `inProgress` in the preparing phase returns to pending.
  - Pending deliveries past validity expire.
  - Core starts with delivery suspended.
- **Reminders and intents** map to occurrences and firings (§8.10.1).
- **R1 ledger records** become **outcome-only parents** (origin path `migrated`): scope, request ID, R1's fingerprint, status and expiry, with an unknown child set until verified bytes attach (§8.12.2). Error bodies are re-rendered from R1's templates, so no caller text survives; success bodies are kept until expiry only if R1's audit shows no deletion after they were recorded.
- **Causal metadata** starts from a documented migration origin. No "latest" timestamp is invented to make migrated values win (R2-MIG-02).

#### 8.14.3 Classifying R1 queue entries (finding 2; approval blocker 4)

**Required:** keep each queued action's input and request identity; decide whether it was applied; convert only known semantics; quarantine ambiguity; never replay blindly (R2-MIG-03). **Owner requirement (2026-11-22):** preserve original inputs for deliberate review whenever the available evidence can't establish an effect.

**What R1's code allows us to conclude (§1.6;** `APIRouter.swift`, `WorkHandlers.swift`, `OfflineQueue.swift`, `CaptureService.swift`**)**
- A refusal can coexist with committed children: task and idea PATCH, preferences PATCH and notification actions commit before a later refusal.
- **A recorded answer describes only the attempt that produced it.** A first attempt can commit a correction and lose its answer (a crash or a failed ledger write before recording, or a 504). A retry with the same request ID then meets the revision check, and the resulting 409 is recorded under the same key. That 409 says nothing about the first attempt.
- `attempts` counts attempts that never left the device, and `lastError` keeps only the latest answer. An entry that got a 504 (maybe applied, no uncertainty set) and then a 401 shows only the 401. `uncertainSince` is set once and cleared only by a requeue.
- A 503 can mean "the same request is still being processed", so the request may have been applied.
- **A requeue** assigns a new request ID and resets attempts, errors and uncertainty. Only the original `id` and the current `requestID` survive; every intermediate request ID, and whatever happened under it, is lost.
- **A capture key proves only that the (caller, request ID) pair was used.** Once the ledger record is gone, R1 answers a capture retry from the key whatever the body, so the queued body may differ from what was captured.
- **A capture's title takes effect only sometimes.** For kinds text, link and Apple News, R1 names the news item with the supplied title only when the capture creates the item; a capture of a URL already saved links to the existing item, whose title stays. The item's evidence records which capture created it, and shared-content items are never pruned (§1.6).
- Ledger records expire after 30 days and are written after the mutation.
- Captures were not deletable in R1 production, but R2 can delete them. Audit events, including `data.deleted`, age out after 365 days.

**Evidence, scoped to the original caller, request and child**
- **The file:** status, attempts, `uncertainSince`, last status and message, kept response, `id`, `requestID` and the original body.
- **Core's read-only lookups** for both `requestID` and `id` in the caller's scope (authorized by a lookup grant or the legacy link, §8.14.4–§8.14.5):
  - the migrated R1 record (an outcome-only parent, §8.12.2) or a v1 parent, with fingerprint match, status and attribution: pre-commit, after a commit, or unknown;
  - for captures, the capture key in live records or in tombstones, and Core's **verdict over every effect-bearing input of the capture's kind**:
    - kind `idea`: kind, trimmed text, trimmed URL and channel. R1 stores the idea as `text ?? url` and ignores a title;
    - kinds `text`, `link` and `sharedAppleNews`: kind, trimmed text, trimmed URL and channel must agree (otherwise `differs`), and then **the title, in both directions**, by the table below. R1's derived title for a body is its `title`, else the first 120 characters of its text, else its URL, else "Shared item". Two facts make the stored title trustworthy provenance: the item's evidence names this capture as its creator (`externalID` = the capture ID, §1.6), and the item's title is still the one set at its creation — its revision is the initial one and no migrated or R2 operation changed it;

      | This capture created its news item | Title still the creation value | Queued body | Verdict |
      |---|---|---|---|
      | Yes | Yes | Explicit title T | `matches` if the item's title is T; otherwise `differs` (R1 received a body without T, or with another title) |
      | Yes | Yes | No title | `matches` if the item's title equals the queued body's derived title; otherwise `differs` (R1 received an explicit title) |
      | Yes | No | Either | `unknown`: the title R1 set can't be read any more |
      | No: the URL was already saved, so this capture linked to the existing item | — | Explicit title T | `unknown`: T had no visible effect, so the stored state can't show which body R1 received, and the owner's intended title isn't shown anywhere |
      | No | — | No title | `matches`: the link is the capture's only effect |
      | The capture or its item was deleted | — | Either | `unknown` (a tombstone keeps no content) |

      An explicit title that equals the queued body's derived title matches either way: R1 would have stored the same title from either body.
    - **R1's ledger record outranks the table:** while R1's record for this caller and request ID exists, its fingerprint over the whole body decides exactly (rules 1 and 2 below), whatever the title shows.

    Core compares and returns only the verdict;
  - for captures with unresolved history, how many captures by the same caller match the queued content (a count, never content). It is shown for review and is never a proof;
  - R1's audit horizon, and whether any capture deletion was audited after `createdAt`.
- **Attribution by R1's exact messages:**
  - **Pre-commit:**
    - the 400 body and schema errors;
    - task title, estimate and deadline 400s;
    - "the task changed since it was read" and "the idea changed since it was read";
    - "send between 1 and 50 changes";
    - "unknown action";
    - "the notification does not exist".
  - **After a commit:**
    - "the task cannot move from …" and "the idea cannot move from …";
    - notification-action errors after the receipt;
    - any refusal of a preference change other than the first.
  - **Unknown:** "the item changed since it was read; reload it and try again", and a 404 from task or idea services.

**History, decided before anything else**

| History | Condition |
|---|---|
| `unsent` | status `pending`, `attempts` 0, `requestID = id` |
| `single` | exactly one attempt, `requestID = id`, no `uncertainSince` |
| `unresolved` | anything else: `uncertainSince` set; `requestID ≠ id` (requeued); two or more attempts; status `sending` |

**Classes**

| Class | Meaning | What the owner can safely do |
|---|---|---|
| A. Provably unsent | Positive evidence that no attempt reached R1 or applied | Converted, with the conversion authority (§8.14.4–§8.14.5), if its semantics are known and its expected revision still matches; otherwise offered prefilled for confirmation |
| B. Fully applied | Positive evidence of a 2xx for this caller, request and fingerprint, or the capture key with the verdict `matches` | Nothing; shown in history |
| C1. Rejected, no effect | A definite refusal attributed to before any commit, or from a single-step route, **on an entry whose only attempt produced it** | Re-enter deliberately from a prefilled form against current state; no duplicate is possible |
| C2. Rejected, partial or unknown effect | A refusal after a commit, of unknown attribution, or on an entry with unresolved history | Child by child — each field, or each preference change — the requested value beside the current one; the owner re-applies only what is missing |
| D. Indeterminate | Anything else | The original bytes are kept for inspection; "apply anyway" warns of possible duplication; or discard |

**Rules, by precedence** (a rule applies only if no earlier rule did):
1. **Identity.** A server record for `requestID` or `id` with another fingerprint, or a capture key whose verdict is `differs` or `unknown` → **D**: a collision or an ambiguity, never an application of this input. The review states which input couldn't be established ("your title 'Plumber notes' isn't shown: this link was already saved as 'Home repairs'").
2. **Positive application.** A 2xx server record with a matching fingerprint for `requestID` or `id`, a capture key whose verdict is `matches`, or status `done` with its 2xx response → **B**. With unresolved history on a route that has no idempotency of its own (notification and reminder actions), the history notes "an earlier attempt may also have applied this", read from R1's data.
3. **Receipts.** Notification receipts → **A**: applying one twice is harmless.
4. **Unresolved history outranks everything below, and never yields A or C1:**
   - a server refusal record for `requestID` or `id` → **C2**, labeled "an earlier attempt may have applied part of this";
   - otherwise → **D**. For a capture, the review shows the matching-content count ("a capture with this text exists"), so a capture made under a lost intermediate request ID is never created again automatically.
5. **One attempt with a server refusal record** → **C1** for single-step routes or pre-commit attribution; **C2** otherwise.
6. **One attempt, no server record:** the last answer was 401 or 429 (both answered before R1's ledger), or one of R1's fixed "never sent" descriptions (not paired, credential unavailable, connection refused, host unreachable), and, for a capture, no capture deletion was audited after `createdAt` with the audit horizon at or before it → **A**. Any other answer (422, 500, 502, 503, 504, transport, decoding) → **D**.
7. **Unsent** → **A**, with the same capture condition as rule 6; otherwise **D**.
8. Everything else → **D**.

Wherever these rules can't establish a child's result from reliable, scoped evidence, its original input is preserved for the owner's deliberate review; nothing is converted or discarded on a guess.

**Restart safety:** the import keeps one record per entry, keyed by its manifest index, `id` and `requestID`, with the stored evidence and class. A conversion goes through the parent `(scope, requestID)` (§8.12.2), so a re-run never converts twice (§8.14.4). Class D bytes are kept exactly.

**Acceptance (M9; S-02):** frozen R1 queue files and R1 datasets built from `491c90a` will cover every rule. Among them:
- **an earlier committed correction followed by a recorded 409:** attempt 1 commits the title correction and R1 is killed before recording; attempt 2 records "the task changed since it was read". Expected: C2 ("an earlier attempt may have applied part of this"), with the title already equal to the requested value; never C1;
- **a capture created under a missing intermediate requeue ID:** `id` r0 fails after its retry period; a requeue gives r1, which R1 applies while the answer is lost; the entry fails again; a requeue gives r2; then the import. Expected: D with a matching-content count of 1; never A, and no second capture;
- **an existing capture key with a different queued body and an expired ledger:** the key exists for this caller and request ID, the ledger record has expired, and the stored capture's text differs from the queued body. Expected: D (`differs`), with the queued input kept;
- **two otherwise identical captures with different titles:** under one request ID, R1 kept the first body (title "Home repairs") and created its news item; after the ledger expired, the queued entry carries the same key, text, URL and channel with the title "Plumber notes". Expected: D (`differs`), the queued bytes kept. Under two request IDs with the same text and URL, the first creates the item; the second links to it: the first is B, the second D (`unknown`), because its title had no visible effect;
- **the shared-URL case:** the URL was saved earlier by another capture with another title; the queued link capture has its own key and a title. Expected: D (`unknown`), its bytes kept; the same capture without a title is B;
- **titles in both directions, with the ledger record expired:** (i) queued title "Plumber notes", the item this capture created titled "Plumber notes" → B; (ii) queued title "Plumber notes", the item created under the derived title (R1 received no title) → D (`differs`); (iii) no queued title, the item created under the derived title → B; (iv) no queued title, the item created under "Home repairs" (R1 received an explicit title) → D (`differs`); (v) a queued title equal to the derived title → B; (vi) the item's title changed by a migrated or R2 operation after creation → D (`unknown`); (vii) with R1's ledger record still present, its fingerprint decides each of (i)–(vi) exactly;
- an idea capture whose body has a title: B when kind, text, URL and channel match (R1 ignores an idea's title);
- a task PATCH whose correction committed and whose status was refused on a single attempt (C2, with the field diff);
- a capture missing together with its record and deletion audit after a test-path deletion and audit ageing (D, not A);
- a 504 followed by a 401 (unresolved history, D, not A);
- a lost 409 recorded on the server for a plan-block action, on a single attempt (C1);
- a raw `sending` entry with and without a server record (D, or B and C2);
- a restart halfway through the import, with nothing converted twice.

Post-downgrade reconciliation cases are also part of S-02 (§8.14.8).

#### 8.14.4 Upgrading the Mac (finding 3)

**What the old Mac is (§1.6):** an unsandboxed app that embeds the host in process as the local owner, or is a loopback owner client of `lisa serve`. Its queue at `~/Library/Application Support/org.example.lisa.mac/Client/queue/queue.json` holds owner-scope entries only when sends failed. It has no pending-actions screen.

**Upgrade procedure (`docs/MIGRATION-R1-R2.md`):**
1. Stop R1 (§8.14.1).
2. Migrate the data directory.
3. Install the R2 Mac app under the same bundle ID; it runs sandboxed, so the old path is outside its container.
4. Enroll with pairing text.
5. Import the old queue as described below.

**Owner-authorized, read-only import, with separate grants**
1. **Select:** Settings › "Import changes from the previous Lisa for Mac" opens an open panel aimed at the old `Client/queue/` folder. The app opens the file read-only, copies it into its import area, records its SHA-256, and keeps only a read-only security-scoped bookmark. The original is checked unchanged after the import.
2. **Manifest:** `{file SHA-256, entries: [(index, id, requestID, kind, method, path, R1 fingerprint)]}`, signed by the Mac and uploaded with an `owner-import` challenge (`POST /v2/migration/imports`). Core stores it as pending and returns an import ID; the Mac shows the manifest digest as eight words.
3. **Lookup grant:** `lisa core migrate import approve-lookups <id> --digest <words>` on the administrative terminal, signed and bound to the import ID, the Mac's device ID, the manifest digest and scope `owner`. It must be used within 10 minutes, then stays valid until the import completes or for 7 days. It lets Core run the read-only lookups of §8.14.3 for exactly the manifest's `requestID` and `id` values. **It authorizes nothing else**, and in particular no conversion.
4. **Items:** the Mac uploads each entry as a **legacy import item**: the entry's fields and original body bytes, signed by the Mac (`POST /v2/migration/imports/{id}/items`).
   - An item's ID is derived from the import ID and its manifest index, so a re-upload is a duplicate (same bytes) or `identity_reuse` (different bytes).
   - Core recomputes each item's R1 fingerprint from its bytes. An item whose fingerprint differs from its manifest line is refused as malformed.
   - Items may arrive in several parts and in any order.
5. **Classification at Core.** Core classifies each item (§8.14.3) from its file evidence and Core's own lookups, and stores the result with the evidence. Later reads return the stored result, so retention running in between changes nothing. The Mac receives `{class, evidence summary}`, never a body, title or value from Core's records.
6. **Review and conversion approval.** `lisa core migrate import review <id>` shows each entry's class, evidence and requested values. `lisa core migrate import approve-conversion <id> --entries <indexes | class-A> --digest <words>` records the owner's signed approval, bound to the import ID, the manifest digest and the listed entries' indexes and fingerprints. This approval is the conversion authority (§8.12.2).
7. **Conversion,** in manifest order: entry i is converted only after every earlier entry has an outcome (converted, or classified and not approved). Each conversion is one transaction through `LisaLegacyRequests`, keyed by `(owner, requestID)`:
   - **no parent:** Core creates the parent (origin path `import`, authority the approval) with its complete child set, the children and their receipts, and records the item's outcome;
   - **a parent with the same fingerprint** (a v1 retry, or a migrated R1 record, got there first): nothing new is created; the item's outcome references that parent and its recorded outcome;
   - **a parent with another fingerprint:** class D (collision); nothing is converted.

   Classes B, C1, C2 and D are never converted automatically. The owner re-enters C1 and C2 values deliberately from the review, and applies, edits or discards D entries in the app's inspection list.
8. **Owner-only and administrative keys.** R1 lets only the owner on the host change some keys, and R2-TRUST-05 keeps administrative keys CLI-only. An approved preferences request that reaches an administrative key waits there: that child is `held_for_administrative_review`, every later child is `pending_behind_review`, and earlier children are converted (§8.12.2). The owner decides at the administrative terminal with `lisa core legacy resolve <token> apply|refuse`, using the decision token `legacy review` prints for that hold (§8.12.2): `apply` continues the request in order, up to the next administrative key, which holds again under its own token; `refuse` ends it as R1 ends a refused change, with the later children `not_reached`. Running the same command again replays the recorded outcome, and a stale token moves nothing. The item reports `awaiting_administrative_review` with the current token meanwhile, and each decision is recorded on the parent, which completes after the last one. Discarding the entry in review before conversion creates nothing.
9. **Resumption.** The Mac records, per entry, that it was uploaded and the item receipt Core signed (class, outcome, parent). After a crash it reads `GET /v2/migration/imports/{id}` and continues. Core's records — manifest, grants, stored evidence, item outcomes and parents — make every step idempotent.

**Recovery facts (import).** *Survives:* the Mac's import record and its copy of the queue file; Core's import record, items, evidence and parents; the unchanged original file. *Who acts:* the owner approves lookups and conversions and resolves held children; Core converts; the Mac uploads and displays. *Safe after an interruption:* re-upload and re-read; the parent key makes a second conversion impossible. *What the owner sees when the outcome is unknown:* the entry as class D, with its reason ("an earlier attempt may have saved this") and its original content, to apply, edit or discard.

**Acceptance (M9 harness; X7; S-03):**
- **the full input range:** a preferences request of 50 valid changes — all to one key, and again spread over three keys with repeats — converted, then relayed to a second replica in reverse order. Every child will have at most one dependency, the replica will apply each key's chain in request order, and the final values will equal R1's;
- a multi-preference request (three changes: one key repeated, one administrative key in the middle), a task PATCH and a capture, imported **in parts and out of order** (items 3, 1, 2): conversion waits for manifest order;
- **the held middle child, in every history:** import first, then a v1 retry through the owner listener; v1 first, then the import; each with `apply` and with `refuse`; and an import entry discarded in review, then converted by a v1 retry. While the parent waits, the frozen queue's retry will get the 503 and stay pending, and the frozen CLI will report R1's "unavailable"; after the decision every retry will get the recorded answer. For every child in every history, its authority (approval, link, R1 credential or the CLI decision), disposition, effect on the projection and Core-signed receipt will be compared: the same IDs and digests everywhere, and the same dispositions for the same decision;
- **crash and retry around the hold:** a kill after the parent and its prefix commit, and another after the decision commits but before the suffix's last child; each restart resumes, and nothing is converted twice;
- **an outcome-only migrated parent:** an R1 record of a three-change preferences request answered 409. A v1 retry with the same bytes will get R1's recorded, sanitized answer; an import item with the same bytes will attach them, and review will show each change's R1 history as `unknown_in_r1` beside the current values; an item with other bytes under that key will be class D and attach nothing;
- **a restricted sibling:** a preferences request changing `model.endpoint` (a synthetic canary URL) between two permitted keys. The actual bytes a relay forwards — change-feed pages, nearby frames, receipts — will be captured and searched: neither the canary, the key name nor R1's fingerprint will appear; the relay will verify membership, order and the redacted receipt; and the replication allowlist check will pass;
- **an interruption after the first durable step:** a kill after the manifest is stored, and another after item 1's conversion commits but before the Mac records it. Each restart resumes, and nothing is converted twice;
- **a partially committed prefix:** an entry whose migrated R1 record shows a later preference change refused after earlier ones committed. The migrated parent is found, nothing is converted, and the class is C2 with the per-change values;
- **a different body declaring the same fingerprint:** an item whose bytes don't match its manifest fingerprint is refused as malformed; an item with another body under an existing parent's request ID is class D;
- **for every child:** its disposition, its effect on the projection, and its Core-signed receipt with its own digest (or its redacted receipt). A simulated relay forwards the children to a second replica in reverse order and checks signatures, ID derivation, `setDigest` and same-entity chains: no child is applied before its predecessor, and none is missing from the set;
- an expired or mismatched grant refused; a lookup outside the manifest refused; the lookup grant shown to authorize no conversion;
- the original file's hash unchanged.

**X7 prerequisite:** a genuine old Mac app.
- The owner builds R1's Mac app from the reconstructed `491c90a` tree (§8.18) at or before X3 and records the Xcode version.
- If it doesn't build, X7 uses a fixture queue file produced by the frozen Linux R1 client and placed at the old path, and reports the genuine-app part as `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (owner; the R1 Mac app doesn't build).

#### 8.14.5 Upgrading a paired iPhone or iPad

**What the old companion supports (§1.6):** only `/v1/` with API major version 1 and R1's ten error codes; pairing by code; a Keychain identity `{installationID, deviceID, credential}`; direct push registration; trust of up to eight owner anchors plus an optional certificate pin.
- After migration it reaches Core only if the owner changes its host address and trust settings to Core's. Its migrated legacy registration then authenticates it.
- Queued mutations go through the v1 translation (§8.12.2).

**Linking the old registration to the new identity**
- On first launch, the R2 app (same bundle ID, same container and Keychain access) reads the R1 identity item and queue file read-only, and computes the queue manifest as the Mac does (§8.14.4).
- Its enrollment request includes `legacyLink {r1DeviceID, r1InstallationID, r1Credential, queueManifestDigest}`. These bytes are secret-bearing and stored like the rest of the request (§8.1.2).
- Core verifies the credential in constant time against the legacy registration's verifier, and checks that the registration is active and unlinked.
- In the consuming transaction (§8.1.2 step 5), Core:
  - records the link with the declared manifest digest;
  - moves eligible device-local settings (notification preferences and categories) to the new device;
  - maps delivery history to it;
  - removes the legacy registration from routing;
  - retires the legacy credential (verifier deleted, state `upgraded`).

  The old registration's push token is deleted when the new device registers its token, and only one of the two is ever eligible.

**Lookups, import and conversion:**
- After linking, the device uploads its manifest and items exactly as the Mac does (§8.14.4), in scope `device:<legacy ID>`, with a `legacy-link-lookup` challenge. Core classifies them (§8.14.3).
- **Authority.** The link proves the R1 credential under which R1 would have applied this device's requests. It therefore authorizes the lookups in that scope and the conversion of class A entries of the declared manifest — no other entries and no other scope. It is not derived from a lookup grant.
- Changes R1 refuses from a device (owner-only keys, deletes) are decided as R1 decides them: `refused (403)`. The app shows them with "Only your administrative terminal can change this setting."
- Classes B, C1, C2 and D are reviewed in the app, where the owner re-enters or discards each one deliberately.
- If the R1 credential no longer verifies (rotated or revoked), enrollment proceeds without a link, and the import needs the owner's lookup grant and conversion approval, like the Mac's, bound to scope `device:<id>`.

**What a frozen Linux client can prove:** R1's `LisaClient` and offline queue, built from `491c90a` on Linux, prove:
- wire compatibility: version header, error decoding, status mapping;
- queue state transitions and the file format;
- the link and lookup flows with an R1 credential.

They cannot prove Apple presentation, Keychain access, the sandbox, URLSession trust, or the update of the installed app on a device. X7 covers those.

**Acceptance (M9; X7; S-03):** the checks will cover:
- link and import with a valid credential: class A entries converted under the link, an owner-only key refused as R1 refuses it, and other classes left for review;
- an item outside the declared manifest refused;
- refusal with a revoked one, and the import then waiting for the owner's grants;
- no device eligible twice at any point (routing checked before and after the link commit);
- delivery history and preferences preserved;
- the old credential refused afterwards;
- on devices at X7: the R1 companion upgraded in place.

#### 8.14.6 Cutover

The R1 host never resumes automatically. The Mac connects as an R2 client. Devices reconcile before normal scheduling starts. The owner reviews, then resumes delivery (R2-MIG-04).

#### 8.14.7 Rollback (finding 13)

**Required:** a compatible pre-migration backup or an explicit reverse migration; never old software on a newer schema; never two schedulers (R2-MIG-04).

1. **Preflight** (`lisa core migrate rollback --preflight`, listener mode) creates **rollback generation R**: a signed record with a counter and a random ID. It is published in capabilities and `GET /v2/rollback`, and lists everything outstanding.
2. **Acknowledgements.** Each device sends a signed `{R, deviceID, incarnation, finalJournalPosition, outstanding {signedUncommitted, waiting, drafts, quarantined, unresolvedResolutions}, exportDigest?, headSeen}`.
   - It covers everything up to `finalJournalPosition`: signed operations without a Core receipt (waiting ones included), drafts, quarantined inputs (R1 queue items and rejected work kept for inspection), and draft conflict resolutions not yet submitted.
   - The acknowledgement is clean only if every count is zero after a final sync. Otherwise it must carry the digest of a protected export containing everything it counts.
   - **Silence is never read as clean:** a device that hasn't acknowledged is listed as outstanding with the last position and pending count it reported.
3. **After acknowledging,** a device keeps saving, because capture is never refused.
   - Entries beyond the acknowledged position are **late**. The device shows "Saved on this device. Lisa Core is being rolled back; export this change." and offers export.
   - A late entry is never shown as synced, and Core never learns of it: only the device and its exports hold it. After Core stops, the app says "Lisa Core was rolled back; export your device-only changes".
4. **Fence** (`--fence`, owner, after preflight review):
   - Core intake is fenced: v2 gets `rollback_in_progress`; v1 gets 503 `unavailable`. No new legacy parent, hold or receipt batch can start after this point.
   - Connectors and the scheduler stop. The outbox is suspended.
   - In-flight attempts settle: Core waits until no delivery is `inProgress`, and a `submitting` attempt that times out becomes `outcomeUnknown`.
   - Cancel commands go to every delegated assignment. Confirmations are collected until the owner proceeds, and unconfirmed slots are recorded "may fire locally".
   - **Legacy parents are settled before any R1 ledger exists** (§8.12.2). Preflight already listed them; the archive isn't written while one is open:
     - **a parent `awaiting_administrative_review`:** the owner decides each hold with `lisa core legacy resolve <token> apply|refuse`, or runs `lisa core migrate rollback --settle-legacy refuse-remaining`, which records a `refuse` decision, with actor "rollback settlement", for every hold still waiting — through the same bound tokens, so a decision already made is replayed, never repeated. The **applied prefix** (children decided before the hold) stays committed. The **unapproved suffix** is protected: a refused hold leaves every later child `not_reached`, and settlement never applies one. The recorded answer is R1's 403 (`forbidden`, "This setting can be changed only on the administrative terminal.");
     - **a receipts parent with partial progress** (a storage failure stopped it after k of n receipts): Core completes it from its stored request bytes, one receipt per transaction advancing the recorded progress, exactly as a retry would, and records the 204. R1 records receipts idempotently (`DeliveryOutbox.recordReceipt` changes only an accepted, unknown or in-progress delivery, `Sources/LisaDelivery/DeliveryOutbox.swift`), so the committed prefix is never applied twice;
     - **interrupted settlement:** every decision and every receipt step is its own transaction. After a crash, `--settle-legacy refuse-remaining` is run again: decided tokens replay their outcomes, receipt parents continue from their progress, and the preflight shows what remains. The archive, `--reverse` and `--install` refuse while anything is unsettled;
     - **what the frozen client gets after the rollback, on either database:** each settled parent becomes an R1 ledger record that the installation writes into the v4 database being installed, before R1 can open it (step 6; §8.14.8): the 403 for a refused hold, which the frozen queue marks failed with that message; 204 for completed receipts, which it marks done; and, for a request whose holds the owner applied, the reversed-record reply of §8.12.2 (the universal 409, which it marks a conflict). R1 replays a ledger record without running the request (`Sources/LisaAPI/APIRouter.swift`), so neither the refused child nor its suffix can run in R1.
   - Then the **rollback archive** is written: the v5 backup plus a rollback record. The record holds the signed acknowledgements; each outstanding device with the last position and pending count it reported; waiting operations; unresolved conflicts; uncertain and held deliveries; reservations; every legacy parent with its children, decision records and recorded answer; **Core's final disposition and digest for every operation it decided**, with its retained-evidence class (exact, or decision only where Core compacted the body, §8.4.5); the **projection map** from each R2 entity to the R1 record reverse migration writes for it, with that record's ID, revision and value — and, for an `accepted_with_conflict` field, which candidate was projected (§8.14.8); and the **identities of both v4 databases** the owner may install: the reversed database (rollback generation R and its identity marker, below) and the `prepare` backup (its file digest, schema version, counts and any last audit event ID, recorded at `prepare`).
5. **Build the rollback candidates.** After settlement, `--reverse` produces the reversed v4 data in a private working directory. Retain the immutable original `prepare` backup. Bind the archive to the cut, both base identities and the safety projection in §8.14.8. For either choice, assemble the v4 database and final R1-format `runtime-state.json` and `model-requests.json`. Record the configuration digest, selected owner zone, affected channels and secret references. Keep `dry-run/captures.jsonl` as protected evidence without overwriting any newer R1 entries. R2 remains fenced throughout.
6. **Stage on the active R1 host** (`lisa core migrate rollback --install reversed|prepare --stage --r1-data-dir <dir>`, or the Mac helper below).
   1. Acquire the frozen host's own exclusive `<dir>/lisa.lock` without waiting. Preserve the lock file and directory in place across the entire procedure. Only accept the verified original fence, an explicitly selected stopped original Mac source matching its migration inventory, or a prior installed generation verified by its durable anchor and independently retained installation record (§8.14.8). If its binding or continuity is uncertain, end staging before any fence or replacement. Complete the locked rebind in its declared lock order, then restart staging and inventory the target again. Reject unknown sources, a missing live database or a busy lock.
   2. Before changing a runnable directory, verify the starting state under that lock. Take and verify a protected, restorable snapshot of the live database (including committed WAL) and each sidecar and auxiliary file in its observed present/absent state under §8.14.1. Previously required output that has since gone missing is recorded as a discrepancy for safety reconciliation. Keep it under a unique prior-generation ID with a durable journal. On reinstall, compare the current installation to its starting manifest and all observed R1 changes. Carry forward later refusal guards, revocations, delivery/receipt state, schedule and spending, and separately report owner data that replacement would discard. If a change cannot be safely accounted for, stop with the existing generation intact. Reconciliation or deliberate owner selection of a specifically inventoried and preserved replacement is required before retry. Never discard intervening changes on an automatic retry.
   3. If the live database is runnable, commit the same newer SQLite `user_version` used by `prepare` on the **actual live file**, under the lock. Checkpoint and close it, require a successful checkpoint with no live WAL frames, sync file and directory, and reopen read-only to confirm the fence. Journal the snapshot digest and live identity. A pre-fence interruption leaves the old complete generation eligible to run; resumption starts with a fresh locked inventory. Release the lock for verification only after the actual live database durably refuses frozen R1.
   4. Assemble a separate owner-only, same-filesystem candidate at R1 version 4. In one transaction project the §8.14.8 safety rows, refusal and ordinary ledger records, plus the immutable installation anchor and initial reconciliation binding defined below. Complete both sidecars from the same cut. Validate v4 schema, record codecs, indexes and actions on both targets. Checkpoint/close the candidate, require no remaining WAL data, sync each file and its directory. Record phase `staged` in a crash-safe journal with installation ID, R, selection, input and final digests/sizes of **all three install files**, prior generation, owner-local budget day/zone, configuration and archive digests. The private candidate is never R1's configured data directory.
7. **Verify this candidate** (`lisa core migrate verify-r1 --copy --staged <ID>` on Linux, `lisa-archive verify-r1 --copy --staged <ID>` on the Mac).
   - **Every installation.** Verify the three sealed input files against their manifest. Open an isolated disposable copy with the verified frozen R1 build/readers (including the CLI and the verifier linked to the pinned frozen codecs, store and request lookup). Check v4 integrity, typed row decoding, projected counts/IDs against the frozen cut, both required sidecars, job timing, budget, protected refusal rows, delivery/receipt/hold state, device revocations, route disablement and the installation anchor. Exercise native lookup by the stored scoped key and fingerprint for each projected live ledger row at the recorded verification time. Check status, expiry in both representations, and the expected sanitized response shape. Check route-specific success only where route evidence exists. For outcome-only rows validate the specified generic 409 or sanitized response through its known common codec. Each invariant has its expected set from the cut, including zero members. Missing required rows/files or unclassified safety state cannot pass. Run only on the copy with outbound I/O blocked, controlled endpoints, isolated keys and a non-serving check of the production configuration’s effective settings. No owner account or production provider is needed for verification.
   - **Release qualification of the tools.** S-02/S-03/S-13 exercise actual frozen typed clients, queue, CLI and worker on seeded candidates whose original requests and expected results the harness owns (§8.14.8). Test each supported platform under its assigned executor. An owner installation uses the qualified verifier and installer for its frozen build and platform. Its local pass binds the actual installation ID, digests/sizes of all three immutable files, cut, configuration, verifier/frozen build identities, expected sets and observed checks. Do not synthesize an unknown request body from a fingerprint, seed tests in an owner's bundle or demand production data matching the fixture. Any input change or failed invariant invalidates the local receipt.
8. **Switch** (`lisa core migrate rollback --install --switch <ID> --r1-data-dir <dir>`). Reacquire the same lock. Check the journal, positive live fence/identity, every staged digest, matching verification pass and current execution configuration/owner-local budget day. If the day or applicable inputs changed, rebuild the affected generation and reverify. Retain the full protected prior generation. Require an unambiguous, closed live database and no recoverable live SQLite side files. Publish the two verified sidecars with same-filesystem atomic replacements while the database is still fenced; sync each and their directory. Verify both at their live paths. Publish the verified, closed v4 database with **one atomic rename over the fenced live file, last**, and sync the directory. Record `installed` and release the lock. Never remove the live database or replace the lock inode. Preserve existing ownership and owner-only permissions.
9. **Restart and resume.** Before the database rename a crash leaves a fenced file at the live path once step 6.3 committed. Recover idempotently from the manifest under the lock, rechecking or repairing each sidecar while fenced. After the rename R1 sees one complete generation. If `installed` was not journalled, recognize the new installation marker and its verified publication record, complete the journal, and preserve any subsequent R1 mutations. Never overwrite a now-runnable database or its newer sidecars to make an old hash match. An abort may reactivate a prior complete, verified R1 generation only if it still enforces all applicable cut and later safety state. Otherwise leave the live database fenced and preserve the prior bundle as evidence. A safe reactivation also publishes the database last under the lock. The owner starts a single R1 runtime only after handover. R2 remains fenced.

**Mac installation and late exports.** Provide a small, owner-built native macOS command-line target, `lisa-archive`, sharing the portable rollback, archive verification, matching, reconciliation and maintenance code. It uses the local R1 directory lock, owner-only loopback endpoint and system SQLite through the same interfaces. Its operations are `install --stage/--switch`, `verify-r1 --copy`, `inspect`, `reconcile`, `reconcile --maintenance`, `rebind --r1-data-dir <dir> --installation <id>`, `revoke-device` and `log`; document exact invocations, input paths and how the owner builds and runs them. The prepared candidate can originate on Linux, but activation, live reads and guarded writes occur on the actual Mac directory. For any late device export, copy only the protected export to the Mac and rerun that installation's local helper with the key backup. Never reconcile against an old Linux copy and overwrite the active Mac. M9/X7 must exercise this workflow on the owner's Mac. If the native path cannot operate against the actual frozen host, report the capability blocked and keep that host fenced until a working alternative is tested.

**What the rollback report claims.** Only what each device acknowledged or reported: "iPhone: acknowledged through position 420 at <time>; nothing outstanding", or "iPad: no acknowledgement; last reported position 512 with 2 pending, at <time>". Work a device saved after its acknowledgement, or after its last report, is **unknown to Core**. The report never counts or estimates it and says: "Changes saved on a device after the time shown aren't known to Lisa Core. They can be recovered from that device's export." Unreachable devices stay visible in the report, and their work is recoverable later through their exports and the post-downgrade tool.

#### 8.14.8 Reverse migration rules and the post-downgrade archive tool

**v5 to v4**
- R2 tables are dropped after being archived. Projections keep R1's record shape; R2 only added optional fields.
- Note pieces become R1's `notes` text in a deterministic order. A blob list is concatenated and checked against its whole-value digest, so a migrated note of any length returns byte for byte (§8.3.3).
- **Fields with several values,** which v4 can't hold:
  - the v4 record keeps the owner-authored value;
  - when owner values conflict, it keeps the one the owner selects in preflight, or otherwise R1's last value;
  - every alternative goes into the report;
  - owner or ambiguous-authority text is never replaced by a model or source value.
- `ownerCorrected` is regenerated from authority.
- **Deliveries:**
  - attempted ones keep their final state;
  - ones R2 created but never attempted become `cancelled` "rollback: not attempted";
  - slots that may fire locally have their R1 deliveries to that device suppressed;
  - uncertain and held deliveries become `outcomeUnknown`.
- **Refused administrative requests on either v4 database.** Keep a content-free guard from each R2 decision to refuse a held legacy request for the dataset's lifetime, including decisions completed before rollback and those whose ordinary reply expired. Store the original R1 caller scope, request ID, fingerprint and sanitized 403 template. Install each as an ordinary R1 `IdempotencyRecord` with the same protected expiration in both its decoded payload and its indexed `expires_at`. Use a finite end-of-supported-time value, e.g. `9999-12-31T00:00:00Z` (`253402214400000` ms), only after the frozen Linux and Mac codecs, lookup and maintenance have proved exact round-trip and retention. Document the supported clock range ending before this value; refuse the handover outside that range or if the precondition fails. Reject an inconsistent existing fingerprint. Frozen R1 then replays the 403 for the same request, returns its request-ID reuse error for changed bytes, and still accepts independently authorized new requests under new IDs. A 503 leaves the frozen queue pending without starting its uncertainty clock. Its age since creation establishes no safe expiry.
- **Other v1 replies.** Install unexpired settled parents with their actual remaining expiry. A content-free capture reply, eligible device's own valid record or 204 can be written as recorded. Other successes use the content-free §8.12.2 409 `reply_unavailable` with a current revision only where the target exists; errors use their sanitized R1 templates. Apply the same rules to outcome-only records. At install, decode each reply with frozen R1 and check both the indexed and encoded expiry. The refusal exception extends only the minimal protective records.
  - **In the `prepare` backup,** a parent whose effects Core applied after `prepare` is recorded the same way, so no retry runs it again — a refused administrative child and its suffix included. Its applied effects are absent from that database. A success that would have been the universal 409 is written as the same 409 with `lisaCode: "applied_after_backup"` and "Lisa R2 applied this request after the backup now installed; its changes may be missing here. Review them with Lisa's archive tool." Each absent effect is then a reconciliation candidate (below).
- **Durable installation identity inside the frozen v4 database.** At stage time write a native `IdempotencyRecord` at the fixed private scoped key `lisa-archive:installation:v1`. Its versioned content-free descriptor binds dataset, random installation ID, rollback generation, archive digest, selected source and pre-overlay source digest, and projection-map digest for the reversed source. Use a digest of the canonical descriptor as fingerprint, status 200, descriptor bytes as the native response, and the same protected expiry in payload and index as the refusal guards. A separate native record at `lisa-archive:binding:v1` binds that installation to a random reconciliation binding ID. On a fresh supported install create fresh IDs, preserving the predecessor's protected evidence. Refuse unknown contents or duplicates in this namespace. On a later upgrade classify only verified reserved rows as installer metadata, outside ordinary caller/retry conversion and replicated payloads. Keep the immutable installer receipt for the exact bundle outside the database, owner-only, with the input descriptor and initial binding. The descriptor never contains its own database file hash. The verified manifest binds that hash after sealing.
  - R1 derives every HTTP key as `<caller.scope>:<requestID>` (`RouterContext`, `APIRouter`), so adding a prefix to a wire request ID cannot reach either private key. Frozen `pruneRequestLedger` deletes by indexed expiry. Test native backup/restore, lookup, ordinary audit and ledger maintenance on frozen Linux and macOS throughout the supported clock range. A v4 compatibility or retention failure blocks handover. Record the historical `lisa.rollback.reverse-migration` and `lisa.rollback.install` audit events as available corroboration. R1 can prune them after its configured retention (one day is valid). Their disappearance alone does not invalidate the immutable anchor.
  - A byte copy inherits the anchor and any binding ID already present. Matching metadata, audit rows, file path, times, inode or database hash can establish compatibility with a saved state. None proves which physical copy continued running. Authority to act comes from the chosen local target, this session's current evidence and a fresh decision for each ambiguous effect.
- Restore only pre-existing legacy registrations still allowed at the final cut and by later R1 evidence on reinstall. For revoked/ambiguous registrations use R1's native revoked state, invalidate the credential verifier and clear the push token before R1 can authenticate or target the device. Honor later token changes and disabled routing; do not import R2 enrollment credentials as equivalent R1 authorization. List R2-only devices for owner pairing.
- **Safety state required for both choices.** Overlay the immutable `prepare` base or the reversed base with the frozen cut and any later safety state retained from the current R1 installation. In the same v4 transaction, reconcile R1's exact delivery de-duplication keys and their occurrence/reminder state with final attempts, accepted effects, receipts, cancellations, reservations, owner holds and local assignments that may fire. Retire a stale pending delivery; materialize the matching nonclaimable R1 row when a worker could regenerate it. Use a final receipt when supported, and R1's non-retrying `outcomeUnknown` or suppressed/cancelled state for uncertainty. Retain R2-only evidence in the protected archive. For a slot, route, device or disabled service without an enforceable R1 equivalent, require a verifiably disabled R1 route or block activation. Receipt 204 proves reply replay; verify the underlying receipt and delivery state separately. Test R1's real scheduler, enqueue de-duplication and worker.
- **Schedule and spending.** For both bases project current frozen-R1 job names, next-run/backoff, last result/maintenance, owner-local budget day and actual charged or uncertain same-day reservations. Include the current R1 generation on reinstall and deduplicate overlapping evidence. Check the day and zone against the destination configuration. An unprovable same-day count consumes the full effective daily allowance or inference remains disabled; no silent fresh-budget fallback. Missing, malformed or stale required outputs fail verification. The original R1 input inventory follows the presence and uncertainty rules of §8.14.1–2. Record the data that was projected, retained only as evidence or left unavailable. Post-`prepare` user work absent from the fallback remains separately reviewable as below.
- **Ordinary R1 retention.** The report gives the actual expiry and consequences for ordinary records. Administrative refusal guards follow the separate supported-clock rule above. A request's age alone does not make it terminal:
  - a capture retry is answered from its permanent capture key while the capture exists. For a deleted capture with an expired ordinary ledger record, a still-pending queue entry can recreate it; show the owner that limitation;
  - a task or idea PATCH retry meets R1's revision check;
  - an ordinary preferences PATCH can be dispatched again after its ordinary row expires; replay of a protected refusal stays blocked throughout the supported R1 clock range;
  - task creation is never queued by R1 (§1.6).

**The post-downgrade tool: `lisa archive`** (Core CLI and native macOS `lisa-archive` share these operations)
- **Runs in files mode:** it never starts Core, never runs a scheduler, and never opens an R2 database for writing. It keeps its own **reconcile store**, a small SQLite file in a working directory the owner names, opened under an exclusive lock. Ordinary proofs use a read-only SQLite snapshot. Direct writes are limited to the stopped-R1, owner-started maintenance session and the binding record in a locked rebind. Keep the reconcile store and its durable per-session send intents outside R1 directories and backup/restore copies. Flush an attempt before sending. If this external evidence disappears, forked send history remains unknown.
- **Bind to the selected active installation.** `inspect` always remains available, including on an unidentified copy. For reconciliation, check before opening a session and before each send or guarded mutation:
  1. Compare the database's native installation descriptor with the owner-protected installation record, the selected source and the authenticated rollback archive. Verify the projection-map digest for a reversed source. Check the canonical scope, expiry, codec and digest. Check that the local clock falls within the frozen qualified range before any send or native-row update. Verify owner-local budget day and time zone independently before restarting inference. An unknown, missing or conflicting anchor or installer receipt requires an explicit reviewed installation under §8.14.7 before this database can receive changes. Read-only inventory works meanwhile.
  2. Compare the native binding ID to the current owner-protected binding in the reconcile store. Each local lease of the tool names this installation and binding, the selected target and archive. The historical installation audit row can have expired normally. Match surviving continuity witnesses and re-read every mapping and completed effect used for an action. A missing required witness, changed target, missing session history, stale binding, contradictory data or lost effect proof stops that session's sends. The same mapping cannot silently follow a clone.
  3. Even if all identifiers match, re-check each action against the current record, deduplication key or route-specific ledger proof. An old matching snapshot only proves state in that image. A `sending` mark with missing terminal proof, a missing mapped create, or a missing external history that could have recorded an earlier send is indeterminate. A previously completed entry never becomes freely sendable because it disappeared. Require a new explicit owner decision on the current target before any ambiguous effect or dependent edit can proceed.
  4. **the listener serves this directory:** the owner endpoint the tool sends to is the one in `<dir>/run/owner-endpoint.json`, its PID is the holder recorded in `<dir>/lisa.lock`, the lock is held, and a `GET` of a record through the listener equals the same record in the snapshot.

  **Owner-run rebind after pruning or replacement.** With R1 stopped run `lisa archive rebind --r1-data-dir <dir> --installation <id>`, or the native Mac equivalent. Acquire the reconcile-store lock then R1’s actual directory lock in the same order used by maintenance. Validate the descriptor, protected receipt, selected directory/host, closed runtimes and matching cut in a WAL-aware snapshot. State what survives, what disappeared and which side effects may already have occurred. Show any safety-state regression (including missing refusal guards, known deliveries, later revocation or spending). A mismatch that cannot be reconciled from evidence blocks sends and activation through the helper. Use the preserved, fenced installer recovery path for a damaged or rolled-back safety projection before restarting R1. An owner can deliberately select the actual directory for a new session. That selection does not attest that the directory is the latest copy.
  - **Two durable records.** Save a pending rebind in the external store, keyed by a fresh operation ID, old observed binding, new binding, installation and the reviewed evidence digest. Flush it before the local transaction. With R1 still locked, compare-and-swap the native binding record to the new ID and operation reference in a single SQLite transaction. Complete the external record before ending the session. After a crash resume the same intent while holding both locks. Old binding plus matching pending intent permits retry after rechecking the review. New binding plus matching intent permits finalization. Any other combination, including a database restored behind the last completed binding, requires another review. No business action runs during rebind.
  - **Reprove each entry.** In the new binding preserve old mappings, decisions, send intents and revocations as history. Only positive present evidence under the route rules can establish an effect. A capture's native caller/request key or a valid 201 ledger answer plus the named task is usable to re-prove it. A matching value alone is satisfied with provenance unknown. Offer map to an actual row, skip, or explicitly submit as a new action with duplicate warning. Keep dependent entries held until the new mapping is proved or chosen. When the external reconcile history is missing, the owner can start a new store against the protected installation receipt, remount the authenticated archives and re-establish the trusted revocation evidence. Treat all prior attempts as unknown and block writes from origins whose eligibility cannot be established. If two snapshots are indistinguishable, the review offers only the same evidence-based choices on each. A successful rebind itself grants zero historical send approvals. With missing witnesses or indistinguishable copies, allow only independently safe current-state conditional outcomes. Missing history can never prove that an effect did not happen. A fresh owner authorization for an ambiguous submission must name the one entry, selected installation/binding, intended effect and duplicate risk. Consume that authorization durably once before the attempt.
- **Resumed work is revalidated, never trusted.** At the start of each run, and before any entry that depends on it is sent, each completion the tool relies on is read again by its R1 record ID: the record must exist with the recorded revision or a later one, and a later revision must still hold the completion's values or a change the log explains. A completion that fails — its record gone or its values replaced — stops the session ("Task T, which this reconciliation created at <time>, is no longer in Lisa R1"). Its dependent edits wait. Re-evaluate the create through the locked rebind and route rules. An unproved earlier send stays indeterminate. Dependent edits wait for a current proved or deliberately selected mapping, then take `expectedRevision` from the current target.
- **Presence is read, never assumed.** A mapping from an R2 entity to an R1 record is used only after the tool reads that record in the selected database and finds it with the projected ID and either the projected revision and value or a later R1 revision. A Core disposition in the rollback archive says what Core decided; it can't show that the installed database holds the result, because the `prepare` backup never does, and an R1 change after the downgrade may have deleted it.
- **Inputs:** the protected local installer receipt, the reconcile store (or the locked new-store review above), the rollback archive, device archives and the owner's key backup directory (current and retired export keys, signing-key history).
- **What each key does.** An archive's payload key is sealed to Core's export key (current Kx2, or a retired one, from the key backup) and to the device's own export key: Kx2 only **decrypts** it. Its contents are **authenticated** by the device's signature, checked against that device's key in a trusted roster — the rollback archive's Core-signed roster, verified through the signing-key history in the key backup. An entry that decrypts but doesn't authenticate is listed and never executed.
- **Logical entry identity,** fixed before anything is shown:
  - a signed operation: `(dataset, operationID, digest)`. The same operation in two archives — overlapping exports from one device, or a device export and the rollback archive — is one entry. The same ID with another digest is shown as an identity conflict, and neither copy is executed;
  - a draft: `(dataset, device, draftID, draftRevision)`; different revisions of one draft are shown together for the owner to choose between;
  - quarantined R1 bytes: `(dataset, device, queue id, requestID)`.
- **Eligibility, one rule** (`LisaArchive.eligibility`), used identically by `inspect`, `reconcile` and Core's `import-journal` (§8.6.6): an entry is eligible if its origin is active in the trusted roster and not revoked there or by `lisa archive revoke-device` (which the owner uses for a device lost or stolen since the rollback); or, if its origin is revoked, if it carries a Core receipt on the same lineage whose commit position precedes the revocation (the pre-revocation receipt exception, §8.5.4). A device-supplied time or the device's own older roster never establishes eligibility. Ineligible entries stay listed, and the owner can only re-enter their content deliberately as new owner actions.
- **`inspect`** lists every entry with its verification (dataset, signature, the key it was sealed to), its eligibility, its **rollback-cut disposition** (below), and the matching R1 record, read through R1's running owner listener with read-only GETs, or from the read-only database snapshot.
- **The rollback cut is consulted before the first send of any entry,** together with the selected database. The tool looks up the operation's ID among the rollback archive's dispositions:
  - **decided `accepted` with the same digest, the selected database `reversed (R)`, and its projected record read back as above:** Core applied it before the fence, and its effect is in the installed database, even if the device never received the receipt. The entry becomes `applied_before_rollback` and **nothing is sent**. The entity mapping is seeded from the verified projection, so a dependent offline edit goes to the existing R1 record;
  - **decided `accepted`, but the projected record isn't in the selected database** — the `prepare` backup is installed, or the record was deleted in R1 after the downgrade: the entry is a **reconciliation candidate**. A create is preserved for the route-specific review below. A missing record in a copy cannot authorize a resend; every entry that depends on it waits until the create's mapping exists, and is never sent to a guessed record. A create whose record was deleted in R1 after the downgrade is shown to the owner ("created before the rollback, deleted in Lisa R1 since"), never re-created automatically;
  - **decided `accepted_with_conflict`:** presence is established only for the candidate the projection map says was projected, and only once read back. An entry carrying another candidate's value is accepted by Core but **not present in R1**: it is shown with both values side by side and sent only if the owner chooses it, as a revision-checked change;
  - **decided, but only the decision survives** — Core compacted the body and no device or archive holds the exact bytes (§8.4.5): with the reversed database, presence is read back from the projection as above, and the entry is `applied_before_rollback` or "deleted in Lisa R1 since"; with the `prepare` backup, the original operation can't be sent, because its bytes and signature are gone. What is recoverable is the disposition and, for an accepted operation, its projected values: the tool shows them and offers "re-enter as a new change", which the owner confirms and the log records as the owner's re-entry, never as the original operation. A disposition without a projection (a rejection, or a value a later operation replaced) is listed as "decided by Lisa Core; content not held", with nothing to send;
  - **the same ID with another digest:** an identity conflict; neither copy is executed;
  - **decided `rejected`:** shown with Core's reason; re-entered only deliberately;
  - **absent:** Core never decided it; the entry is a reconciliation candidate.

  **With the `prepare` backup selected,** every operation Core accepted after `prepare` (from the rollback archive, together with device exports) is a candidate in dependency order: creates first, each dependent waiting for its create's mapping. Legacy children Core applied after `prepare` — the applied prefix of a settled preferences request among them — are candidates too, under their own route's rules below; the ledger record already stops a frozen retry from running the request again.
- **`reconcile`** asks the owner about each eligible candidate, in dependency order, and records every step in the store **before** acting:
  1. **Intent:** the entry, the attempt number, the R1 route, the exact body bytes, the R1 request ID derived from the entry identity and attempt (`lisa-rc-` followed by 26 base32 characters), what R1 showed before (record ID, revision and value, or "absent"), and the time. Committed before anything is sent.
  2. **Sending:** a `sending` mark, committed immediately before the first request byte.
  3. **Send** through R1's owner listener, after the binding checks above.
  4. **Completion:** R1's status and answer, the resulting R1 record and revision, the **entity mapping**, and a post-read of the record. An entry that edits something an earlier entry created uses the mapping, and waits while it is missing.
  5. **An intent without a completion** (R1 or the tool was killed, or the answer was lost) is resolved by its route's proof below **before any further attempt**, or shown as indeterminate.
- **Proof of the outcome, per route** (R1 facts, §1.6). R1 records every answer in 2xx, 400, 403, 404, 409 and 413 under the request's scoped key and fingerprint (`Sources/LisaAPI/APIRouter.swift`, `Sources/LisaApplication/RequestLedger.swift`), so **a matching record proves only that R1 answered; its status and route-specific body say what the answer was**:

  | Change | R1 route | Outcome after a lost answer |
  |---|---|---|
  | Create an idea | `POST /v1/captures` with kind `idea`, R1's only way to create one; then `PATCH /v1/ideas/{id}` for its other fields, through the mapping | **Within a proved current session,** R1 deduplicates captures permanently by caller and request ID and never prunes or (in production) deletes them, so resending the same bytes under the same request ID returns the existing capture with its idea, or creates it once in that database. An older copy can lack the capture or later owner action. After a continuity break, read a present native capture key or keep the earlier attempt indeterminate and require a new scoped owner decision |
  | Capture text or a link | `POST /v1/captures` | The same |
  | Create a task | `POST /v1/tasks` | **Not sent** (the continuously held, current reconcile history proves the intent never advanced to `sending`): send once with the owner's existing approval. Missing history or a rebound session cannot supply this proof. **Applied** only if a read-only snapshot of the selected database holds R1's ledger record for `owner:<request ID>` with the intent's fingerprint, **status 201 and a `TaskDTO`** naming the task, which then exists by that ID; the mapping is recorded. A retained ledger answer can have been inherited by a copy. It proves the named state in the selected database after readback, irrespective of physical writer identity. A matching record with a 4xx status is **refused by R1**, nothing created; the refusal is shown. **Anything else is indeterminate:** R1 writes the record after its commit and prunes it after its retention, and no other R1 record identifies the request, so neither a time window nor a listing proves the create didn't happen. The owner sees every task in the same snapshot whose content equals the intent's (title, notes, priority, deadline, estimate, splittable), with R1's creation times labeled as R1's clock, and chooses: use one (the mapping is recorded), send again as a new attempt (warned that it may duplicate), or skip |
  | Field edit or status change of a task or idea | `PATCH` with the recorded `expectedRevision`, which R1 checks for equality before changing anything (`Sources/LisaAPI/WorkHandlers.swift`) — a real conditional mutation | Read the record. With intact binding, an unchanged revision permits the original conditional attempt on this target. With lost history that revision cannot prove nonexecution elsewhere; show the current result or require a new scoped owner decision before a write. On an authorized resend use the same request ID, which R1 either answers from its record or runs once. **Applied** only if the snapshot holds R1's ledger record for `owner:<request ID>` with the intent's fingerprint, **status 200, and a DTO at the expected next revision carrying the intended values**. A matching record with **409** means R1 refused it because the record had changed: **not applied by this request**, whatever the record holds now; the same request ID would only replay that 409, so nothing is resent. Then, and for a moved revision with no ledger record: every intended value present → **satisfied, provenance unknown** ("already has these values; whether this change or another set them is unknown"), nothing sent; anything else (a partial correction, or someone else's change) → indeterminate, with the values side by side, and only a new attempt with a fresh read and a new request ID, after the owner chooses it |
  | Preference set, confirm or delete | `PATCH /v1/preferences` for an absent key only; otherwise the maintenance session | The rules below |

- **Preferences: what protects the intended record at the instant of mutation.** A pre-read and a post-read can't protect anything by themselves: a value replaced between the pre-read and the mutation is destroyed, and the post-read can't tell. Each kind is therefore applied only through a mechanism that checks at the commit, or held for the owner. Each change is first compared with the key's record at the rollback cut (record ID, revision, value, origin, confirmed), when the selected database is `reversed (R)`, and with R1's current record.

  | Kind | What R1 offers (`Sources/LisaOwnerData/PreferenceService.swift`, `Sources/LisaAPI/SettingsHandlers.swift`) | How the tool applies it |
  |---|---|---|
  | **Set** to v, key present (record ID p, revision r) | `set` checks `expectedRevision` **only while a record exists**, and compares revisions, never record IDs; IDs are random (`Sources/LisaDomain/Preference.swift`). So a record deleted after the tool's read is inserted again — undoing the deletion — and a replacement record that reached revision r is overwritten | **Never through R1's API.** Applied only in the maintenance session, where one transaction checks record ID p, revision r, value, origin and confirmed before it writes; otherwise held for the owner |
  | **Set** to v, key absent | Revisions start at 1 (`Sources/LisaDomain/Revision.swift`), and R1 passes `expectedRevision` through without a range check, so `expectedRevision: 0` makes R1 insert only while no record exists and refuse with 409 otherwise — a real conditional insert, inside R1's own transaction | Send with `expectedRevision: 0`. A key created in between is refused, never overwritten; a 409 under the matching fingerprint is that refusal |
  | **Confirm** of inferred v | `confirm` takes no revision and confirms whatever record holds the key | Never sent through R1's API. Applied only in the maintenance session; otherwise held for the owner |
  | **Delete** | Deletion takes no revision | Never sent through R1's API. Applied only in the maintenance session; otherwise held for the owner |

  Further rules for each kind:
  - **Set:** present at the cut and absent now (deleted after the downgrade) → indeterminate, because inserting would recreate what someone deleted; another record ID (deleted and recreated) → indeterminate; the current record already holds v, explicit and confirmed → **satisfied, provenance unknown**: nothing is sent, and the log says "already had this value; who set it is unknown", never "applied".
  - **Confirm:** the current record already confirmed with v → satisfied, provenance unknown; another value, another record ID, or absent → indeterminate, or nothing to confirm.
  - **Delete:** absent → satisfied, provenance unknown ("already absent; who deleted it is unknown"); recreated or changed → indeterminate. A delete is applied only as the owner's own change, after the owner confirms it, because R1 refuses deletes from devices.
- **The maintenance session** (`lisa archive reconcile --maintenance --r1-data-dir <dir>`): R1's own exclusive single-writer mechanism. R1 lets exactly one runtime own a data directory, through an exclusive `flock` on `<data>/lisa.lock` that every R1 runtime holds for its lifetime and that R1's own `restore` and `doctor` take (`Sources/LisaRuntime/DataDirectoryLock.swift`, `Sources/LisaHost/HostMaintenance.swift`, `Sources/LisaCLI/DoctorCommand.swift`).
  1. The owner quits Lisa R1. The tool takes R1's lock without waiting; if R1 still runs, the lock is held and the session refuses. While the tool holds it, any R1 start exits with R1's "data directory in use" (exit code 4), so nothing else writes. The binding checks above run under the lock (the listener check is replaced by the lock itself).
  2. Each held set, confirm or delete runs in one SQLite `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction on R1's database: read the key's record; continue only if it equals the expected record (record ID, revision, value, origin, confirmed) — for a set, the "before" its intent recorded; apply exactly R1's mutation — for a set, the value, origin `explicit`, `confirmed`, `updatedAt`, the revision advanced and R1's `preference.set` audit event with R1's detail (`consequential` or `ordinary`); for a confirm, `confirmed` set, `updatedAt`, the revision advanced and R1's `preference.confirmed` audit event; for a delete, the record removed and R1's `data.deleted` audit event (`Sources/LisaOwnerData/DeletionService.swift`); read it back; commit. Any difference rolls the transaction back and leaves the entry indeterminate, with what the session found.
  3. The session releases the lock. R1 reads its settings fresh when it starts and runs its start-up reminder sync and planning, which stand in for the follow-up work its API would have run in-process.

  That these writes equal R1's own is checked at M9 by a differential fixture test against the frozen R1 services (a temporary harness built from `491c90a`): the same set, confirm and delete through R1's API and through the session must leave identical rows, audit events and post-start state.
- **Without the maintenance session** (R1 running, or the owner declining), sets of a present key, confirms and deletes stay **held for the owner's review**, never issued: "Lisa R1 can't apply this change safely while it runs. Quit Lisa R1 and run the maintenance session, or make the change yourself in Lisa R1." The owner's own change in R1 is the owner's decision; the tool then reports the key's state as satisfied, provenance unknown, or indeterminate.
- **The owner-visible log:** `lisa archive log` prints every decision, intent, R1 answer, proof, mapping, binding check and revalidation, with times. A rerun of `reconcile` skips completed entries whose revalidation passes and resolves open intents through the proofs above.

**Recovery facts (rollback and reconciliation).** *Survives:* the rollback archive (dispositions with their retained-evidence classes, legacy parents and decisions, projection map and both database identities), the protected installation receipt, both native records and any remaining audit witnesses, the kept fenced file, device archives, the key backup, and the reconcile store with its identity, installation ID and continuity point. *Who acts:* the owner settles legacy holds, chooses and installs one database, approves every entry, maps indeterminate creates, runs the maintenance session or makes held preference changes in R1, and records lost devices; the tool proves outcomes from the installed database's own records. *Safe after an interruption:* rerun `--settle-legacy`, `--install --stage` or `--install --switch`, or the pending locked `rebind` or `reconcile` step; R1's file is always either the fenced database or the complete installed one; an intent is never resent without its route's proof; a maintenance transaction that didn't commit changed nothing; a session that finds another installation starts over without reusing a mapping. *What the owner sees when the outcome is unknown:* "Entry 421 (create task 'Call the plumber'): R1 may have created it; R1's record of this request is gone or was never written. One task with exactly this content exists (task <id>, created <time by R1's clock>). Use this task, send again (may duplicate), or skip." With the `prepare` backup installed: "This Lisa R1 database is the backup from before the upgrade. Task T3, created by your iPad after the upgrade, isn't in it: send it (its two later edits wait for it)."

**Acceptance (M9; S-02, S-03, S-13; narrative §19.2).** Each case below states the expected R1 database state and what the owner sees.
