- a save after acknowledgement; a device that never acknowledges (listed, never read as clean); an acknowledgement that counts a quarantined item and a draft resolution, which isn't clean until the export covers them; **the rollback report naming only acknowledged and reported positions**, with the iPhone's later saves absent from it;
- a sandboxed Mac export outside its container (X3, X7); at M9/X7 build the Mac helper without the full R2 service, switch the active R1 directory, edit a record in R1, then recover a newly arrived protected export including a guarded preference change through the native helper. Prove checks use the live local endpoint and maintenance lock and preserve the newer R1 edit;
- loss of the originating installation; an export-key rotation before the export;
- an interrupted reverse migration resumed with no partial state; a 3 MiB migrated note returned byte for byte;
- provider acceptance across the cutover: accepted before stays accepted, and accepted by R2 arrives as accepted;
- conflicting values that v4 can't hold, reported with owner text kept;
- the frozen R1 `run-once` on the copy sending nothing again;
- **Seeded frozen caller qualification (M9/X7):** on independent candidates made from synthetic histories, drive the frozen R1 CLI, typed client and queue with the known original bytes for a deleted capture, a task created and deleted, a PATCH with expired reply for a surviving task, a preferences reply formerly containing a deleted key, a parent R2 migrated without learning its route, and an error canary. Check expected native replies, decode results, effects and privacy. Run frozen workers against synthetic endpoints. Keep these release fixture results separate from each actual candidate’s local pass under §8.14.7 step 7.
- **settlement before the R1 ledger** (S-03 rollback part): at `--fence`, a preferences request from the frozen R1 CLI on the loopback owner listener, submitted after `prepare`, waits at an administrative hold (change 2) with change 1 applied and changes 3–4 behind it, and a receipts request stopped after 2 of 5 receipts. `--reverse` and `--install` will refuse while either is open. `--settle-legacy refuse-remaining` is killed after its first transaction and run again: the hold's refusal will be recorded once, change 1 will stay applied, changes 3–4 will be `not_reached`, the receipts will be completed from the third, and each will be recorded once;
- **a frozen retry after the refusal, on both rollback paths** (S-13): the same preferences request's frozen queue entry is retried against R1 after (a) the reversed database and (b) the `prepare` backup was installed. In both, R1 will answer from the installed ledger record with R1's 403, which the frozen queue marks failed with "This setting can be changed only on the administrative terminal."; R1's preference rows will be unchanged by the retry — change 2 and changes 3–4 never applied — and the receipts retry will get 204 and be marked done. In (a), change 1 will be present in R1 (projected). In (b), **the backup lacks change 1, which the final R2 cut holds:** R1's row for that key will still hold its `prepare`-time value; `lisa archive inspect` will list change 1 as "applied by Lisa R2 after the backup now installed; not in Lisa R1", and, being a set of a present key, it will be held until the owner runs the maintenance session, where its record (ID, revision and value as at `prepare`) is checked and the set applied with R1's mutation and audit event;
- **expiry, scope and first startup:** on each base, a genuine frozen owner-scoped queue receives 503, goes offline without `uncertainSince`, then retries after 31 and 366 days in independent runs after real R1 ledger pruning. Separately settle a refusal more than 30 days before rollback so its ordinary body has already expired, and replay it via the owner listener. Require native 403, unchanged prefix/no suffix, no new effect audit, native mismatch for changed bytes and normal handling of a new request ID. Test an already-revoked device still active in `prepare`, a receipt completed by settlement, an R2-only accepted attempt, a locally held slot, stale schedule and same-day exhausted budget. On first R1 start and after a restart prove bearer rejection, no push, nonclaimable duplicate keys, zero synthetic provider submissions for those slots, preserved job schedule and zero new model dispatch on exhausted budget;
- **a three-file switch and reinstall** (S-13): for both bases and I1→I2, interrupt before/after the live re-fence, after each sidecar replacement, before/after the final rename, and before the journal's final record. Attempt a normal frozen R1 start at each boundary. It sees the unchanged complete old generation before the fence, refuses while fenced, or sees only the complete new generation. Exercise non-empty WAL, failed checkpoint, wrong/omitted file, mutated staged input, missing/stale verification receipt, changed day/zone, insufficient disk and new writes after publication but before journal recovery. Resumption must preserve genuine newer R1 writes, logs and safety evidence. Validate the archived prior generation independently; observe zero duplicate provider submissions and no additional model reservation beyond the effective cap;
- **reinstall at the same path** (S-02, S-13): I1 contains a reconciled create and an independent later owner edit, new revocation, delivered reminder and budget charge. An unattended reinstall must stop before discarding the edit. After explicitly resolving its disposition on a preserved test copy, install I2. Reject I1's mappings, establish fresh mappings from I2's actual records and keep the new revocation, spent budget and terminal delivery enforced. An unmarked manual copy stays inspect-only; same-ID native restore with missing receipt/audit continuity stops dependent sends;
- **Audit pruning and late export (S-02/S-13, M9 and X7):** on each base use one-day audit retention. After a handled create, remove its effect in R1, take a later genuine edit, run actual maintenance past cutoff and submit an authenticated late export. Original audit rows can be gone. Under the local lock rebind the genuine directory, recover an independently provable action, leave the missing create indeterminate and its dependant held. Repeat on an earlier copy with the same installation anchor, on a byte-identical copy of a previously examined state, and after losing the external session log. No stale approved send or mapping transfers. Repeat with a missing/mismatched installation anchor or receipt, and interrupt rebind before/after its SQLite commit and external completion. Check the native markers survive pruning and R1 backup/restore, and cannot be addressed through a real caller scope.
- **Real candidate with optional inputs (S-13):** prepare a healthy deterministic-only R1 database before its first cycle, model reservation or dry-run capture. Verify tagged absences, a next-run schedule derived safely, a justified zero daily count (then a second fixture with prior or uncertain charges), and complete valid destination sidecars. Try truncated JSON, an unreadable present file and a missing sealed output. None can pass as a pristine zero-budget input. Include a minimal candidate and one containing only outcome-only legacy metadata. The candidate verifier must produce a meaningful pass without retained request preimages. Seed a missing safety row and a wrong budget to prove that the per-install pass fails.
- **the R1 listener serving another directory:** a second R1 instance started against another data directory, its endpoint swapped into the session: the binding check will find its PID isn't the lock holder of the named directory and refuse to send;
- **reconciliation (S-02):**
  - **the rollback cut first:** the iPad's create of task T3 was committed by Core before the fence, and its receipt was lost; its offline title edit of T3 was never sent. Both arrive in the iPad's export. The create will be marked `applied_before_rollback` and never sent; the mapping from T3 to its R1 record will be seeded from the projection map; the edit will go to that record with its revision; R1 will hold one T3, with the new title;
  - **overlapping exports:** two iPhone archives holding the same operations, and an iPad archive holding an operation also present in the rollback archive: each reconciled or skipped once, with every archive named in the log;
  - **a task create with both processes killed after R1's commit,** in four variants: R1's ledger record written with 201 (applied; mapping recorded; no second task); R1 killed before writing it (indeterminate; the snapshot's equal-content candidate shown; the owner maps it); **R1's clock set back one day** between the attempt and the proof (the same results, because no rule reads a clock); **past R1's retention**, with the record pruned (indeterminate, never resent automatically). A variant killed before the `sending` mark sends once. A variant with 120 tasks in R1 (three API pages) shows that candidates come from one snapshot, never from pages;
  - **a lost 409:** the tool's title PATCH of T2 reaches R1 after the owner edited T2 in R1; R1 answers 409 and records it under the tool's fingerprint; the answer is lost. On rerun the proof will find the matching record with status 409: not applied — R1 holds the owner's title, unchanged by the tool — and the entry will be indeterminate with both titles shown, or satisfied, provenance unknown, where the owner's title equals the intended one; nothing is resent under that request ID;
  - **a restart between intent and completion,** for every route;
  - **preferences, set against a present key** (each with R1's database state and the owner's view): with R1 running, the set is held and nothing is sent ("Quit Lisa R1 and run the maintenance session …"); in the maintenance session, (i) the record unchanged → applied: value v, revision r+1, the same record ID, R1's `preference.set` audit event; "Applied while Lisa R1 was stopped (record unchanged)"; (ii) **deleted between the tool's read and the set** → nothing inserted, the key stays absent; indeterminate: "This setting was deleted in Lisa R1 after the rollback; Lisa didn't recreate it"; (iii) **deleted and recreated, the new record edited up to the same revision r** → the record ID differs, nothing written, the replacement kept; indeterminate, both values shown; (iv) the value changed in R1 (revision moved) → nothing written; indeterminate; (v) the value already v → satisfied, provenance unknown. In every case a start of R1 during the session exits with "data directory in use";
  - **preferences, set against an absent key:** `expectedRevision: 0` while another client inserts the key in between (R1's 409; the inserted value kept; indeterminate); with nothing inserted (created once);
  - **confirm and delete, never unprotected:** with R1 running, a confirm and a delete stay held for the owner and nothing is sent; in the maintenance session, a confirm and a delete whose records match are applied, and a delete whose value was replaced after the tool's last read (by an R1 run before the session) is rolled back and left indeterminate, the replacement kept; the differential test shows the session's rows, audit events and post-start state equal to R1's own for set, confirm and delete; a delete of an already absent key is satisfied, provenance unknown;
  - **an idea created through R1's capture path,** with the answer lost: the resend returns the existing capture and idea, and the dependent tag edit uses the mapping;
  - **the revision-checked title edit:** unchanged revision → resent; moved with the value present and R1's 200 ledger record of the tool's request found → applied; moved with the value present and no such record (the owner had typed the same title in R1) → satisfied, provenance unknown, nothing sent; moved otherwise → indeterminate;
  - **the same work against both databases** (S-13): one post-`prepare` create of task T5 by the iPhone, its dependent offline title edit, and two overlapping iPhone exports holding both. Run 1, the reversed database installed: T5's projected record read back, the create `applied_before_rollback`, the edit sent to that record once. Run 2, the `prepare` backup installed instead: the identity `prepare backup`, a new installation ID; T5's create preserved and sent once; the edit waiting until T5's mapping exists, then sent to the new record; no mapping from run 1 used. In both runs each overlapping entry is executed at most once;
  - **decision-only entries:** an accepted iPhone edit whose body Core compacted and no export holds: with the reversed database, `applied_before_rollback` from its read-back projection; with the `prepare` backup, listed with its projected value and offered only as "re-enter as a new change", which the owner confirms and the log records as the owner's re-entry; a rejected decision-only entry listed as "decided by Lisa Core; content not held";
  - **`accepted_with_conflict` with one candidate projected:** the iPhone's title A and the iPad's title B were both accepted with a conflict, and reverse migration projected A. The iPhone's entry is `applied_before_rollback` after A is read back; the iPad's entry is shown as "accepted by Lisa Core, not in Lisa R1" with both values, and is sent, revision-checked, only if the owner chooses B;
  - **a projected record deleted in R1 after the downgrade:** the create is shown as "deleted in Lisa R1 since", never re-created automatically, and its dependent edit isn't sent;
  - **one eligibility rule everywhere:** an operation from a device revoked with `revoke-device`, carrying an early creation time, is ineligible in `inspect`, `reconcile` and `import-journal` alike; one with a same-lineage pre-revocation receipt is eligible in all three; an ineligible entry is re-entered deliberately as a new owner action;
  - **keys:** an archive that decrypts with Kx2 but whose signature doesn't verify against the trusted roster is listed and never executed;
  - for each case, the resulting R1 rows and IDs, and the log, checked;
- recovery after R1 resumes, using only the rollback archive, the device exports and the documented key backup in a fresh archive-work directory on the owner-designated R1 host. The installation retains its protected receipt. Missing external history requires rebind and the uncertainty treatment above.

### 8.15 Limits, SQLite capacity and performance (R2-LIMIT-01–03; protocol limits; SQLite capacity and latency)

#### 8.15.1 Defaults (R2-LIMIT-01), each configurable and checked

| Resource | Default |
|---|---|
| Enrolled devices | 10 |
| Operation body (canonical, metadata included) | 64 KiB (§8.3.3) |
| Operation batch | 100 operations and 512 KiB of signed bytes, whichever comes first |
| Encoded HTTP request body | 768 KiB (§8.16.2) |
| Nearby frame | 1 MiB total |
| Change page, snapshot chunk | 512 KiB |
| Content blob | 1 MiB, in 256 KiB chunks, committed inside SQLite; blob lists only for notes over 1 MiB that came from R1, and edits of them (§8.3.3); 256 MiB of staged blobs per replica; orphans expire after 7 days |
| Paged artifacts | Causal-summary pages 256 KiB; manifest pages 4,096 entries; roster pages 64 KiB; all Merkle-bound (§8.1.3) |
| Dependencies | 32 per operation; 1,000 waiting per origin incarnation; 64 MiB of waiting bodies; per transaction at most 256 edges expanded, 256 entries enqueued and 256 released; cycle walks of 1,024 nodes per step (§8.2.2) |
| Incremental history | 90 days |
| Waiting operations | 14 days |
| Local notifications | 48 hours ahead, 32 requests (§8.10.5) |
| Inference | One in flight, ten a day plus the rolling window, one counted repair (§8.9.2) |
| Legacy children | At most one dependency each (the immediate same-entity predecessor); up to 50 per preferences request, as R1 allows (§8.12.2) |
| Trust bundles | 64 roster pages, 16 key transitions and 4 MiB per bundle; 16 bundles per recovery; three outstanding evidence codes, each valid for 10 minutes (§8.5.3) |
| Provider connectors | R1's message-size limits; calendar windows as R2 specifies (§8.19.2); Graph at most 4 concurrent requests per mailbox; backoff honoring `Retry-After` |
| Retries | Finite per attempt cycle, with persisted next-attempt times |

#### 8.15.2 SQLite capacity: readers, WAL and storage exhaustion

**Required:** storage exhaustion never reports a successful unsaved capture (R2-L33); unsynchronized captures, pending operations, conflicts, uncertain deliveries and replay metadata are never evicted (R2-LIMIT-02).

- **WAL in practice:** `journal_size_limit` only truncates the WAL file after a checkpoint resets it; it does not cap a live WAL. The live WAL grows while any reader holds an old snapshot. It is bounded here by bounding readers and checkpointing:
  - `wal_autocheckpoint` stays on; a passive checkpoint runs after each batch install, and a truncating one after a snapshot swap.
  - Every long reader belongs to Lisa: snapshot generation on Core, staging catch-up passes and export assembly on clients, search queries. Each runs on a read connection registered with a **reader monitor** that knows its start time and purpose.
- **Release before the reserve is threatened.** The store keeps a capture reserve (64 MiB on clients, configurable on Core). The monitor samples free space (`statfs`) and the WAL size every second while readers are active, and acts when either of these holds:
  - free space falls below the reserve plus twice the WAL size;
  - a reader has been open longer than its budget (search 5 s; catch-up pass 2 s; export 60 s; snapshot generation 10 minutes).

  It then interrupts the reader (`sqlite3_interrupt` on its connection), finalizes its statements, ends the read transaction, and runs a truncating checkpoint. The interrupted work is retried later; for snapshot generation, from a new cut.
- **Exhaustion:** `SQLITE_FULL` (or an `ENOSPC` from staging files) aborts only that write's transaction. A capture reports "Not saved: storage is full" with the action needed. Earlier saves are intact, and the replica stays openable.
- **Truthful under bursts faster than the sample.** The one-second sample only decides when to release readers; it never decides whether a save succeeded.
  - Every save's outcome is its own commit result, so a burst that fills the disk between samples still ends in "Not saved" for the write that failed, never in a false "Saved".
  - Writes other than captures (staging, blob promotion, snapshot generation, release steps) read free space with a fresh `statfs` at the start of each bounded transaction and stop before the reserve.
  - **A ballast file** the size of the capture reserve (`reserve.bin` in the replica directory, excluded from backups like the rest) is released only by the capture path: on `SQLITE_FULL` the capture truncates the ballast and retries once. If that succeeds, the capture is saved and the app says "Storage is almost full"; the ballast is restored when space returns. If it fails, the capture reports "Not saved".
- **Measured together** (M2, clients; M3, Core): staged tables, staged indexes, the live WAL and the database file at their peak during an installation, against a reserve sized from those numbers.

**Acceptance (S-17)**
- **M2:** the first real use of the rootless engine (§9) runs the replica harness inside a `swift:6.4.2-trixie` container with the database directory on a `--tmpfs …:size=…` mount, so the kernel itself returns `ENOSPC`. Checks:
  - a pinned reader (a long search) during continuous captures: WAL growth measured, the reader interrupted at its budget or at the space threshold, then the WAL truncated;
  - storage filling during a staged installation: the capture before the reserve saved, the capture that doesn't fit reported unsaved, and the database consistent after restart;
  - **a burst that fills the tmpfs between two samples:** every capture reported "Saved" is present after a restart, every failed one reported "Not saved", and the ballast lets one capture through after other writers filled the disk;
  - `max_page_count` for the in-database case on the Fedora host.
- **M3:** the same on Core's snapshot generation and intake.

The engine, context, architecture and image digests of that first run are recorded (O-10).

#### 8.15.3 Performance (R2-LIMIT-03)

- **Data:** synthetic, with 10,000 searchable records, 100,000 historical operations, three clients and recurring calendars.
- **Targets:** capture p95 within 500 ms; search p95 within 1 second; 100 small operations converging within 5 seconds after an authenticated foreground connection on a healthy link; normal reads never waiting for Core or a model.
- **Capture p95 is overall.** It includes captures that waited on a snapshot swap or a release batch, broken down by cause.
- **Reporting, in separate classes:**
  - **Fedora host** (M2, M9): hardware and method recorded.
  - **Simulated link** (M6): labeled simulated.
  - **Physical devices at X7:** 100 small operations timed over an already authenticated, healthy foreground link between named devices, against the 5-second target. Hardware, OS versions, payload size, dataset size and the actual result are recorded.

### 8.16 Transfer sizes, encoding and streaming (R2-LIMIT-01; protocol limits)

#### 8.16.1 Encoding

- **Signed bodies:** one canonical JSON form. UTF-8 (invalid UTF-8 refused), object keys sorted by Unicode scalar value, no whitespace, integers only (instants in milliseconds, decimals as strings), minimal escaping, no duplicate keys, nesting depth at most 16.
- **Signatures:** raw 64-byte P-256 values over a domain-separated prefix plus the body. Nothing re-encodes a signed body.
- **Over HTTP:** signed bodies travel as base64url inside JSON envelopes.
- **In nearby frames:** raw bytes.

#### 8.16.2 The four budgets, checked separately

| Layer | Unit | Limit | Where checked |
|---|---|---|---|
| Semantic | Canonical operation body, including metadata | 64 KiB | At creation (client) and intake (Core), before decoding the payload |
| Semantic | Batch | ≤ 100 operations and ≤ 512 KiB of bodies plus signatures | Client splits; Core checks |
| HTTP | Encoded request body (JSON envelope with base64url bodies) | 768 KiB, which is ≥ 512 KiB × 4/3 plus 64 KiB of envelope | Server, on the bytes actually received |
| Frame | Nearby frame (8-byte header and payload) | 1 MiB total | From the header, before allocation |

- **Streamed enforcement.** The server counts received body bytes whether or not `Content-Length` is present.
  - With `Content-Length` over the limit, it refuses before reading.
  - Without it (chunked), it reads into a bounded buffer and stops at limit + 1, answering 413 (`payloadTooLarge` for v1, oversized for v2) and closing the connection.
  - A body shorter or longer than its declared `Content-Length` is a framing error.
- **Responses** stream change pages, chunks and blobs without assembling them in memory.
- **UTF-8 and escaping:** limits count encoded bytes, so multi-byte characters and escaped control characters count at their encoded size (§8.3.3).

#### 8.16.3 Blobs and staging

- **Blob-backed values:** a capture text, note or description over its value limit becomes a blob of at most 1 MiB (a migrated note over 1 MiB, a blob list, §8.3.3). The operation carries its digest and length, so the signature covers the content. The operation waits, as a dependency, until the blob is committed.
- **Where committed blobs live: inside SQLite,** on Core and on every replica, in a `blob_chunks` table of 256 KiB rows keyed by blob digest and chunk index. A blob is therefore committed, backed up and restored with the records that reference it, and no file can be orphaned or half-moved.
- **Staging and promotion:**
  - Incoming chunks of 256 KiB are verified by digest and written to staging files, with progress recorded per blob; interrupted staging resumes from the last verified chunk.
  - **Promotion** is its own transaction: once every chunk is verified, the blob's chunks are inserted from the staging file (at most 1 MiB, so the transaction and its WAL growth stay bounded) with reference count zero and a 7-day orphan deadline. The staging file is deleted after the commit; a crash in between leaves a stale staging file that the next start removes, because the committed blob is already complete.
  - **Commit order:** promotion first, then the operation's transaction, which increments the reference count. An operation never references a blob that isn't committed.
  - A committed blob still unreferenced after 7 days expires (an orphan). Staging is bounded at 256 MiB per replica; beyond it, `insufficient_storage` for new blobs.
  - **Backups** need nothing extra: SQLite's online backup copies the blob rows with the records at the same cut (§8.4.4).
- **Core-wide bounds:** storage for blobs, waiting operations and staging is bounded per dataset. Exceeding a bound gives backpressure (`insufficient_storage`) and a health warning, never silent loss.

**What fits where:** every signed unit (operation, batch, roster page, receipt, change page) fits one frame and one HTTP message. Snapshots, artifacts and blobs are paged or chunked, and checked piece by piece. Attachments are still not replicated (R2-DATA-03).

**Flow control:** chunks are 256–512 KiB, within TN3213's advice of at least 64 KiB. Each send waits for buffer space, and each receive is consumed before the next read.

**Acceptance (M1 codec, M3 HTTP, M6 frames; S-16):** the checks will cover:
- exact boundaries: a 64 KiB body accepted and one byte more refused; 100 operations accepted and 101 refused; a 512 KiB batch accepted and one byte more refused; a 768 KiB HTTP body accepted and one byte more refused;
- the same over chunked transfer without `Content-Length`, refused at the byte count and with memory measured;
- a 1 MiB frame accepted, and one byte more closing the session;
- a batch of maximum-size operations whose content needs multi-byte UTF-8 and escaped control characters, with base64 and JSON overhead measured;
- a blob interrupted mid-staging and resumed; a kill between promotion and the referencing operation (the blob stays committed and unreferenced until it is referenced or expires), and between the promotion commit and the staging file's deletion;
- an orphan expiring;
- staging at its bound.

### 8.17 Data handling: provenance, allowlists, retention and locked behavior (privacy and retention)

- **Provenance:** every record carries its origin (source, capture, device, Core or migration), its authority (owner, model, source, system or ambiguous), source times, last retrieval or sync, revisions, interpretation status, and whether it is canonical, local or provisional.
- **Replication allowlist:**
  - **Clients receive:**
    - owner entities: tasks, ideas, captures, permitted preferences, owner-visible proposal fields, owner reminders, suggestions;
    - derived records: calendar occurrences in the window, briefs for 30 days, plans, bounded evidence excerpts, news cited by briefs or captures, source freshness;
    - trust and coordination: the public roster and artifacts, lineage, the backup catalogue, their own assignments, the trust bundle.
  - **Never replicated:** secrets and secret references, provider tokens and the token store; connector configuration and cursors; raw content and full email bodies; deliveries, the submission journal and audit; v1 parents (with R1 fingerprints) and idempotency records; the bodies of restricted legacy siblings, of which only redacted receipts replicate (§8.12.2); quarantined trust bundles; other devices' credentials or push tokens; Core's model settings.
- **Export allowlist:**
  - Core's owner export: owner entities, derived records, operation metadata, and waiting, rejected and quarantined work. Never secrets, verifiers or tokens.
  - Client archive: unsynchronized work only (§8.6.6).
- **Core retention:**
  - raw content 90 days with a 500 MB cap; delivery history 180 days; audit 365 days; unreferenced feed items 30 days — R1's defaults, §1.6;
  - change-log history 90 days;
  - operation metadata, tombstones (with request keys) and compact v1 parents for the life of the dataset;
  - operation bodies per §8.4.5; v1 response bodies per §8.12.2;
  - backups per the retained set R and owner pins;
  - the recovery ledger for the life of the dataset.
- **Client retention:** the working set; the journal per §8.6.4; blobs by reference count; staging dropped after installation or invalidation; bounded logs.
- **Deletion:** §8.6.7.
- **Locked devices:**
  - The replica and Keychain items are available after first unlock.
  - Before the first unlock after a restart, nothing is saved (§8.6.2).
  - Notification actions and Siri work while locked after first unlock; completing a task requires unlocking.
  - Background refresh may run locked; nearby runs only in the foreground.
  - Secure Enclave keys are usable after first unlock.
- **Diagnostics and logs:** IDs, counters, hashes and states only, with secrets and private content redacted (R2-API-04, CONSTRICTION).

### 8.18 Compatibility, fixtures, reproducibility and live-service limits (verification and maintainability)

- **Old clients:**
  - R1 companions use v1 through the translation layer until upgraded (§8.12.2, §8.14.5). Their actual behavior and presentation are documented from R1's code.
  - The R1 Mac app is replaced, not kept running (§8.14.4).
  - Legacy devices are never roster members.
  - An R2 client meeting an unknown required feature gets `upgrade_required`.
- **Frozen R1 artifacts, reproducible from a fresh checkout** (M0):
  1. Reconstruct R1's sources with `git show 491c90a:<path>`, walking the tree from `git show 491c90a:` recursively, into `.tmp/r2/<M0 run>/r1-src/`. This is permitted Git use (§3). A `git archive` or a checkout of another commit is not.
  2. Build there with Swift 6.3.2: R1's `lisa` (debug and release), plus a small frozen-client harness linking R1's `LisaClient` and offline queue.
  3. Generate synthetic R1 datasets and queue files with that build: pending, uncertain and dry-run deliveries; every queue state of §8.14.3; schedules and budgets.
  4. Record the SHA-256 of every source file, binary and fixture, and the commands, in `docs/VERIFICATION-SCENARIOS.md`.

  A later run can rebuild the same artifacts from the same commit. An R1 Apple build is the owner's step at X3 from the same tree (§8.14.4).
- **Scenario records:** each S-nn in `docs/VERIFICATION-SCENARIOS.md` names its commands, inputs, tool versions (Swift, SQLite, OpenSSL, engine), input hashes, expected outcomes and evidence location (the run directory under `.tmp/r2/`). Results are recorded only when a run produced them.
- **Live-service limits (owner decisions, 2026-11-22):** Claude never contacts a live mailbox, calendar, X, Discord, model provider, SMTP server, Twilio or APNs, and never uses the owner's Apple, Microsoft, Google, iCloud, Proton, OpenAI, Anthropic, Ollama or Twilio accounts, credentials, existing sessions or connected-account tools. Every credentialed or real-service check is the owner's, run under the owner's identity with the owner-verification harness (§10.8); until the owner runs it, it waits on the owner's checklist with its actual cause (§10.1). Claude verifies the production code against deterministic fixtures and controlled servers in the authorized rootless environment, including a controlled local Ollama server, which exercises Ollama's real protocol without any owner account; that result is reported apart from a run against the owner's deployment.

### 8.19 Required integrations: mail, calendars, models and calls (owner decision of 2026-11-22; MAIL-01, MAIL-02, CAL-01, CAL-02, EMAIL-01, CALL-01, MODEL-01–04, R2-INT-01–05, R2-NOT-06)

**Required (owner decision):** working production integrations, with comprehensive case-level tests, for both mail and calendar on personal Outlook.com, personal Gmail with Google Calendar, iCloud Mail and iCloud Calendar, and Proton Mail and Proton Calendar — Microsoft 365 tenants, Google Workspace and Ollama Cloud are outside the required matrix; OpenAI, Anthropic Claude and a self-hosted Ollama on the owner's machine or private network, both for Core inference and for the authorized Apple fallback; and R1's Twilio Programmable Voice. Every calendar is read through **authenticated, private, read-only** access: a link that anyone holding it can open is unacceptable, even for a synthetic test calendar, and a file import, a manual export, a simulated provider or an anonymously readable feed can't certify a live calendar integration. Required integrations stay in the release gate even when they are disabled by default or no credential is configured; at runtime each is enabled only by the owner's explicit opt-in. Controlled servers and a local account-free Ollama serve development; owner-live checks against the actual deployments remain required (§10.9).

**What each provider needs from the owner, recorded as prerequisites to verify.** The owner has made no commitment to obtain any of them; each is checked in the owner harness's preflight (§10.8), and a missing one leaves its rows `NOT_RUN_CREDENTIALS` (owner; prerequisite not available):

| Provider | Second identity | Paid tier | Registration |
|---|---|---|---|
| Outlook.com | A dedicated personal Microsoft account | None known for mail and calendar | An app registration, which must live in an Entra directory (an Azure account, whose sign-up may ask for payment details, or a developer-program tenant) |
| Gmail and Google Calendar | A dedicated Google account | None | A Google Cloud project and Desktop OAuth client created **by the dedicated account itself**, with user type Personal |
| iCloud | A dedicated Apple Account with two-factor authentication (a trusted phone number or device) and an app-specific password | None expected for Mail and Calendar (to verify) | None |
| Proton | A dedicated Proton account | "Proton Mail Bridge is available only with a paid plan that includes Proton Mail" | P-09a, decided (§8.19.5); an amd64 host for the sidecar |
| OpenAI, Anthropic | — | Billing on the owner's API accounts | API keys |
| Ollama | — | — | The owner's own installation |
| Twilio | — | The owner's R1 account and number | — |

**For the owner harness,** Microsoft needs a verification app registration separate from production. Google needs an owner-confirmed verification-only Cloud project, a Desktop OAuth client registered in that project, and the confirmed dedicated Lisa account for a live grant-revocation case. Google revocation affects the user's grants for every client registered under that project. A second client in a production project is insufficient. The preflight and local-only cleanup are in §10.8. [Google OAuth token revocation](https://developers.google.com/identity/protocols/oauth2/native-app#tokenrevoke)

#### 8.19.1 Accounts, authorization and credential custody

- **Dedicated Lisa accounts (Mechanism).** Each provider is reached through an account that exists only for Lisa. Its mailbox is Lisa's dedicated mailbox (MAIL-01), its address is the identity Lisa sends from (EMAIL-01), and the owner shares the calendars Lisa should read with it, **read-only**, from the owner's own account. No connector signs in to the owner's personal account. This keeps R2-SCOPE-02 ("Lisa does not obtain access to the owner's personal mailbox"; calendar subscriptions read-only) and `CONSTRICTION.md`'s rule against silently turning subscribed sources into account access. It also confines credentials that providers can't narrow — an iCloud app-specific password reaches all of an account's mail, contacts and calendars, and every IMAP scope is read-write — to Lisa's own account, while the share enforces read-only on the owner's calendars at the provider.
- **OAuth 2.0 for Microsoft and Google** (`LisaProviderAuth`):
  - one public client per provider, registered by the owner. Microsoft: an app registration in an Entra directory with "Personal Microsoft accounts only" and authority `consumers`. Google: a Desktop client in a Google Cloud project that **the dedicated Google account itself creates with user type Personal**: "Projects configured with a user type of Personal limit authorization requests exclusively to the single Google Account that created the application", and such apps "do not require OAuth app verification, are not subject to the OAuth user cap or 7-day test user token expiration". Another Google account is refused by Google with `client_user_mismatch`. If Personal mode isn't available to that account, External with publishing status "In production" (unverified, a one-time warning screen) is the documented fallback; "Testing" is never used, because its tokens expire after 7 days. **The two user types treat a wrong account differently,** and each is covered apart (`OV-MAIL-GMAIL-03`): with Personal, Google refuses any other account (`client_user_mismatch`) before a token exists; with External, Google issues tokens to any account that consents past the warning, and only Lisa's identity binding refuses it;
  - authorization code with PKCE and a loopback redirect. `lisa core connector authorize <provider> <account>` prints the consent URL with `login_hint` set to the dedicated address and `prompt=select_account`, so a browser already signed in to another account shows the account chooser instead of consenting silently. The owner completes consent in any browser and either pastes the final redirected address back into the CLI, or lets it arrive through an SSH forward of the loopback port. The device-code flow isn't used: Google doesn't allow Gmail or Calendar scopes in it, and Microsoft's own documentation disagrees about personal accounts.
- **Identity binding, before any mailbox read, retained grant or send.** The configured account is an address the owner typed; the authenticated identity is whatever account completed consent. Core binds them before it keeps anything:
  - **Google:** the scopes include `openid email`. Core verifies the ID token (issuer, audience, expiry and Google's signature) and reads `sub`, `email` and `email_verified`, and may read the OpenID Connect userinfo endpoint. `users.getProfile` reads the mailbox (its message counts and `historyId`), so it comes only after the binding, as the first call of the initial sync, which stops if its `emailAddress` differs from the bound address. **Microsoft:** the scopes include `openid profile email User.Read`. Core verifies the ID token, requires `tid` = `9188040d-6c67-4c5b-b112-36a304b66dad` (personal accounts), reads `oid`, then reads `GET /me` for `mail` and `userPrincipalName` (with `User.Read`, the profile, not the mailbox).
  - **What may be called before the binding:** identity-only calls — the token exchange, the provider's signing keys, the ID token, the OpenID userinfo endpoint and Graph `/me`. Every mailbox read (Gmail `users.getProfile`, messages and history; Graph mail folders and messages), every calendar read (the Calendar API, Graph calendars, CalDAV) and every send waits for the binding. The controlled servers classify each request this way, and the wrong-account cases require none of the last three kinds (S-21, §10.9).
  - **The first binding** requires: a verified address equal (ignoring case) to the configured dedicated address; that address not among the owner's own addresses in configuration (approved recipients and the owner's listed identities); and the owner's confirmation at the administrative terminal ("Authorized as <address>, account ID …7c1f. Is this Lisa's dedicated account? Type the address to confirm."). Core then stores the **stable identifier** — Google's `sub`, which Google says to use "as the unique-identifier key for the user", or Microsoft's `oid` with `tid` — because email and `preferred_username` "can change" and must not be used for authorization.
  - **Every later authorization** (reauthorization, a new grant after revocation) must return the same stable identifier.
  - **Identity mismatch.** Discard all provisional tokens locally and keep the established binding and credential intact. Show which account was used and how to restart consent. The rejection issues zero mailbox reads, calendar reads, sends or remote grant changes, including automatic Google revocation. Tell the owner that the provider may retain consent and that removing it in the provider UI can affect other clients of the same Google project. An explicitly selected live verification revocation is a separate operation subject to §10.8.
  - **Wrong-account consent in an existing browser session** is tested with the controlled OAuth servers (S-21): a browser profile signed in to account X while the configured account is Y; the hint and account chooser shown; consent completed as X; binding refused, provisional tokens discarded locally with zero revocations; the request log shows identity-only calls and no mailbox read, calendar read or send. Google's two user types run apart: Personal (Google refuses X with `client_user_mismatch`, so no token exists) and External (Google issues X tokens; Lisa refuses them). The owner-live variant (LS-1) uses only an account other than the owner's personal one: for Google, any other account, against the user type the deployment uses — refused by Google before any token with Personal, by Lisa's binding with External; for Microsoft, a second test account if the owner has one, otherwise `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (owner; no second test account). It never uses the owner's personal account.
- **The provider-token store.** Refresh tokens rotate (Microsoft: "Refresh tokens replace themselves with a fresh token upon every use", and "doesn't revoke old refresh tokens when used"), so they can't live in read-only secret files. Core keeps them in `<data>/provider-tokens/`, one file per account, encrypted with AES-256-GCM under a key from the secret file `provider-token-key` (§8.11.2), with the account's stable identifier and a version counter in the authenticated data.
  - **Atomic rotation:** a refresh result is written to a new file, `fsync`ed, renamed over the old one, and the directory `fsync`ed, all before the new access token is used. A crash leaves the old file or the new one, never a mixture.
  - **Concurrent refresh:** only Core refreshes (the CLI asks Core's listener). One serialized refresher per account deduplicates concurrent callers, which wait for its result. A refresh whose answer is lost is retried with the stored token: Google's doesn't change, and Microsoft's previous token isn't revoked by use, so a retry never falsely marks a grant revoked.
  - **Backups:** the token files are in every backup as ciphertext, listed in the manifest by account, stable-identifier digest, version and file digest; `provider-token-key` is a secret, referenced by name and never backed up (R2-CORE-06). A restore with the key restored from the owner's secret store keeps the grants; without it, every account is `needs_authorization`. Restored tokens are used only from recovery stage 3b for reading and stage 4 for sending (§8.5.4), and the writer fences, not the tokens, decide who may write (§8.5.1).
  - Tokens are Core-only secrets: never replicated, exported, logged or displayed.
- **Failure, revocation and reauthorization.** `invalid_grant`, or a 401 after a refresh — revoked consent, a password change on an account with Gmail scopes, six months unused, Google's limit of 100 tokens per client and account, Microsoft's 90-day lifetime — puts the source in `needs_authorization`: its data is marked stale, the planner treats its calendars as unverified, nothing is deleted, sending through it waits, and the owner sees "Authorize again: lisa core connector authorize …". Reauthorization repeats the identity binding against the stored identifier. `lisa core connector revoke <account>` deletes the local tokens and revokes at Google, and names the provider page where the owner removes the grant (Microsoft account › Privacy › Apps and services; Google Account › Third-party connections). On a verification Core, the listener requires the same §10.8 project and subject checks for every provider-side grant mutation, including a direct administrative command. A local-only deletion has no provider side effect.
- **Passwords for iCloud and Proton:** an iCloud app-specific password (two-factor authentication required) and the Proton Mail Bridge password are ordinary Core secret files, as R1's IMAP and SMTP passwords are (§1.6).
- **What a credential change proves after a restore:** nothing that ends a previous writer's submissions. §8.10.6's table records, per sender, what an old writer may still hold and why no revocation is accepted as a cutoff; only the writer fence ends them (§8.5.1).

#### 8.19.2 Mail and calendar, per provider

| Provider and service | Production route | Authentication and refresh | Supported scope | Owner prerequisites | Milestone | Differences from R1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Outlook.com mail | Microsoft Graph v1.0: `/me/mailFolders/{folder}/messages/delta` for new mail in the configured folder (default Inbox), `/messages/{id}/$value` for raw MIME, with immutable IDs; `/me/sendMail` (saving to Sent Items) for briefs and reminders | OAuth code with PKCE, `consumers`; identity bound by `oid`; the rotating refresh token in the token store | `Mail.Read` (read-only at the provider), `Mail.Send`, `User.Read`, `openid`, `email`, `profile`, `offline_access` | A dedicated Outlook.com account; an app registration in an Entra directory; consent once | M7 | R1's IMAP password login no longer works for Outlook.com; delta tokens and immutable IDs replace UIDs. IMAP with XOAUTH2 (`IMAP.AccessAsUser.All` is read-write; IMAP is off by default) isn't used |
| Outlook.com calendar | Graph `GET /me/calendars` with the dedicated account: each calendar the owner shares appears there with `isSharedWithMe: true`, `owner.address` equal to the owner's configured address, `canEdit: false` and `canShare: false`, under an ID local to the dedicated mailbox (Microsoft: the IDs "correspond to the local copy … in Adele's mailbox"; the owner's IDs fail there). **One owner can share several calendars, whose names needn't differ:** `lisa core connector calendars <account>` lists every shared calendar (name, owner, color, `canEdit`, local ID), the owner selects each one Lisa reads, and Lisa stores the local IDs; one with `canEdit: true` is refused, because the share must enforce read-only, and a selected calendar that disappears goes `needs_authorization` alone. Events by `/me/calendars/{id}/calendarView` over the window, paged by `@odata.nextLink`, times in UTC; changes found by comparing event IDs and `changeKey`, because v1.0 event delta is documented only for the primary calendar and shared folders support no change notifications | As above | `Calendars.Read`, which Microsoft lists for reading the sharee's local copy; `Calendars.Read.Shared` isn't requested, because Lisa never reads the owner's mailbox | The owner shares a calendar with "Can view all details" (role `read`); the dedicated account accepts the invitation in an Outlook client | M7 | Replaces the secret ICS link. **Unconfirmed for personal accounts:** Microsoft's sharing pages don't mention them (E-13) |
| Gmail | Gmail API, **incoming label** configured (default `INBOX`): initial and incremental sync below; `users.messages.send` for briefs and reminders | OAuth code with PKCE (Desktop client, Personal user type); identity bound by `sub`; the refresh token in the token store | `gmail.readonly` (read-only at the provider), `gmail.send`, `openid`, `email` | A dedicated Gmail account that creates its own Cloud project and OAuth client | M7 | Gmail refuses R1's password login; IMAP with OAuth would need the full-access `https://mail.google.com/` scope, so the API is used; Gmail message IDs and history IDs replace UIDs |
| Google Calendar | Calendar API v3 `events.list` per shared calendar ID, with the sync strategy below | As above | `calendar.readonly`, which covers calendars shared with the account (`calendar.events.owned.readonly` doesn't) | The owner shares the calendar ("See all event details") with the dedicated account, which accepts it | M7 | Replaces the "secret address in iCal format" |
| iCloud Mail | IMAP `imap.mail.me.com:993` (TLS) through R1's `LisaIMAP` (`EXAMINE`, `UID SEARCH`, `BODY.PEEK[]` only), with the identity rules below; SMTP `smtp.mail.me.com:587` (STARTTLS) through `LisaSMTP` | An app-specific password, a Core secret; no refresh; revoked by any change of the account password | The password reaches the whole account, so read-only rests on Lisa's closed IMAP command set | A dedicated Apple Account with two-factor authentication and an app-specific password | M7 | None in protocol; configuration and limits documented |
| iCloud Calendar | CalDAV (RFC 4791) through `LisaCalDAV`: `PROPFIND` of `https://caldav.icloud.com/` for `current-user-principal` (RFC 5397), then `calendar-home-set`, then a depth-1 listing of the home, where the shared calendar is expected; its `current-user-privilege-set` (RFC 3744) must show no write privilege; `sync-collection` (RFC 6578) increments with an ETag fallback, `calendar-query` for the window. Only PROPFIND, REPORT and GET are ever sent; ICS is parsed by `LisaICalendar` | The same app-specific password | Read-only on the owner's calendar through a private, read-only share; Lisa sends no write method | The owner shares the calendar privately and read-only with the dedicated Apple Account, which accepts on iCloud.com | M7 | Replaces the public or secret `webcal` link. **Unconfirmed:** Apple documents app-specific passwords for third-party calendar apps but not the CalDAV endpoint, nor whether shared calendars appear to such apps (E-14) |
| Proton Mail | Proton Mail Bridge in a sidecar that shares Core's network namespace (§8.19.5); R1's `LisaIMAP` and `LisaSMTP` to `127.0.0.1:1143` and `127.0.0.1:1025`, with Bridge's certificate pinned; the IMAP identity rules below | The Bridge password, a Core secret; Bridge keeps the Proton session in its own keyring | Lisa's closed IMAP command set; Bridge itself has full access to the account | A dedicated Proton account on a paid plan; the owner's one-time interactive Bridge login; **P-09a**, decided (§8.19.5, §15) | M7 | No protocol change; a new runtime component, amd64 only |
| Proton Calendar | **No compliant route** (§8.19.6) | — | — | — | — | The required row stays open under the owner's settled policy |

**Gmail, against the configured incoming label** (`users.history.list`, `users.messages.list`, sync guide):
- **Initial sync,** after the binding: read `users.getProfile` (its `emailAddress` must equal the bound address; its `historyId` is h0); list `users.messages.list` with `labelIds=[label]` and `includeSpamTrash=false`, paging to the end; fetch each unseen ID with `messages.get` (`format=raw`) and ingest it only if its current labels include the configured label and not `DRAFT`; then catch up the history from h0, so a change made during the listing isn't missed.
- **Increments:** `history.list` from the stored cursor with `historyTypes` `messageAdded`, `labelAdded`, `labelRemoved` and `messageDeleted`, **without** the `labelId` filter, whose effect on label removals Google doesn't document. For each touched message: a `messageAdded` or a `labelAdded` of the configured label → read its current labels (`format=minimal`) and ingest it under the rule above, once; a `labelRemoved` of the label → recorded as "left the incoming label", nothing deleted; `messageDeleted` → recorded, nothing deleted (as R1 keeps ingested evidence).
- **Sent-only mail** (the dedicated account's own sends, `SENT` without the label) and **drafts** (`DRAFT`, even if the label is also present) are never ingested; a message **moved into** the label later is ingested then; one moved out and back is already ingested.
- **The cursor** is the highest `historyId` processed, committed with what it ingested; a `history.list` 404 ("typically valid for at least a week, but in some rare circumstances may be valid for only a few hours") starts a **rescan**: initial sync again, with identity by the Gmail message ID within the bound account, so nothing is ingested twice.

**IMAP (iCloud, Proton), when message identity is uncertain:**
- The cursor is (mailbox, `UIDVALIDITY`, highest ingested UID). If the server advertises `OBJECTID` (RFC 8474), its `EMAILID` is the identity and survives a `UIDVALIDITY` change.
- **On a `UIDVALIDITY` change without `EMAILID`,** the mailbox is rescanned and each message is matched by its RFC 5322 `Message-ID` and a digest of the full message:
  - a `Message-ID` that matches no ingested message → new, ingested once; a later rescan finds it as known;
  - a `Message-ID` that matches exactly one ingested message, with the same digest → the same message, not ingested again;
  - a `Message-ID` that matches with another digest, or several ingested messages → **identity unproven**;
  - no `Message-ID` (or a duplicated one): a digest that matches no ingested message → new, ingested; a digest that matches ingested messages → **identity unproven**, whatever the counts. Two legitimate messages with identical content can't be told apart from one message seen twice, and a deletion followed by an identical new arrival leaves the counts unchanged.
- **Identity unproven** messages are not ingested automatically. The owner sees "2 messages identical to ones received before the mailbox was reset; Lisa can't tell whether any is new", with "Ingest as new" per message.

**Google Calendar, a valid incremental sync over a rolling window** (`events.list`, sync guide):
- **The query.** `syncToken` "cannot be specified together with" `timeMin`, `timeMax`, `updatedMin`, `q`, `orderBy`, `iCalUID` or the extended-property filters (a 400 otherwise), and every other parameter must equal the initial sync's. Google doesn't document what increments cover after a `timeMin`-bounded full sync. So the full sync is **unbounded**: `singleEvents=false` (masters, exceptions and cancellations, not expanded instances), `maxResults=2500`, no time bounds, paged with `pageToken` until the last page, which alone carries `nextSyncToken`. Increments repeat exactly that query with `syncToken`, and include deletions by rule ("it is not allowed to set `showDeleted` to False").
- **The rolling window is local.** Core keeps the complete set of masters, exceptions and cancellations for each shared calendar, expands them with R1's `LisaICalendar` over the coverage window, and moves the window without any new query: the stored set is complete as of the last token, so a day entering the window is covered.
- **Cancellations.** A cancelled exception carries only `id`, `recurringEventId` and `originalStartTime`, and Google says to store such events "for the lifetime of the parent recurring event"; Lisa keeps them as exclusions of that instance. A cancelled event carries only its `id` and removes that event.
- **Expiry (410).** "The client should clear its storage and perform a full synchronization." Lisa performs the full sync into a **staged generation** while the published one stays visible as "Resyncing — not yet verified", and the planner treats that calendar's days as unverified — never as free — until the staged set is complete (last page reached, token stored) and replaces the published one in one transaction (the partition-repair rules of §8.4.3 at Core). An interrupted resync restarts the staged sync; it never publishes a partial set.

**Incoming mail, on every provider:**
- only the dedicated mailbox is ever authenticated, and only after identity binding (§8.19.1);
- **read-only:** Graph `Mail.Read` and `gmail.readonly` can't change flags; IMAP keeps R1's closed command set (`EXAMINE`, `UID SEARCH`, `BODY.PEEK[]`), so `\Seen` is never set (MAIL-01);
- **cursors and pages:** Graph delta links and `@odata.nextLink`, Gmail history IDs and page tokens, IMAP UIDs with `UIDVALIDITY` (MAIL-02). A cursor commits in the connector's transaction together with what it ingested (§8.3.5), so a restart repeats nothing;
- **changed identities:** a changed `UIDVALIDITY`, an expired Graph delta token or a Gmail history 404 starts a rescan under the rules above; ambiguity is preserved, never resolved by guessing;
- **MIME and attachments:** raw MIME through R1's `LisaMIME`, with R1's size limits and unsupported types shown as unsupported;
- **revocation:** `needs_authorization`; data kept; the owner told.
- **Outgoing mail** (R1's brief and reminder email, EMAIL-01): sent only from the dedicated account's own address and only to R1's approved addresses, with other recipients refused before any provider call; through the submission journal and its holds, with the slot's deterministic `Message-ID` (§8.10.6). Provider limits are documented in each setup guide: Outlook.com's free-account limits are unpublished and lower than a subscriber's 5,000 recipients a day; Gmail allows 500 messages a day; iCloud 1,000 messages and 1,000 recipients a day; Proton's limit depends on the account's reputation, with at most 100 recipients a message.
- **Calendars, on every provider:**
  - private, authenticated access, refused after revocation: `needs_authorization`, the days shown "Using saved calendar information", never as free;
  - an initial sync, then incremental syncs;
  - zones and DST from each provider's zone data and R1's embedded zones; floating and all-day events follow the zone rules of §8.4.2;
  - recurring series with moved and cancelled instances; all-day and multi-day events by R1's overlap rule (§1.6); cancelled events removed;
  - **no write path at all:** no write method is implemented, and no write scope is requested;
  - provider changes picked up at the next poll; a stale or partial read never replaces a window (coverage per source, §8.4.2);
  - retries with backoff that honor `Retry-After`; conservative planning while a source is unverified (CAL-01's conservative behavior).

  Each provider's propagation delay is documented in its setup guide, and owner-live cases wait within a stated bound (15 minutes by default) before recording a failure with the cause "not propagated within the bound".
- **Optional public calendars:** R1's `LisaCalendarFeeds` stays for genuinely public calendars (holidays, for example). A secret or shared link on one of the four providers' hosts is refused at configuration, with a pointer to the authenticated connector.

**The two calendar experiments (owner-run at LS-1; §10.5):**
- **E-13, a calendar shared between two personal Microsoft accounts.** Owner side: in Outlook.com, the owner shares a synthetic test calendar with the dedicated address as "Can view all details" (role `read`). Microsoft supports listing `calendarPermission` "on behalf of only the calendar owner", so Lisa never reads it — that would need the owner's own token; the owner records the role shown in Outlook's sharing dialog as a manual observation. Recipient side: the dedicated account accepts in an Outlook client ("The invitation and adding a shared … calendar occur in an Outlook client"). The owner shares two synthetic calendars read-only — one named like a calendar of the dedicated account — and a third with edit rights. Lisa then lists `/me/calendars` and must find each shared calendar with `isSharedWithMe: true` and the owner's address as `owner.address`, the two read-only ones with `canEdit: false` and `canShare: false`; the owner selects those two, and the editable one is refused; Lisa reads each one's `calendarView` and must see the owner's seeded events, an edit and a cancellation within the propagation bound, each in its own calendar. **If the lookup fails,** the harness walks the documented causes — the invitation not accepted, propagation still within the bound, the role chosen — and repeats. A failure after those is a failed E-13 with the Outlook calendar row open; it doesn't justify broader access. Authorizing Lisa against the owner's own account would change the dedicated-account design and R2-SCOPE-02's boundary, which only the owner can decide (§22).
- **E-14, an iCloud calendar shared privately.** With the dedicated Apple Account's app-specific password, the harness runs the discovery above and must find the shared calendar in the dedicated account's calendar home with no write privilege, read the owner's seeded events, and see an edit within the bound. It records every property Apple returns for the shared calendar, because Apple documents none of them. A shared calendar that doesn't appear is a failed E-14, with the iCloud calendar row open.

#### 8.19.3 Model providers, on Core and as the Apple fallback

| Provider | Route | Structured output | Usage for budgets | Errors and uncertainty | Configuration example (placeholders) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| OpenAI | Chat Completions through `LisaOpenAICompatible`, extended: `response_format` with `json_schema` and `strict: true`; `max_completion_tokens`; sampling parameters only where the model's profile allows them; `X-Client-Request-Id` set to Lisa's request ID; `x-request-id` recorded | Strict schema; `message.refusal` and `finish_reason: "length"` count as invalid results | `prompt_tokens`, `completion_tokens` | 401 and 403 authorization; 404 unknown model; 429 rate limit (honoring `Retry-After`) or `insufficient_quota`; 5xx; a timeout after sending is `uncertain` and never resent, because no idempotency key exists | `provider: "openai"`, `endpoint: "https://api.openai.com/v1"`, `model: "<model ID>"`, `credential: "keychain:<item>"` on a device or `"file:<path>"` on Core |
| Anthropic | The Messages API through `LisaAnthropic`: `anthropic-version` pinned in configuration (`2026-10-21` when read), `system` at the top level, `max_tokens` sized to include thinking, no sampling parameters; content read by block type | `output_config.format` with `json_schema` (no recursive schemas, numeric or length constraints; Lisa's own validator still enforces every rule) | `input_tokens` plus both cache fields; `output_tokens`, thinking included | `stop_reason: "refusal"` (HTTP 200) and `"max_tokens"` count as invalid results; 401 and 403; 402 billing; 404 model; 413; 429 with `retry-after`, or the spend-limit 429 without it (failed, not retried); 500; 504 `timeout_error`; 529 `overloaded_error`; `request-id` recorded; uncertainty as above | `provider: "anthropic"`, `endpoint: "https://api.anthropic.com"`, `apiVersion: "2026-10-21"`, `model: "<model ID>"`, `credential: …` |
| Ollama (self-hosted) | Native `POST /api/chat` through `LisaOllama`, with `stream: false`, `options.num_ctx` set from Lisa's bounded context (the 4,096-token default would cut prompts silently), and `keep_alive` | `format` with the JSON schema, validated by Lisa | `prompt_eval_count`, `eval_count` | 404 model missing; 503 queue full (refused before processing); Ollama has no authentication of its own, so on a private network the owner puts it behind a TLS reverse proxy, which may require a bearer token that Lisa sends | `provider: "ollama"`, `endpoint: "http://127.0.0.1:11434"` (loopback) or `"https://10.0.0.5"` with a pinned SPKI (a private literal), `model: "<model name>"` |

- **Owner-run Ollama setup** (`Harness/docs/SETUP-OLLAMA.md`): install Ollama on the owner's machine or a private-network host and pull the chosen model; for the Mac on the same machine, keep it on `127.0.0.1:11434`; for Core and the iPhone and iPad, put a TLS reverse proxy with a bearer token in front of it on a private address, and pin the proxy's SPKI in each configuration. Lisa never exposes Ollama beyond what the owner configures.
- **Endpoint policy:** R1's model-endpoint rules stay (§1.6): endpoints named by DNS are public only (A records only on Apple, §8.9.3); `localhost`, loopback or private IP literals may use local networks, **for the configured model route only** — its exact endpoint, the authenticated `POST`, no redirects — never for retrieval or a redirect target (§8.9.3); plain `http` only on loopback. Ollama's `/v1/chat/completions` endpoint also works through `LisaOpenAICompatible` as an alternative configuration, but isn't the default: it can't set the context size per request and has no strict mode.
- **Unsupported optional features are marked:** none of Lisa's workflows uses tools, streaming or images, so they are off for every provider; Anthropic's OpenAI compatibility layer isn't used, because Anthropic doesn't consider it production-ready.
- **Shared rules:** R2-INT-01–05 and §8.9.2 apply to every provider: device-local credentials and consent, one request in flight, ten a day, one repair, no resend after a possibly charged timeout, restarts without duplicate spending, withdrawal of consent, and the R2-INT-02 workflows with Core unavailable. Core inference uses the same adapters with Core's credentials (R2-TRUST-04).

#### 8.19.4 Calls (Twilio Programmable Voice)

- **Kept from R1 (CALL-01):** the Calls resource with TwiML `<Say>`; the approved number, urgency, acknowledgement, quiet-hour and rate controls (§1.6); and the submission journal and holds (§8.10.6).
- **Lifecycle evidence:** Core is reachable only over the VPN, so it can't receive Twilio's status callbacks. After an accepted create, it reads the call resource (`GET …/Calls/{Sid}.json`, read-only, with bounded backoff for up to 30 minutes) and records queued, ringing, in-progress, completed, busy, failed, no-answer or canceled. Twilio's acceptance, the call's lifecycle, its receipt by the phone and the spoken message are recorded as four separate facts.
- **No automatic second call:** Twilio documents no idempotency key for creating calls, so a timeout after submission, a restart or a restore leaves the call `outcomeUnknown` and held (§8.10.6); only the owner's release places another.
- **Account-free tests** run against a controlled Twilio-shaped server; Twilio's test credentials (which never place a real call) and the live call to the owner's designated test number are owner-run cases (§10.8).


#### 8.19.5 Proton Mail: the Bridge deployment (P-09a, decided by the owner on 2026-11-23)

**Decision.** Revision 7 proposed P-09a, and the owner accepted it on 2026-11-23 (§15, §22). Revision 8 corrects four details inside the accepted component — the writable cache directory that holds Bridge's process lock, mutual exclusion between login and service, the Compose service reference, and the source of the verification policy — and the pin procedure, and keeps account-free startup checks apart from the owner-only E-15.

Proton's own facts (proton.me support pages and the ProtonMail/proton-bridge repository at `v3.27.2`, read 2026-11-23, §1.5): Bridge serves IMAP on 1143 and SMTP on 1025 on `127.0.0.1`, "accessible only to applications running on the same device"; it needs a Secret Service keychain or `pass` on Linux, and its README says "only gnome-keyring is usable without major problems"; it keeps its TLS certificate and key in its encrypted vault, generated when the vault is created (`internal/vault/certs.go`), and its CLI can export them (`cert export`); it starts its IMAP and SMTP servers when it starts, with no account check (`internal/services/imapsmtpserver/service.go`); it allows one instance per user: the lock file is `<cache>/protonmail/bridge-v3/<config name>.lock` under the user cache directory (`internal/locations/locations.go`, `GetLockFile`), and a second process that finds it held "raises" the running instance and exits (`internal/app/app.go`); automatic updates are on by default; "Bridge supports only 64-bit systems. It doesn't support ARM devices other than those running on Apple silicon"; it is officially supported "for the latest LTS version of Ubuntu and the latest version of Fedora Workstation"; and it "is available only with a paid plan that includes Proton Mail". Proton documents no headless command reference beyond `--cli` and `--noninteractive`; E-15 records what the CLI actually offers with an account.

**The accepted option — P-09a: the official Bridge 3.27.2 as a sidecar on an amd64 Core host.**
- **Exact release.** Tag `v3.27.2` at commit `3e9f85aa3c676a573b64b1d90829970ded26dbfa` (GitHub release published 2026-11-06; Proton's update feed lists 3.27.2 with revision `3e9f85aa3c` and release date 2026-11-19). Package `protonmail-bridge_3.27.2-1_amd64.deb`, SHA-256 `cf08332dcc4a332c3592476855e2007e6daadbcdac11fab6af83470a3255a8dc` as GitHub reports it, with its detached signature `protonmail-bridge_3.27.2-1_amd64.deb.sig`, from the GitHub release or `https://proton.me/download/bridge/`.
- **Verification, with each input from its documented source.** A separate, recorded fetch step downloads:
  - the package and its signature, from both GitHub and proton.me, which must be identical;
  - Proton's key `bridge_pubkey.gpg` from `https://proton.me/download/bridge/bridge_pubkey.gpg`, the location Proton's verification page gives, and the GitHub copy; both must be identical and have the pinned SHA-256 `d5279ed2dc52ffad8875569df0bcca45bd7448bf737fe79722762337e204c221`;
  - Proton's `debsig` policy `bridge.pol` from `https://proton.me/download/bridge/bridge.pol`, **its only documented source** — the verification page tells the reader to fetch it from there, gives no inline copy, and whether GitHub attaches it to the release couldn't be determined. Its SHA-256 is recorded at the first fetch (M7), like the base image digest, and compared at every rebuild; a changed policy fails the build and comes back to the owner. Its content is checked to name origin `E2C75D68E6234B07` and keyring file `debsig.gpg`.

  The image build then fails unless: the package's SHA-256 equals the pinned value; the key's digest equals the pinned value; and `debsig-verify`, with the key dearmored to `/usr/share/debsig/keyrings/E2C75D68E6234B07/debsig.gpg` (the file name the policy requires) and the policy in `/etc/debsig/policies/E2C75D68E6234B07/`, as Proton's verification page prescribes, prints "Verified package from 'Proton Technologies AG (ProtonMail Bridge developers) …'". Proton's page prints no key fingerprint, and still names 3.8.2 as the current version, so the full key fingerprint is recorded at the first fetch and compared at every rebuild.
- **The image** (`deploy/proton-bridge/Dockerfile`, reproducible): base `ubuntu:26.04`, the latest Ubuntu LTS, pinned by digest at the M7 build; packages from a pinned `snapshot.ubuntu.com` timestamp — the `.deb`'s dependencies plus `gnome-keyring`, `dbus` and `ca-certificates` (which brings `openssl`); a fixed non-root user; a read-only root filesystem; `tmpfs` for `/tmp` and the D-Bus runtime directory; no published ports.
- **Writable directories, and why each exists.** Bridge's base directories come from the XDG variables, set explicitly in the image: `XDG_CONFIG_HOME=/bridge/config`, `XDG_DATA_HOME=/bridge/data`, `XDG_CACHE_HOME=/bridge/cache`. Each is a named volume, writable by the sidecar's user: `bridge-config` (the vault, `vault.enc`, encrypted under a key held in the keyring), `bridge-data` (Bridge's mail cache and its logs), **`bridge-cache` (Bridge's process lock, `protonmail/bridge-v3/<config name>.lock`; the account-free checks record the actual file name)**, and `bridge-keyring` (the gnome-keyring files). The cache directory is a named volume rather than a `tmpfs` so that the service and the one-off login container see the same lock file.
- **Namespace and listener boundary.** The Compose service `proton-bridge` uses `network_mode: "service:lisa"` — `lisa` is Core's service name in `deploy/compose.yaml`, the one `lisa-deploy` and the deploy check already use (§8.11.3) — with `depends_on: [lisa]`. Bridge runs in Core's network namespace, so its loopback listeners are reachable by Core at `127.0.0.1` and by nothing outside that namespace — the "same device" boundary Proton states, with no forwarding. Bridge's outbound connections to Proton use Core's egress.
- **Password store.** The entrypoint starts a private D-Bus session and `gnome-keyring-daemon` with the Secret Service component, unlocking the login keyring with a password read from the Docker secret `bridge-keyring-password`, which the owner creates on the host and which never enters the image or Git.
- **Login and service exclude each other.** One Bridge instance per vault is the rule; two processes on one vault are never run:
  1. `deploy/lisa-deploy proton-bridge login` first stops the service (`docker compose stop proton-bridge`) and waits until it has exited;
  2. it runs `protonmail-bridge --cli` in a one-off container with the same namespace, volumes and secret. The owner types `login` (Proton username, password, two-factor code, mailbox password), `info` to read Bridge's IMAP and SMTP username and password — which the owner writes into the Core secret file `proton-bridge-password` — `updates autoupdates disable`, and `cert export` into `/run/bridge-export`, a `tmpfs` of that one-off container, then exits;
  3. after the CLI exits, the helper prints the SHA-256 of the exported certificate's public key (`openssl x509 -pubkey` over `cert.pem`, DER-encoded) and removes `cert.pem` and `key.pem`; the `tmpfs` disappears with the container, so Bridge's private key never leaves it;
  4. it starts the service again, which runs `protonmail-bridge --noninteractive` ("no way to add or remove client, get bridge password").

  If the service is somehow still running, the shared lock in `bridge-cache` makes the second process raise the first and exit instead of opening the vault; the helper reports that and stops. Claude never sees the Proton credentials, the Bridge password or the exported key.
- **Certificate trust on IMAP and SMTP, from a fingerprint obtained independently of the listeners.** Core trusts Bridge's self-signed certificate by an **SPKI pin**, in whichever mode Bridge's settings use (STARTTLS or implicit TLS; R1's `TransportSecurity` supports both, `Sources/LisaConfig/TransportSecurity.swift`):
  - **initial enrollment:** `lisa core connector proton pin --expect <SHA-256>` takes the fingerprint the login helper printed from Bridge's own `cert export`. Core then connects to both listeners inside the namespace and computes each presented leaf's SPKI SHA-256. It stores the pin as `current` only if **both** equal the expected value; two listeners that agree with each other but not with the expected value are refused, as is either one alone;
  - **rotation:** after Bridge is set up again (a new vault means a new certificate), the owner exports the new certificate in a login session and runs `pin --expect <new SHA-256> --as-next`, which stages `next` only after both listeners present it; a certificate matching neither `current` nor `next` is refused on each protocol, the connector shows "Bridge's certificate changed: confirm it with lisa core connector proton pin", and promoting `next` retires `current`;
  - **a wrong certificate is refused on each protocol separately,** including when both listeners present the same wrong certificate: against fixture listeners (S-21), and in the owner harness against harness TLS listeners started inside the same namespace on spare ports, to which the disposable verification Core's connector is pointed for that case (§10.9).
- **Restart.** `restart: unless-stopped`. At start the entrypoint unlocks the keyring and starts Bridge; Core's connector waits for the IMAP greeting with the pinned certificate and shows the source as stale meanwhile; email sending through Proton waits and retries as a transient failure.
- **Backup and restore — a P-09a design choice, not O-8.** Bridge's four volumes are outside Lisa Core's backup sets: they hold Proton session material, Bridge's own mail cache and its lock, not Lisa's data, and Core's backups carry only Lisa's database and recovery files (§8.4.4). O-8, which excludes replicas, sidecars and blobs from device backups, concerns the Apple apps' local stores and isn't the basis for this. After a restore or a host move, the owner repeats the login, writes the new Bridge password and pins the new certificate again.
- **Updates.** Automatic updates are switched off with the CLI's `updates autoupdates disable` in the login session, and the entrypoint compares Bridge's reported version with the pinned one, refusing to serve on a mismatch. A new Bridge version is a new image with a new exact pin, which needs the owner's approval like any dependency change.
- **Host, CPU and tier.** amd64 only; a paid Proton plan that includes Mail; the Ubuntu LTS userland in the container.
- **Two kinds of evidence, kept apart:**
  - **account-free startup checks** (Claude, rootless engine, M7, once implementation is approved; controlled real-protocol class, §10.1): the verified build; the version check; the keyring unlocked from a test secret; Bridge starting with no account on fresh volumes; the lock file created in `bridge-cache`; a second process on the same volumes raising the first and exiting without opening the vault; `cert export` and the fingerprint helper on a test vault; whether both listeners accept a TLS handshake with no account, and if so, the pin procedure and both wrong-certificate refusals against them — if Bridge doesn't serve without an account, that is recorded and those two checks move to E-15; restart behavior; the update setting persisting;
  - **E-15 and live mail** (owner only, LS-1 and LS-3): login with the dedicated paid account, the keyring unlocked after a restart with a live session, the listeners and the pin with the real vault, sending and receiving through the dedicated account. A headless container isn't a configuration Proton documents, so these are verified by the owner.
- **arm64.** On an arm64 Core host the sidecar can't run. Proton's GPL source has a `make build-nogui` target, but documents no arm64 build, and a self-built binary isn't the official package, so it isn't proposed. On an arm64 host Proton Mail stays `NOT_RUN_ENVIRONMENT` (owner; no amd64 host for Bridge) unless the host is amd64 or the requirement changes.
- **What the decision covers, exactly:** (1) the Bridge sidecar as a new runtime component — `deploy/proton-bridge/`, its Compose service and the `lisa-deploy proton-bridge` commands; (2) the exact package version, SHA-256 and signing-key digest above, the Ubuntu base image digest recorded at the M7 build, and, as part of the same verification step, the policy file's digest recorded at that fetch; (3) building that image and running it in the rootless engine for development and tests, with no account, once implementation is approved. **Not covered, keeping their separate scopes:** any use of the owner's accounts (only the owner logs in), production deployment on the owner's host, host administration, image publication, and any later Bridge version.

**P-09b (not chosen).** The owner-managed alternative — Proton's package installed directly on an amd64 Core host, reached through the rootless engine's host-loopback access — is host administration and wasn't selected.

**Not offered:** running Bridge on the owner's Mac with the Mac app as the Proton connector would respect Bridge's boundary, but it moves a private source connector and email delivery out of Core, which R2-ARCH-02 assigns to Core. Only a change of that requirement could allow it.

#### 8.19.6 Proton Calendar: a provider capability blocker
